MedCEA Platform Manual

How to get access to, use and run the MedCEA platform for regulated controlled-environment cultivation: the web apps, the edge devices, the access controls and the records they keep.

Platform overview

MedCEA is an open, self-hosted platform for growing medicinal mushrooms and plants in controlled chambers so that the same recipe gives the same result, and so that every step can be shown to an inspector afterwards. This chapter explains what the platform is for, the parts it is built from, how a sensor reading reaches a dashboard and how a recipe ends up switching a relay, the web addresses you use, and the words the rest of this manual relies on.

What MedCEA is

MedCEA stands for Medicinal Controlled Environment Agriculture. It is the prototype built in a Design Science Research PhD project at ISCTE-IUL and Instituto de Telecomunicações, with ESACB (2025 to 2028). The main organism is Hericium erinaceus (lion's mane). The goal of the production is not the largest harvest. It is a reproducible amount of the target bioactive compounds, made in a way a regulator can accept. All custom code is released under the MIT licence.

The project sets the platform four goals, called pillars:

PillarWhat the platform must do
I. Compound-centred controlRun a recipe as a programme of steps, each with its own target ranges for temperature, humidity, CO2 and light, and keep the chamber inside those ranges with a control algorithm, which can be a simple on/off rule or an AI method.
II. Reproducible outcomesFollow the recipe and know the genetic origin of each lot, so that differences between batches can be traced to the environment rather than to chance.
III. Compliance by designBuild the rules of GACP, EU GMP Annex 11, the Novel Food regulation and THMPD into the data model, the access control and the event records, rather than produce them afterwards as reports.
IV. Traceability and auditRecord every event in a lot's life in an audit trail that can be added to but not edited, with access split by role (operator, quality, administrator, auditor).

This is a prototype. Recipe execution, control algorithms, sign-in with eleven roles, changes stamped with the person who made them, approvals recorded in the database and a hash-chained history of access policy changes are built. Batch and lot records, laboratory results, the Electronic Batch Record, GS1 EPCIS event export and a regulator interface are not built yet. Where a later chapter describes a duty that has no screen yet, it says so.

To learn the main tasks by doing them on the platform, and to check after a change that they still work, follow the exercises in Training.

Building blocks

Everything runs on one Ubuntu computer (the host) with MicroK8s, a small Kubernetes. Two scripts install it: ./deploy-infra.sh for the infrastructure and ./deploy-apps.sh for the applications. The administrator runs them on the host; everyone else works in a web browser. See Administration.

Infrastructure services

ServiceWhat it does
KeycloakThe one sign-in page for every web application (realm medcea). It holds the accounts and the role of each person.
PostgreSQLThe shared database medcea_db: units, devices, recipes, runs, measurements, approvals and the audit records. It is reachable only inside the cluster.
MosquittoThe MQTT message broker. Boards and services exchange readings and commands through it. Every client signs in with its own credential; there is no anonymous access. Boards on the local network connect on port 31883.
Node-REDSubscribes to every MQTT message under medcea/ and writes it into the database as a measurement. Engineers also use its editor to build flows.
GrafanaDashboards that read the measurements from PostgreSQL.

Web applications

The Portal is the start page. It shows three cards: Grafana, JupyterHub (AI Workbench) and Administration. The Administration card opens the Platform Management page, which holds the cards for Node-RED, Database Management, MQTT Explorer, System Configuration, Recipe Schedule Manager, Run Monitor, User Manager and pgAdmin. The device onboarding wizard has no Portal card; the table says where to find it.

ApplicationWhat you do there
System Configuration (Config UI)Manage locations, production units, zones, devices, recipes and their steps, control models, and which recipes each unit may run. Its tabs are Locations, Units, Devices, Control Models and Recipes. The Onboard Device button on the Devices tab opens the device onboarding wizard.
Recipe Schedule Manager (Scheduler)Start a run now (Run Now), schedule runs (once, hourly, daily, weekly, monthly), stop or retry runs, read run logs.
Run MonitorLive view of active runs, cycle progress, and sensor readings against the step's limits.
Database ManagementExport the definition and data of the database objects in the groups your role may export; every export is logged.
pgAdminA PostgreSQL web console, for the admin role only. It shows the schema and the configuration, not production data. Reached from the Platform Management page.
User ManagerTabs Requests, Users, Decisions, E-mail settings, Templates, Access review and Change log. Each person sees only the tabs their role may use.
MQTT ExplorerBrowse, publish and subscribe to MQTT topics in the browser.
Node-RED editorBuild and change data flows.
AI WorkbenchJupyterHub: notebooks and VS Code with a personal workspace, for research on the platform's data.
Device onboarding wizardRuns on the host, because it flashes boards over USB. It registers a board in a slot and writes its configuration. Open it with the Onboard Device button in the Config UI.

Every card is shown to everyone, but which applications a person can open depends on their role. See Access and roles and Using the applications.

Edge boards

The chambers are equipped with ESP32 boards running MicroPython. Each board is onboarded with one of two roles and runs the application for that role:

  • SENSE reads the sensors fitted to it and publishes the readings. The supported sensors are a Sensirion SCD40/SCD41 or SCD30 (CO2, air temperature and air humidity), a DS18B20 substrate temperature probe and an analogue water level probe.
  • CONTROL drives a relay board: grow lights (red, blue, UV), air heater pad, air cooler, humidifier, air flow fan, CO2 fan, CO2 flow steriliser, substrate heater. Which relay drives which equipment is set in the board's channel map during onboarding. It reports back what it actually switched.

Onboarding, replacing and calibrating boards is covered in Devices.

Units, zones and slots

The physical layout is stored as three levels:

  1. A location is a site or room.
  2. A production unit is a chamber in a location, identified by a unit code such as UNIT01.
  3. A zone is a controlled area inside a unit, identified by a zone code such as ZONE01.

Each zone has exactly two slots: one for a SENSE board and one for a CONTROL board. A board's name comes from its slot: the unit code, s or c, and the zone code, in lower case, for example unit01-s-zone01. That name is its network hostname, its MQTT user name and the hostname shown in the Config UI. A board that is not registered yet is called cea-un- followed by the last six characters of its MAC address.

All MQTT traffic for a zone sits under one topic base, medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>:

TopicWho publishesWho listensContent
medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>/sense/<tag>SENSE boardNode-RED, the run in progressA reading, for example tag co2, air_temperature or air_humidity
medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>/control/<tag>The run in progress (Scheduler); an engineer during commissioningCONTROL boardA command for the equipment that affects that reading, or light for the grow lights
medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>/control_setpoint/<tag>CONTROL boardNode-REDWhat the board actually switched

The broker enforces this: a SENSE board may only publish to its own sense topics, and a CONTROL board may only read its own control topics and publish to its own control_setpoint topics. Setting a device's Status to Inactive in the Config UI locks it out of the broker within about 15 seconds.

How data flows

From a sensor to a dashboard

  1. The SENSE board in a zone reads its sensors and publishes each value on medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>/sense/<tag>, with a timestamp and the board's name.
  2. Mosquitto checks the board's credential and topic, then passes the message on.
  3. Node-RED receives every message under medcea/ and stores it in the database table of measurements.
  4. Grafana dashboards and the Run Monitor read the measurements from the database and show them.

From a recipe to a relay

  1. An engineer writes a recipe in the Config UI: a sequence of steps, each with a number of cycles, light ON and light OFF minutes, a light type (UV, BLUE, RED or DARK), and lower and upper limits for air temperature, humidity, CO2 and (optionally) substrate temperature.
  2. The recipe is allowed on a unit in the Recipe ↔ Unit Permissions panel of the Config UI's Units tab, with a control algorithm and its parameters.
  3. In the Scheduler someone starts a run (Run Now) or creates a schedule that starts it later. The run is recorded with the person who started it, or, for a schedule, the person who created the schedule.
  4. The run goes through the steps in order and repeats each step for its number of cycles. During a cycle it listens to the sense topics of the unit. At present a run follows one zone per unit.
  5. For each reading the control algorithm compares the value with the step's limits and decides UP, DOWN or OFF, and the run publishes that on the matching control topic. Once per pass it also publishes ON or OFF for the grow lights on control/light: ON during the light ON minutes of the cycle, OFF after.
  6. The CONTROL board looks the command up in its channel map and switches the relay of the right equipment, for example the heater for UP on air temperature.
  7. The board publishes what it did on control_setpoint. Node-RED stores that too, so the record shows not only the command but that it reached the hardware.
  8. When the last cycle ends, the run is marked Completed. A stopped or crashed run is marked Failed.
StageSensor pathControl path
ChamberSensor on a SENSE boardRelay on a CONTROL board
TransportMQTT sense topic via MosquittoMQTT control topic via Mosquitto, answered on control_setpoint
PlatformNode-RED stores the measurement in PostgreSQLThe Scheduler's run applies the recipe step and the control algorithm
PeopleGrafana, Run MonitorConfig UI (recipe), Scheduler (run), Run Monitor (progress)

Changes to locations, units, zones, devices, recipes and their steps, recipe-unit permissions, algorithms, parameter sets, schedules, scheduler settings, runs and cycles are stamped with who made them (the signed-in person; system:scheduler acting for the person behind a schedule; or db: followed by the database login for a change made directly in the database, such as by a deploy script) and copied to an append-only change log. Every placement or move of a board in a slot is kept in a slot history. See Compliance and records.

Web addresses

Every application has its own host name under one base domain, and every one is served over HTTPS with a certificate signed by the platform's own certificate authority. Each computer or phone that opens the platform must trust that authority once, or the browser shows "Not secure".

The administrator chooses the base domain at install time:

  • Name-based (medcea): addresses such as https://portal.medcea. The host answers name lookups for the medcea names, and Tailscale split DNS sends every device on the tailnet to it. This needs one setting in the Tailscale admin console, once per tailnet.
  • IP-based (the default): addresses such as https://portal.100.94.119.12.nip.io, which need no DNS setup.
  • A domain in your own DNS.

When the platform moves from the IP-based to the name-based domain, the old addresses (the alias) stay as permanent redirects, so bookmarks keep working. The first time you open a name-based address, type the https://: browsers treat a one-word ending as a search until they have visited it.

Address (name-based domain)Application
portal.medceaPortal: start here
access.medceaUser Manager; access.medcea/register opens the Request access tab of the sign-in page
config.medceaSystem Configuration
scheduler.medceaRecipe Schedule Manager
monitor.medceaRun Monitor
dbmgmt.medceaDatabase Management
pgadmin.medceapgAdmin
grafana.medceaGrafana
node-red.medceaNode-RED editor
mqtt-explorer.medceaMQTT Explorer
ai-workbench.medceaAI Workbench (JupyterHub)
onboard-device.medceaDevice onboarding wizard
keycloak.medceaKeycloak (sign-in page; its admin console is for recovery only)

With the IP-based domain, replace medcea with the IP-based domain, for example grafana.100.94.119.12.nip.io. Boards do not use these names: they reach the broker on the host's local network address, port 31883.

Key terms

TermMeaning
Unit (production unit)One cultivation chamber, identified by its unit code (for example UNIT01). Operators are assigned to units and see only the runs, run logs and telemetry of the units assigned to them.
Commissioning (test mode)A flag on a unit. While it is on, an engineer may send commands to the unit's control topics from the MQTT Explorer, and no run can start on the unit. An engineer proposes it with a reason and a different qa person approves it; ending it applies at once and qa reviews it afterwards.
ZoneA controlled area inside a unit, identified by its zone code. Each zone has one SENSE and one CONTROL slot and its own MQTT topic base.
SlotThe place for one board in a zone: unit, zone and role (SENSE or CONTROL). A board is registered into a slot; replacing a board means marking the old one inactive and onboarding the new one into the same slot.
RecipeA cultivation programme: an ordered list of steps, each with its cycle count, light type, light ON and OFF minutes, and lower and upper limits per environmental parameter. A recipe is a draft until a qa person who did not write it approves it. An approved recipe cannot be edited; a change starts a new draft. The qa person approves it, or returns it with a reason, in the Configuration UI's Approvals tab; the approval is recorded in the database. A recipe is retired, never deleted.
RunOne execution of a recipe on one unit, with its status (Queued, Running, Completed, Failed), its log and the person it is attributed to.
CycleOne repetition of a recipe step. It lasts the step's light ON plus light OFF minutes; a step runs as many cycles as its cycle count says.
Algorithm (control model)The rule that turns a reading and the step's limits into UP, DOWN or OFF. Built in, as named on the Control Models tab: If-Then-Else (a simple on/off rule, and the default), PID Controller, Fuzzy Logic Control, Model Predictive Control and Q-Learning.
Parameter setA named set of settings for one algorithm (for example PID gains), for one unit or for any unit. It starts as a draft and is used once a qa person approves it. Schedules, recipe-unit permissions and runs refer to a parameter set, so a run uses settings that were reviewed.
Learned State (Q-table)What a learning algorithm has saved for a unit, kept between cycles and runs so it does not start from zero; for Q-Learning this is its Q-table. The Config UI shows it in the Learned State column. It is never deleted; only a qa person can retire it, and the next run then starts from zero.
ReleaseThe record that a qp person who neither created, changed nor executed the run (any attempt) released the output of a completed run. It is done in the Configuration UI's Approvals tab, with an optional note, and shown as a Released badge in the Scheduler. A release is final; a run nobody releases stays unreleased, and so does a run from before attribution, whose creator and executor are not recorded. Once a run is released, it can no longer be reset for a retry.
Decision (account decision)A recorded change to an account made by an administrator: a grant, a role change, a unit assignment, a deactivation or activation. A decision that widens access waits until a qa person who is neither its maker nor the person it concerns confirms it in the User Manager's Decisions tab. Deactivation and narrowing take effect at once.
Access reviewThe quarterly review of who holds which role and unit. A qa person creates it in the User Manager's Access review tab and signs it off; the sign-off cannot be undone. Auditors can read it.

Getting access and roles

Every person signs in to MedCEA with one personal account, and every account holds exactly one platform role. You ask for an account on the sign-in page. An administrator reviews the request, and a different person holding qa confirms it before the account can be used. Your role decides which web apps you can open and what you may do in them. This chapter covers how to request access, what each of the eleven roles is for, how to sign in and out, how to manage your profile and password, what happens when your role changes or your account is deactivated, and what to do when a page says you have no access.

Requesting access

The platform has one sign-in page, served by Keycloak in the MedCEA look. It has two tabs: Sign in and Request access. Below the sign-in form there is also a link, "No account yet? Request access". Opening https://access.<domain>/register brings you to the same tab.

What the form asks

FieldRule
First name, Last nameRequired, up to 100 characters each.
E-mailRequired, a valid address. The decision is sent here.
OrganisationOptional, up to 200 characters.
Username3 to 32 characters: lowercase letters, digits, dot, dash or underscore, starting with a letter or digit.
Password, Repeat passwordAt least 10 characters; both must match. This is the password you will sign in with.
Role you needChoose one role from the list. Each role shows its label and a short description. Roles not yet available carry the tag "not yet in use".
Business justificationWhat you will use the platform for, 20 to 2000 characters.
  1. Open the sign-in page and choose the Request access tab.
  2. Fill in every field and choose the role you need.
  3. Choose Submit request. The page answers "Request received. You will get an e-mail once an administrator has reviewed it."
  4. Wait for the e-mail. Until then, signing in shows "This account is not active".

The form refuses a request when another request for the same e-mail address is already waiting, or when the username or e-mail address already has an account. One network address can send at most 10 valid requests per hour.

What happens to your request

Submitting creates your account at once, but disabled. It stays disabled until two different people have acted on it:

  1. An administrator opens the request in the User Manager under Requests, and either approves it with a role (the one you asked for or a different one) or rejects it with a note explaining why. The administrator also writes the e-mail you will receive, starting from a prepared text, or unticks Send e-mail to (followed by your address) so that none is sent.
  2. An approval does not enable the account. It becomes an account decision that waits under Decisions until a person holding qa confirms or refuses it. That person must not be the administrator who approved it, and must not be you.
  3. When qa confirms, the account is enabled with the granted role and the e-mail the administrator wrote is sent. If the granted role differs from the one you asked for, the prepared text the administrator starts from says the role was reassigned.

A rejection, or a qa refusal of the approval, deletes the disabled account. You may apply again.

E-mails depend on the platform's mail account, which an administrator sets up in the User Manager under E-mail settings. Without it, the decision is still recorded but no e-mail goes out, so ask an administrator directly.

Your request is stored with every field except the password, which only Keycloak keeps. Each step (submitted, approved, reassigned, rejected) is added to the request's history, with who decided and when. The approval and the qa confirmation are kept as one account decision that names the maker, the checker, both times and any notes, and the e-mail text the administrator wrote is stored with the request.

The eleven roles

The roles come from the access policy (apps/iam/policy/defaults.yaml, section roles). The role key is the name the platform uses in the User Manager and in records. Two roles include another: a production_manager may do everything an operator may do, in every unit, and qa may do everything an auditor may do.

RoleLabelWhat the person doesAvailability
operatorCultivation OperatorRuns the cultivation day to day in the units assigned to them. Executes and stops runs a production manager prepared for their unit; watches telemetry, runs and run logs of their own units in the Monitor; reads recipes and schedules.In use
production_managerProduction Manager (Process Owner)Owns production planning. Creates runs and schedules, starts and stops runs, and assigns approved recipes and approved parameter sets to units without editing them. Includes everything an operator may do, in every unit.In use
maintenanceMaintenance and CalibrationReplaces and calibrates boards and sensors in slots that already exist (never creates a new slot). Reads telemetry, runs and device history to diagnose faults.In use
qcQuality Control AnalystTests product and records lab results. Reads runs and telemetry of the batches under test. Lab results are not yet part of the platform.Not yet in use
qaQuality AssuranceIndependent approver. Approves recipes, controllers, settings, master data and device changes made by someone else; confirms every account grant, role widening and unit assignment an administrator makes; approves every change to the access policy; signs off the quarterly access review; reviews audit trails and export evidence. Includes everything an auditor may do.In use
qpQualified / Responsible PersonReleases the output of a completed run they neither created, changed nor executed (any attempt); reads and exports the run record and its histories. Creates and changes nothing.Not yet in use
engineerAutomation EngineerBuilds and maintains the automation: drafts recipes, controllers, parameter sets and master data for qa to approve; onboards devices; maintains Node-RED flows and Grafana dashboards; commissions units over MQTT while a unit is flagged for commissioning.In use
bio_scientistBio-process ScientistStudies the biology. Reads recipes, controllers and telemetry and analyses data in the AI Workbench.In use
control_scientistControl and AI ScientistDesigns control algorithms. Reads controller definitions, parameter sets and telemetry and develops and evaluates algorithms in the AI Workbench.In use
adminSystem AdministratorKeeps the platform running. Administers accounts and unit assignments (every grant confirmed by qa), drafts policy and settings changes for qa to approve, administers the workbench hub, and views the schema and configuration in pgAdmin. Has no access to regulated or history content. In the prototype the same person operates the host, which counts as break-glass access.In use
auditorAuditor / InspectorReads and exports records, configuration and audit trails. Changes nothing.In use

"Not yet in use" means the role exists and can be requested, but the work it is meant for is not yet in the platform. The request form shows these roles with the tag "not yet in use".

One platform role per account

An account holds exactly one platform role. To do work that needs another role, ask an administrator to change your role; you do not get a second one. When a role changes, the new role is added before the old one is removed, so a failure in between leaves the account with two roles for a moment, never with none.

Two rules protect the separation between administration and approval:

  • No person may hold an admin account and a qa account at the same time. The User Manager checks this against all of a person's accounts, enabled or not, when a role is changed and again when the change is confirmed.
  • The User Manager refuses to take away the last enabled admin or the last enabled qa.

Operators and their units

An operator works only in the units assigned to them: they see the telemetry, runs and run logs of those units and start or stop runs only there. With no unit assigned they see none of this. The administrator assigns units on the platform host with apps/iam/units.sh, not in the User Manager. Each assignment waits until a qa person other than the administrator confirms it in the User Manager under Decisions; a removal takes effect at once. A production_manager is not limited to assigned units. The Administration chapter describes how assignments are made.

Which web apps each role can open

Each web app sits behind a sign-in gate that admits only the roles the access policy names for it. The administrator can print the current list from the policy with:

python3 apps/iam/compiler/cli.py hosts

The table below is that list for the default policy. Hostnames are shown with the default prefixes from config.env.example; your installation's domain follows the dot.

Web appHostRoles admittedGate status
Service portalportal.All eleven rolesChecked
Configuration UIconfig.All eleven rolesChecked
Schedulerscheduler.All eleven rolesChecked
Monitormonitor.auditor, bio_scientist, control_scientist, engineer, maintenance, operator, production_manager, qa, qc, qpChecked; the two scientist roles read telemetry but not runs, so the run list stays empty for them
Database Managementdbmgmt.auditor, qa, qpChecked
User Manageraccess.admin, auditor, qa, qpChecked
MQTT Explorermqtt-explorer.engineerChecked
Node-REDnode-red.engineerChecked; the rights inside the editor also come from the policy (Node-RED's own sign-in)
pgAdminpgadmin.adminFrom the policy, checked by pgAdmin's own sign-in
Grafanagrafana.auditor, engineer, maintenance, production_manager, qa, qc, qpChecked by Grafana's own sign-in setting: engineer is Editor, the others Viewer; any other role cannot sign in
AI Workbenchai-workbench.admin, bio_scientist, control_scientist, engineer, qaChecked by JupyterHub's own sign-in setting; admin is hub administrator, but cannot open anyone's workspace
Device onboardingonboard-device.engineer, maintenanceFrom the policy, checked by the wizard at sign-in and at every renewal of the sign-in during a run
Access Policynot deployedPolicy: admin, auditor, qa, qpNot deployed yet (IAM Phase 4)

Opening a web app is only the first gate. What you may do inside it (create a recipe, start a run, approve a change, export data) is decided again for each action by the service and by the database, from the same policy. For example, every role can open the Configuration UI, but only some roles can save a draft there and only qa can approve one.

The service portal shows every card to everyone. A card for an app your role cannot open leads to the "No access" page described in Why can't I open an app.

Signing in and out

  1. Open any platform address, for example the service portal. If you are not signed in, you are sent to the sign-in page.
  2. On the Sign in tab, enter your Username or e-mail and your Password. Show displays the password as you type it.
  3. Tick Remember me if you want Keycloak to keep your sign-in on this browser after you close it.
  4. Choose Sign in. You return to the page you asked for.

One sign-in covers every web app on the platform: once signed in, opening another app does not ask for your password again. pgAdmin, too, goes straight to the platform sign-in; it shows its MedCEA sign-in button only after you used pgAdmin's own Logout, and choosing it uses the same sign-in.

Messages you may see on the sign-in page:

  • "Invalid username or password." Check both and try again.
  • "This account is not active." Your request has not been confirmed yet, or your account was deactivated. Contact an administrator.

To sign out, open the user menu and choose Sign out. This ends your platform sign-in session, not only the page you are on:

  1. The app you are in signs you out of itself at once.
  2. The sign-in page asks "Do you want to log out?". Confirm it. This ends your platform sign-in.
  3. When the AI Workbench is installed, you then pass through the workbench, which ends its own session. This happens even if you never opened the workbench.
  4. You land on the service portal's sign-in page.

Without the AI Workbench, Sign out works as before. After the "Do you want to log out?" page it returns you to the app you signed out from, which asks you to sign in again.

File → Log Out in the AI Workbench also ends your platform sign-in in the same way, not only the workbench.

The other apps keep a short session of their own. When it ends, each app checks with the platform sign-in again, without asking for your password while that sign-in lasts, and follows whoever is signed in now:

AppIts own session lastsWhat you notice when it ends
Apps behind the platform's sign-in gate (portal, Configuration UI, Scheduler, Monitor, Database Management, MQTT Explorer, User Manager, Node-RED)checked every 4 minutes (the app you choose Sign out in ends at once)Nothing, or the sign-in page if you are signed out.
AI Workbench5 minutesThe next workbench page signs you in again without a prompt. A JupyterLab tab that stays open stops saving and opening files about 5 to 10 minutes after it was loaded ("Forbidden"). Reload the tab, or open the workbench in another tab, and it works again. Unsaved changes stay in the tab, and your kernels keep running.
Grafana5 minutes without use, and at most 1 hour from when you signed in to itThe page reloads once and comes back to the same dashboard. Dashboard changes you have not saved are lost at that reload, so save as you go. A tab you keep using (a dashboard that refreshes itself counts) is not interrupted before the hour, so you do not lose a panel you are building every 5 minutes.
Node-RED editor1 hour from when you opened itThe editor signs you in again. If you have changes you have not deployed, the browser first asks whether to leave the page: choose to stay, export your flows (menu → Export), then reload and import them, or deploy before the hour is up. Five minutes would interrupt editing too often, so the editor keeps one hour.
pgAdmin60 minutes without use, and at most one daypgAdmin signs you in again without a prompt, as whoever is signed in to the platform then. pgAdmin offers no shorter session: its start-up accepts only whole days.

Switching accounts in one browser

After Sign out, or when someone else signs in on the same browser, every app follows the new person within the times in the table above. Until then, an app you had open may still show the previous person. These are known gaps:

  • The AI Workbench may show the previous person for up to 5 minutes (an open JupyterLab tab for up to 10 minutes). The new person is never shown the previous person's workbench server: the workbench refuses it.
  • Grafana may show the previous person for up to 5 minutes after its tab was last used, and for up to an hour after they opened it while a tab keeps it in use (for example a dashboard that refreshes itself). Close Grafana's tabs before you hand over the computer.
  • The Node-RED editor may show the previous person for up to an hour after they opened it, and acts as that person, when the new person may also open Node-RED. Editor sessions started before 1 November 2026 were set to last a week; the deploy of that day ended them.
  • pgAdmin may show the previous person until it has gone 60 minutes without use, and for at most a day. Use pgAdmin's own Logout before you hand over the computer.
  • The app you chose Sign out in is signed out at once. Another app you had open in another tab can keep working in that tab for up to 4 minutes.
  • Open live connections stay connected until you close their tab. These are JupyterLab kernels, MQTT Explorer's broker connection and Grafana's live updates.

On a shared computer, do not tick Remember me. Sign out, then close the browser, or use a private window for each person.

The user menu and your profile

Signed-in pages (the service portal, the Configuration UI, the Scheduler, the Monitor, Database Management, MQTT Explorer and the User Manager) show your name with your initials at the top right. Choosing it opens the user menu:

  • Profile opens your profile page.
  • Sign out signs you out.

If the profile service cannot be reached, the menu shows "Account" instead of your name and still offers Sign out. In that case Sign out ends your sign-in for that app only, so close the browser if you need to be sure you are signed out everywhere.

If Sign out stops on an error page of the AI Workbench after you confirmed "Do you want to log out?", your platform sign-in has already ended; only the workbench did not answer. Its own session ends by itself within 5 minutes, and the other apps follow within the times in the table above. Close the browser if you need to be sure, and tell an administrator that the workbench did not answer.

Profile

The profile page, "Your MedCEA account", has two parts:

  • Personal details: you can change your first name, last name and organisation and choose Save changes. Your username and e-mail are shown but cannot be changed here; ask an administrator.
  • Password: Change password takes you to the platform's password page. You may be asked to sign in again first. When the change is done you return to your profile, which says "Your password was changed." The password goes only to Keycloak, never through the platform's own services.

The Back to link at the top left (for example "Back to portal") returns you to the page you opened the menu from, or to that app's home page.

Forgotten password

The sign-in page shows a Forgot password? link only when the platform's mail account is configured and confirmed. Then:

  1. Choose Forgot password? on the sign-in page.
  2. Enter your username or e-mail address on the "Reset your password" page.
  3. Follow the link in the e-mail you receive and set a new password.

When the link is missing, mail is not configured: ask an administrator for help.

An administrator sets the mail account in the User Manager under E-mail settings. The change takes effect, and the Forgot password? link appears, only after a qa person confirms it under Decisions. This stops an administrator from sending password-reset mail through a mailbox they read.

When your role changes or your account is deactivated

Only an administrator changes a role, activates or deactivates an account, in the User Manager under Users. A role change and a deactivation need a reason; for an activation the reason is optional. An administrator cannot change their own account.

ChangeWhen it takes effect
Role change that gives more accessWaits under Decisions until a qa person confirms it. When it is confirmed, any MQTT Explorer credentials you hold are revoked; the Explorer then asks for a new one with your new role.
Role change that only removes accessApplied at once. Any MQTT Explorer credentials you hold are revoked: the broker refuses them within about 15 seconds.
Activation of a disabled accountAlways waits for a qa confirmation.
DeactivationApplied at once. Every platform sign-in session is ended (Grafana, pgAdmin and AI Workbench sessions as described below the table), MQTT Explorer credentials are revoked (the broker refuses them within about 15 seconds, and no new one is issued on the old sign-in), and any decision still waiting about the account is withdrawn, except unit assignments.

You do not need to sign out for a role change to reach you. The sign-in gate in front of each app checks your session with Keycloak every 4 minutes, and the proof of sign-in passed to the services is valid for 5 minutes. So, for the apps behind such a gate (all except Grafana, the AI Workbench and pgAdmin, which keep their own sessions; the AI Workbench is described below the list):

  • after a role change, the apps you can open and the actions you may take follow the new role within about 4 minutes;
  • after a deactivation, your sessions end at once, and a page you still have open stops working within about 4 minutes, at the next check.

The AI Workbench checks your role each time you sign in to it, and a workbench session lasts 5 minutes before the sign-in runs again (without a password while your platform session is open). So a role that no longer includes the workbench, or a deactivation, reaches it within 5 minutes; a notebook tab left open stops saving within about 10 minutes. Your files are kept, and come back if access is restored. A notebook that reads platform data with your own sign-in (Reading platform data from a notebook) follows the platform's 5-minute rule instead: after a role change its next requests run with the new role, and after a deactivation they are refused within five minutes.

The device onboarding wizard checks your role when you sign in and again each time it renews your sign-in during a run (at least every five minutes). If you lose engineer or maintenance during a run, it stops before the board is erased. pgAdmin checks your role only when you sign in: a pgAdmin session you already have open stays open after a role change until it ends, an hour after pgAdmin last saved it.

Grafana also checks your role only when you sign in. A Grafana tab you already have open keeps the Grafana role it was given, and keeps showing every unit's telemetry, after a role change or a deactivation, until that Grafana session ends (5 minutes after the tab was last used, at the latest one hour after you signed in to Grafana) or Grafana restarts (it restarts when the policy changes who gets which Grafana role). Then Grafana sends you through the sign-in again, which checks your role. This is a known gap.

Every role change, activation and deactivation is kept as an account decision, with the reason, who made it, who confirmed it when confirmation is needed, and the times. Each one that takes effect is also added to the account's status history, with the old and new role for a role change. These histories can only be added to, never edited.

Changing the access policy

Who holds which level on what is the access policy. Since slice 4.4 (November 2026) a change to it is made by one person and approved by another, in the Access Policy:

StepWhoWhere
Draft and edit a change of the matrix (who holds which level), submit it, withdraw itadminAccess Policy → Changes and Matrix
Propose a change of the platform skeleton (roles, groups, routes, hosts, network rules) from a git commitThe host operator./apps/iam/apply.sh --propose-skeleton … --from-git <commit> (see apply.sh)
Approve or return a submitted versionA qa person who is none of its authors and whose role it does not widen; for a skeleton version also not the person apply.sh named, with the attestationAccess Policy → Approval
Apply an approved versionThe host operator./apps/iam/apply.sh <version>: slice 4.5, not built yet. Nothing approved changes anyone's access before then.
  • The two switches. Approving and returning go to the database with the qa person's own sign-in token, so they need IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES to be true (see IAM switches); otherwise the page says approving is not possible yet. Drafting does not need them.
  • One version in flight. At most one change or skeleton version is open (draft, submitted or approved) at a time. An open change blocks a skeleton proposal and an open skeleton proposal blocks a new draft, until it is applied, withdrawn or returned.
  • Returning. A qa person returns a version with a note. A returned change becomes a draft again for its authors; a returned skeleton version is withdrawn, and the host operator proposes again.
  • Withdrawing. Any admin withdraws the open version in the Access Policy (Withdraw…), approved ones included. When the page cannot (a rollback, a broken page), the host operator runs ./apps/iam/apply.sh --withdraw-open --by <username> --reason "<why>".
  • Break-glass blocks approvals. While a policy break-glass is open nothing can be approved, until a normal deploy ends it.

Every draft, saved cell (with the checker's verdict), submission, approval, return and withdrawal is an event in the tamper-evident policy history, with who, when and the reason or note. An approval and a return also keep the signed sign-in token as evidence. Nothing is ever deleted: a withdrawn version stays in the record.

Why can't I open an app

When your role does not admit you to an app, its gate shows a page titled No access to followed by the app's name, for example "No access to Node-RED". The page says:

  • that you are signed in, but your role does not include the app;
  • which roles may open it, by label, as listed in Which web apps each role can open;
  • that you should ask an administrator to change your role if you need it for your work.

It offers Back to the portal and Sign out.

Grafana checks your role in its own sign-in, so it shows no such page: you land on Grafana's sign-in page with a message that the login was denied.

The AI Workbench also checks your role in its own sign-in. Its refusal is the workbench's own error page (403) with the text "No access to AI Workbench", the roles that may open it and the address of the portal. Its File → Log Out, like Sign out in the user menu, ends your platform sign-in.

If you are refused right after switching to another account in the same browser, the previous account's sign-in is usually still active, and the app is checking that account's role. Sign out from the user menu (your name at the top right, then Sign out) and sign in again, or use a private window for the second account. The Sign Out buttons on the portal page sign you out of the portal only, not of the platform: the portal then signs you in again as the first account.

The service portal has its own page, No access yet. It means your account has no platform role at all, which usually means your access request has not been confirmed yet.

A sign-in failure also shows an error page, but with its own message (starting "Login Failed") and a Try again button. That is a problem with the sign-in itself, not with your role.

Being able to open an app does not mean every button works for you. If an action inside an app is refused, the service checked your role for that action. Ask an administrator which role the action needs, or see the role descriptions in The eleven roles.

Using the web apps

The platform is a set of web apps, each on its own address under the platform domain, all signed in through the same platform account. This chapter takes them one at a time: what each app is for, which roles can open it, and the main tasks as step lists. It also says what each action records. The access policy decides who may do what. An app that your role may not open answers with an error page that names the roles that may open it. In most apps a button your role may not use stays on the screen; once the policy is enforced, the action is refused with a message that names the reason.

Which roles can open which app

Every app sits behind a sign-in gate that admits only the roles the access policy compiles for that address. The table shows the gates at this release. Inside an app, the policy then limits each action further, as the sections below explain.

AppDefault addressRoles admitted
Portalportal.<domain>All eleven platform roles
Platform manual (this manual)portal.<domain>/manual/All eleven platform roles (the portal's gate)
Configuration UI (System Configuration)config.<domain>All eleven platform roles
Scheduler (Recipe Schedule Manager)scheduler.<domain>All eleven platform roles
Run Monitormonitor.<domain>operator, production_manager, maintenance, qc, qa, qp, engineer, auditor; bio_scientist and control_scientist for telemetry only (no runs)
Database Managementdbmgmt.<domain>qa, qp, auditor
User Manageraccess.<domain>admin, qa, qp, auditor
Access Policy (the Policy UI)policy.<domain>admin, qa, qp, auditor
MQTT Explorermqtt-explorer.<domain>engineer
Node-REDnode-red.<domain>engineer
Grafanagrafana.<domain>engineer (Editor); production_manager, maintenance, qc, qa, qp, auditor (Viewer). Checked by Grafana's own sign-in setting
AI Workbench (JupyterHub)ai-workbench.<domain>admin, bio_scientist, control_scientist, engineer, qa, checked by the workbench's own sign-in
pgAdminpgadmin.<domain>admin only, from the access policy, checked by pgAdmin's own sign-in

The addresses are the defaults from config.env.example. An installation can change them in config.env. How to obtain a role is described in the access chapter.

Report-only and enforcing

The Scheduler, the Run Monitor and the Configuration UI's file libraries check every request against the access policy. The switch IAM_ENFORCE in config.env decides what a failed check does:

  • While it is false (report-only), the request goes through and the service writes a "would refuse", "would limit" or "would withhold" line to its log.
  • While it is true, the request is refused: 401 when the app cannot tell who you are, 403 when your role does not hold the right, and 503 when the service cannot load the policy.

Database Management, the User Manager and the Access Policy always enforce, whatever the switch says. The changes you save in the Configuration UI go straight to the database, which adds its own checks once IAM_DB_ROLES is true. Each app section notes where the two modes differ. The switches themselves are explained in the administration chapter.

The user menu

Every signed-in app except Grafana, the AI Workbench, Node-RED and pgAdmin shows your name at the top right. It opens a menu with two entries:

  • Profile: your personal details and your password.
  • Sign out: ends your session on the platform, not only in that app.

Portal

The portal is the start page. It lists the apps as cards; clicking a card opens that app in a new browser tab. Every platform role can open the portal. An account without a platform role gets the page No access yet, which explains that an administrator still has to approve the access request.

The main page, MedCEA Platform, shows four cards:

  • Grafana: the MedCEA Dashboard.
  • JupyterHub: the AI Workbench.
  • Documentation: the Platform manual, this manual (see below).
  • Administration: opens the Platform Management page.

Platform Management holds the cards for these apps:

  • Node-RED
  • Database Management
  • MQTT Explorer
  • Configuration
  • Scheduling (Recipe Schedule Manager)
  • Operations (Run Monitor)
  • User Manager

The portal shows every card to every role. A card for an app your role may not open still opens it, and the app's gate answers with an error page. When an administrator uninstalls an app, its card stays on the page, greyed out and not clickable, and it comes back when the app is deployed again.

  1. Open https://portal.<domain> and sign in with your platform account.
  2. Click a card. For the apps under Platform Management, click Administration first.
  3. Use Back to return to the main page, or the user menu to sign out.

The platform manual in the portal

Since 3 November 2026 this manual is part of the platform. The Platform manual card on the portal's main page opens it in a new tab at https://portal.<domain>/manual/; /manual without the slash leads there too. It sits behind the portal's own sign-in, so everyone the portal admits can read it (every platform role) and nobody else: signed out, the address leads to the platform sign-in page, and an account without a platform role gets the portal's No access yet page. It is not in the user menu.

The copy you read there is built from the manual's chapters in the repository each time it is published, by every ./deploy-infra.sh and by ./guide.sh → Infrastructure → Publish the manual to the portal (see the manual in the portal). Your browser checks for a newer copy at every load, so a republished manual shows at once on reload. Reading it leaves no event in the platform's records; a read appears only in the access logs of the sign-in proxy and of the portal's web server. The Updated and Code lines at the top say which edition you read.

Configuration UI

The Configuration UI, titled System Configuration, holds the definitions the platform runs on:

  • the physical plant: locations, units, zones and devices
  • the control algorithms, their parameter sets and the learned state of learning controllers
  • the recipes, which recipe may run on which unit, and the steps of each recipe
  • the approval of drafts and proposed changes

Every role can open it. What you may change depends on your role:

AreaWho may change itWho may only read it
Locations, units, zones, componentsengineerEvery other role
Devicesengineer; maintenance only into a slot that already existsoperator, production_manager, qa, admin, auditor
Recipes and stepsengineer, drafts onlyoperator, production_manager, qa, bio_scientist, control_scientist, auditor
Recipe ↔ Unit Permissions (assignments)production_manager, approved recipes onlyThe roles that read recipes
Control algorithm defaultsengineer, drafts onlyproduction_manager, qa, bio_scientist, control_scientist, auditor
Parameter setsengineer, drafts onlyproduction_manager, qa, bio_scientist, control_scientist, auditor
Approvals (drafts and proposed changes)qa approves or returns; the proposer withdraws their own proposalauditor reads the whole queue; qp reads only its commissioning proposals; both read the approval history; every person sees their own proposals and drafts
Unit commissioning flag (test mode)engineer proposes it, with a reason; qa approves it (never the proposer). Ending it: engineer, at onceEvery role sees the flag; who sees names and reasons is described under commissioning a unit
Learned state (Q-tables)qa may retire itproduction_manager, engineer, auditor

The page has six tabs: Locations, Units, Devices, Control Models, Recipes and Approvals. The Approvals tab is shown only while the database enforces roles (IAM_DB_ROLES=true), like Q-table retirement; its count is what you can act on. The Portal link at the top left returns to the portal.

Locations

A location is a site, for example a building. Each one has a name, an address, a contact, coordinates, a description and a status (Active or Inactive).

  1. Open the Locations tab.
  2. Click + New Location, or Edit on an existing row.
  3. Fill in at least Name, then click Save Location.

↑ Import loads locations from a file on your computer or from the Platform Location Library. ↓ Export All and the row's ↓ Export save them to your computer or to the library. Once the services enforce, only an engineer may save to the library.

Units and zones

A unit is one cultivation chamber at a location; a zone is a part of a unit. Telemetry, runs and device names all carry the unit and zone codes.

  1. Open the Units tab and click + New Unit, or Edit on a row.
  2. Enter the Unit Code, choose the Unit Type (Greens, Mushrooms or Other) and the Location. + New beside the location creates one without leaving the form.
  3. Click Save Unit.
  4. To add a zone, open the unit with Edit, click + Add Zone, enter the Zone Code and click Save Zone.

Unit codes and zone codes are unique regardless of case. A duplicate is reported as "already exists". A code becomes part of each board's network name, so choose codes of letters, digits, _ and - only; the devices chapter explains why.

Commissioning a unit (test mode)

A unit flagged for commissioning is in test mode: engineers may send commands to its control topics from the MQTT Explorer, and no run can start on it. The broker applies a change of the flag at its next check, within about 15 seconds. The Commissioning column of the Units tab shows each unit's state: Off or Commissioning, a run that is running or scheduled on it, a proposal waiting for qa, an end waiting for qa's review, and the last change.

To flag a unit (engineer):

  1. Open the Units tab and click Flag for commissioning… in the unit's Commissioning cell.
  2. Type the Reason (required; qa reads it) and click Propose.
  3. The cell reads "Waiting for qa: flag on … (your proposal)". Nothing changes until a qa person other than you approves it.

Withdraw… takes your waiting proposal back (the reason is optional); it never applies. A proposal another engineer made shows without buttons: only its proposer withdraws it, and only qa returns it.

To end commissioning (engineer), click End commissioning…, type the reason and click End commissioning. Ending applies at once: the broker stops engineers' commands within about 15 seconds and runs can start again. It then waits for qa's review.

To approve (qa), click Review… in the unit's cell, or on the row in the Approvals tab: the same review opens, with the proposer, the time and the reason. Type an optional note and click Approve and apply, or type a reason and click Return…. For an end that already applied, the button is Mark reviewed. Review… is off on a proposal you made yourself.

The refusals, as the page words them:

  • "Run #N is running" or "Run #N is scheduled": Flag for commissioning… is off while the unit has a run running or scheduled, and the database refuses the proposal, and the approval, while it has one. The other way round, no run can be scheduled on a flagged unit (a new run, or a failed run set back to scheduled): schedule it once commissioning ends;
  • you proposed this change: another qa person must approve it;
  • "Nothing to change": the unit is already in that state;
  • a proposal for this unit already waits for qa: it is approved, returned or withdrawn first;
  • the proposal was already applied, returned, withdrawn or replaced: the list is refreshed;
  • only an engineer proposes commissioning changes.

Who sees what: the proposer's name and reason are shown to engineer, qa, qp and auditor; the approver's name to qa, qp and auditor; every other role sees the flag and "Waiting for qa". The run that blocks a unit is shown only to roles that read every unit's runs (not to operator). qa, qp and auditor also get History: every proposal for the unit with its proposer, value, reason and outcome (applied, applied at once and reviewed, returned, withdrawn, replaced, waiting).

While IAM_DB_ROLES is false the column only shows the flag, with a note that commissioning changes need the database's enforcement; nobody can change it.

Each proposal is a row in medcea.pending_change with the proposer's account, person, name, role and reason. Each approval, review, return and withdrawal is a row in medcea.approval. The flag change is in the row-change log and in the tamper-evident policy history (commissioning_enabled, commissioning_disabled), with the unit, the person who made it take effect and the reason.

Devices

The Devices tab lists every board with its hostname, type, unit and zone, model, serial number and status. The Online column checks whether the board answers on the network: click the icon in a row to check that board, or the icon in the column header to check all of them. Edit opens a device, and its components can be added with + Add Component.

Boards are normally added with the onboarding wizard, not typed in here. That procedure, and the rule that maintenance may only re-onboard into an existing slot, are in the devices chapter.

Recipes and steps

A recipe is the growth programme for a crop: a sequence of steps, each with a number of cycles, a light type, the minutes the light is on and off, and lower and upper limits for:

  • air temperature
  • CO2
  • humidity
  • substrate temperature
  1. Open the Recipes tab and click + New Recipe. Enter a Name and click Save. A new recipe is a draft.
  2. Click Steps → on the recipe's row.
  3. Click + Add Step. Fill in Step #, Step Name, Cycles, the light settings and the limits, then click Save Step.
  4. Repeat for every step, then click ← Back.

Other actions on a recipe:

  • ⎘ Copy makes a new draft with the same steps. On an approved or retired recipe it reads ⎘ Copy to a new draft: that is how an approved recipe is changed.
  • Retire… (in the recipe's detail) takes a recipe out of use. Nothing is deleted: a retired recipe keeps its steps for the record, cannot be changed, assigned or run again, and cannot be brought back. Show retired lists retired recipes too.
  • Remove step… (in a draft's step) does not delete the step either: it copies the recipe to a new draft without that step and retires the old draft.
  • ↓ Export downloads the recipe as a file.
  • ↑ Import reads a recipe from your computer or from the Platform Recipe Library.

Saving a recipe to the Platform Recipe Library is refused for every role once the services enforce, because the library cannot check the rules that limit who may change a recipe (drafts only for engineers, assignments only for production managers). Downloading a recipe, and the recipes in the database, are not affected.

No role may delete configuration records once the database enforces roles (IAM_DB_ROLES=true), so the Delete buttons of locations, units and devices are refused. To take a unit, location or device out of use, set its Status to Inactive. Recipes, steps and parameter sets have no Delete button any more, in either mode: they are retired.

Assigning recipes to units

The Recipe ↔ Unit Permissions panel on the Units tab is a grid of recipes against units. A tick means the recipe may run on that unit, and a circle means it may not. Under the tick is the algorithm chosen for that pairing. Inactive recipes are faded and inactive units struck through; their cells show a dash and cannot be clicked. This is the production_manager's planning task.

  1. Open the Units tab and scroll to Recipe ↔ Unit Permissions.
  2. Click the circle where the recipe's row meets the unit's column. The cell turns into a tick and Permission Settings opens.
  3. Pick a Default Algorithm (left empty, the system default IF_THEN_ELSE is used), optionally enter Parameter Overrides (JSON), and click Save. To change these later, click the tick.
  4. To withdraw the assignment, click the tick and then Revoke Permission.

Once the database enforces roles, the recipe must be approved before it can be assigned. A run then takes its algorithm and parameters from an approved parameter set: the schedule's, or else the assignment's. The parameter overrides typed here are not used. Parameter sets are created on the Control Models tab; this screen does not yet attach one to an assignment, and the Scheduler lists the sets that exist.

Control Models and learned state

The Control Models tab has three panels.

  • Control Algorithms lists each algorithm with its code, name, description and Default Params (JSON), and its approval status under the code. An engineer may edit the defaults of a draft and click Save. An approved algorithm is frozen: its defaults are read-only and Save is off; a unit is tuned with a parameter set instead. This screen does not create a new algorithm.
  • Parameter Sets lists the parameter sets of each algorithm (filter by algorithm; Show retired adds the retired ones) with the unit they are for (or any unit), their parameters, their approval status and who last edited them. An engineer clicks + New Parameter Set (or Edit on a draft), enters a Name, the Algorithm, the Unit and the Params (JSON object), which start from the algorithm's defaults, and clicks Save draft. A name is unique and at most 120 characters; a copy is named "(copy)", "(copy 2)" and so on. A set whose unit was deleted shows that unit as "#N (deleted unit)" and keeps it when saved, unless you choose another. An approved set opens read-only, with ⎘ Copy to a new draft; Retire… takes a set out of use, without deleting it.
  • Production Units lists, for each unit, how many recipes are allowed on it and the learned state each learning controller keeps for it. Click the record count to see the state per channel and when it was last updated.

Drafts, approval and the author rule

Recipes (with their steps), control algorithms and parameter sets have a status: draft, approved or retired.

  • A new definition starts as a draft.
  • A qa person approves it. The approval is refused when the approver changed the definition since its last approval. It is also refused when any change since then has no recorded person; such a definition has to be re-created before it can be approved.
  • An approved definition is frozen. The Configuration UI offers no edit of an approved or retired definition, in either mode. Through other clients (the API, the Scheduler's algorithm defaults) the database refuses the edit while IAM_DB_ROLES is true; while it is false it allows it but turns the definition back into a draft, with an advisory. Make a copy with ⎘ Copy to a new draft and have the copy approved instead.
  • A parameter set can only be approved once its algorithm is approved.

While the database enforces roles, only approved recipes may be assigned to units, and a run uses only approved algorithms and parameter sets.

What the author sees. The Recipes table has an Approval column (Draft — waits for qa approval, Approved, Retired; its tooltip names who last edited the recipe and when) next to Active. An approved or retired recipe opens with View instead of Edit, under a banner that says it is frozen; its form, + Add Step and the steps' Edit are not offered. Algorithms and parameter sets show the same status. Where your own work stands (waiting, returned with qa's reason, approved) is under My proposals and drafts on the Approvals tab.

Approving drafts and proposed changes

The Waiting for approval panel of the Approvals tab lists the draft recipes, control algorithms and parameter sets, and the proposed changes: log settings proposed in the Scheduler and unit commissioning proposed on the Units tab. Each row says what it is, who changed it since its last approval (or who proposed it, with the reason), when it was last approved and by whom, and how many changes there are. qa approves. auditor reads the whole queue except the completed runs. qp sees the commissioning proposals and the completed runs waiting for release (see releasing a completed run), not the drafts or settings (the queue lists only the groups a role reads, and qp reads master data but not recipes, controllers or scheduler settings), so an empty queue for qp does not mean nothing waits for qa. Neither auditor nor qp approves or returns these. The count on the tab is what you can act on: what you may approve (not the drafts you returned, which wait for their author) and your own returned drafts.

  1. Open the Approvals tab.
  2. Click Review… on a row.
  3. Read What you approve (the recipe with its steps, the steps changed since the last approval highlighted; the parameters; or the proposed value) and Changes since the last approval: each change with its time, the person, role and source, and the fields with their old and new values.
  4. Optionally type a Note. It is recorded with the approval.
  5. Click Approve (Approve and apply for a proposed change, which then takes effect). The message names the approval record.

To send work back instead, type the reason in the same field and click Return…. A returned draft stays an editable draft, is marked returned in the queue until its author changes it, and shows the reason to its author. A returned proposal is closed and never applies; its proposer may propose again. Ending a unit's commissioning applies at once; it is listed for review afterwards, and Mark reviewed records that you checked it.

When you may not approve a row, it says why under the button, and the Approve button is off. The database checks again when you click, and its refusal is shown in plain words:

  • you changed the item (or one of its steps) since its last approval, or you proposed the change: another qa person must approve it;
  • a change was made without a signed-in person: copy the item into a new draft and approve the copy (a proposal without a person is returned);
  • your role cannot approve it;
  • a parameter set's algorithm is not approved yet: approve the algorithm first;
  • it was approved, returned, withdrawn or replaced meanwhile: the list is refreshed;
  • a log setting below the minimum, or commissioning a unit that has a run running or scheduled, cannot be applied.

An approval here is an attributable record, not an electronic signature; signatures in the sense of 21 CFR 11.50, 11.70 and 11.200 arrive with Section 7 of the plan.

Releasing a completed run

A completed run is released by a qp person who neither created it, changed it nor executed it (any attempt of it). The Waiting for approval panel lists, for qp only, every run with status Completed that nobody has released yet, as Completed run rows named "Run #<id> (<recipe> on <unit>)". Only a role that may release runs and reads every unit's runs gets these rows; an operator, who reads only their own units' runs, never does.

  1. Open the Approvals tab and click Review… on a Completed run row.
  2. Read What you release: the run's status and unit; the recipe and the parameter set, each with its approval status today and the approval in effect when the run last started; who created the run; who executed each attempt, and when; the number of attempts; the cycles by status; the start and end; and whether you may release it, and why not. Below it, the changes recorded on the run, with person, role and time.
  3. Optionally type a Release note. It is recorded with the release.
  4. Click Release and confirm. The message names the approval record.

The run log is not shown: qp reads runs, not run logs. A release is final. There is no hold or refusal: a run you do not release simply stays in the queue, unreleased, and nothing records why (see the known gaps). A released run can no longer be set back to Queued for a retry.

When you may not release a run, the row says why and Release is off. The database checks again when you click; its refusals are:

  • you created, changed or executed this run (one of its attempts): another qp person must release it;
  • a change to the run was made without a signed-in person: it cannot be released, and stays unreleased;
  • no recorded person created or started the run (a run completed before attribution was built, or written without a signed-in person): who made it is unknown, so it cannot be released and stays unreleased in the queue (see the known gaps);
  • the run is not completed, or was released meanwhile: the list is refreshed;
  • your role cannot release runs: qp releases.

The release shows in the Scheduler as a Released badge, with who released the run and when (see Scheduler), and in Approval history under Run releases, with the person and the note.

A release writes a row to medcea.run_release (the run, a digest of its row, the note, the person's account, person, name, role and source, and the time; one per run, never changed) and a row to medcea.approval with action release, the same note, and the persons who created, changed or executed the run.

My proposals and drafts

Every signed-in person sees, on the Approvals tab, their own proposals and the drafts they changed since the last approval, with where each stands: waiting for qa, returned (with qa's reason), applied, applied at once and waiting for review, withdrawn, or replaced. Withdraw… takes back your own proposal while it waits; the reason is optional and recorded. Approvers' names and notes are not shown here.

Approval history

qa, qp and auditor also see Approval history: the newest 100 approval records, filtered by All, Recipes, Control algorithms, Parameter sets, Settings and commissioning, Master data and device reviews, Run releases or Q-table retirements, each with the time, the item (a run as "Run #<id> on <unit>"), the action (approve, apply, review, return, withdraw, release, retire, grandfather), the person with role and source (for a release, who released it), the number of authors and the note. History of this item in the review shows the records of that one item.

While IAM_DB_ROLES is false the Approvals tab is not shown and nobody approves from the apps.

Every change to these definitions records who made it, with which role, and when; it is also kept in the append-only row-change log. Each approval, return and withdrawal is a row naming the person, their role and source (their own sign-in), the authors since the last approval, a digest of the content, the note or reason, and the newest change it covers. The status change an approval makes is also in the row-change log.

Retiring a Q-table

A learning controller, such as Q-learning, keeps what it learnt in a Q-table between runs. Starting a unit afresh means retiring that table. Only qa may do it, and nothing is deleted: the next run on that unit starts cold.

  1. Open the Control Models tab and find the unit in Production Units.
  2. On the row of the algorithm whose state you want to retire, click Retire….
  3. Enter the reason. A reason is required and is recorded.
  4. Confirm. The message names the unit and the number of channels retired.

Retiring needs the database to verify your sign-in, which it does only while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES are both true. Without them, nobody can retire a Q-table. Any other role gets the message that retiring learned state needs a qa person signed in.

For each channel retired, the controller-state event log gets a retired event with the unit, the algorithm, the retired state, the reason and the qa person, and the approval record gets a row naming that person. Neither record can be changed afterwards.

Scheduler

The Scheduler, titled Recipe Schedule Manager, is where runs are started, stopped and followed, and where recurring schedules are kept. A run is one execution of a recipe on a unit. Every role can open the page and sees all four sections. Once the services enforce, the Scheduler refuses the data and actions your role does not hold, as the table shows.

TaskRoles
See current and completed runsproduction_manager, maintenance, qc, qa, qp, engineer, auditor; operator for their own units only
Read a run's logproduction_manager, qa, engineer, auditor; operator for their own units only. Not maintenance, qc or qp.
Create a run (Run Now in the header)production_manager
Execute, stop or retry a run, or start a schedule with ▶ Run Nowproduction_manager; operator for a run a production manager prepared in one of their units
See schedulesoperator, production_manager, qa, engineer, auditor
Create, edit or retire a scheduleproduction_manager
See log settingsadmin, qa, engineer, auditor
Propose log settingsadmin; a different qa person approves

The page has four sections: Current Runs, Scheduled Jobs, Completed Runs and Log Management. A run's status is one of:

  • Queued: created, not yet started.
  • Running
  • Completed
  • Failed: the run failed or was stopped.

A completed run that a qp person has released shows a Released badge next to its status in Completed Runs, with who released it and when (UTC); so does the run's window with Full log ticked. The Scheduler only shows it; runs are released in the Configuration UI (see releasing a completed run). An operator still sees only their own units' runs.

Starting a run now

  1. Click Run Now at the top right.
  2. Choose the Production Unit, then a Recipe. The list offers only the recipes allowed on that unit. If the unit already has an active run, a warning asks you to finish or cancel it first, and a new run is refused.
  3. Optionally choose an Algorithm and enter Param Overrides (JSON). Once the database enforces roles, leave these empty: the run uses the approved parameter set of the recipe's assignment, and typed parameters are refused.
  4. Click Start Run. The run is created and executed in one go and appears under Current Runs.

If the run is created but cannot start, the message begins "Run created but not started", and the run waits as Queued.

Executing, stopping and retrying

Each row in Current Runs shows the buttons that fit its status:

  • ▶ Execute on a Queued run.
  • ■ Stop on a Running run. Stopping marks the run Failed, then ends the process.
  • ↺ Retry on a Failed run. Retrying sets the run back to Queued and executes it again, optionally with another algorithm.
  • 📋 Log on every run.

The arrow at the left of a row expands the cycles of the run, with each cycle's step, start and end times, status and parameters.

An operator may execute, stop or retry a run only when both of these hold:

  • a different person holding production_manager created it, or a schedule such a person created started it;
  • its unit is one of the operator's units.

This keeps preparing a run separate from starting it.

A run records who created it and who executed it, with their roles. Each cycle is recorded as the runner completes it. A run started by a schedule is recorded with the actor system:scheduler, on behalf of the person who created the schedule.

Schedules

  1. Click New Schedule.
  2. Choose the Production Unit and the Recipe.
  3. Pick a Frequency: Daily, Weekly, Monthly, Hourly or Run Once. Set the days, the date and the time. All times are UTC. Generated Schedule shows the result.
  4. Choose the Parameter set. While the database enforces roles, you must choose an approved set, and the schedule runs that set's algorithm and parameters. Otherwise the list is for information only, and the schedule runs the Algorithm and Param Overrides (JSON) fields below it.
  5. Optionally add a Description, leave Enabled on, and click Save.

In Scheduled Jobs, each schedule has three buttons:

  • ▶ Run Now starts the schedule's recipe at once.
  • Edit changes the schedule.
  • Retire stops the schedule from firing.

A schedule is retired, never deleted. A retired schedule stays in the record and cannot be edited or enabled again. Create a new schedule instead.

Run logs

Click 📋 Log on a current run, or View Log on a completed one, to open the run's log. For a running run, the log follows new lines as they are written.

A role that does not hold read access on run logs sees the run but not its log: the window shows "(your role does not hold R on run logs)", where R means read. This applies to maintenance, qc and qp while IAM_ENFORCE is true. While it is false, the log is shown and the service records a "would withhold" line.

Log Management: compression, never deletion

No person compresses or deletes a run log. The scheduler's own nightly job runs every day at 03:15 UTC. It compresses (gzips) the log of each run that has finished, Completed or Failed, once the log is older than the Compress run logs after setting. It never touches the log of a run that is queued or running, and it never deletes a log. A compressed log still opens from View Log.

The Log Management section shows:

  • the number of run log files, their total size and the oldest one;
  • when the job last ran and how many files it compressed or kept;
  • the smallest values allowed: 1 day for compression and 30 days for the retention value.

Retention setting (not applied) is kept as a value but nothing reads it, because run logs are never deleted.

  1. As admin, change Compress run logs after (in days) and click Propose retention.
  2. While the database enforces roles, the message says the value is proposed and "pending qa approval". The new value applies only after a qa person other than you approves it in the Configuration UI's Approvals tab; until then the old value stays in force. While the database does not enforce roles, the value is saved at once and the message reads "Defaults saved".
  3. Under the settings, a Reminder line names your proposal, its values and when you sent it, with a link, See where it stands in the Approvals tab, until the proposed value is in force. The reminder is kept in this browser for your account only and does not know whether the proposal was returned or withdrawn: where it stands (applied, returned, withdrawn) is under My proposals and drafts on the Approvals tab, where you may also withdraw it. Dismiss hides the line.
  4. While your proposal for a setting waits, you may propose that setting again: the newer proposal replaces yours. Another person's proposal for a setting that already waits is refused ("already waits for qa"): it is approved or returned by qa, or withdrawn by its proposer, first.

A proposed setting is stored as a pending change with its author. The approval records the qa person who confirmed it.

Operators see only their own units

An operator sees only the runs, cycles and logs of the units assigned to them. A run in another unit looks the same as a run that does not exist. The filter applies while IAM_ENFORCE or IAM_DB_ROLES is true. An operator with no unit assigned sees no runs and can start none. Units are assigned with apps/iam/units.sh and confirmed by qa in the User Manager's Decisions tab, as described in the administration chapter.

Run Monitor

The Run Monitor is a live view of every run that has not completed: running, queued (shown as Scheduled) and failed runs. Each run gets its own card. The Monitor is for watching. It has no buttons that change anything.

The roles admitted are operator, production_manager, maintenance, qc, qa, qp, engineer and auditor, and since IAM 3.2b bio_scientist and control_scientist, who may read telemetry but not runs: the Monitor lists runs, so for them it shows "No active runs at this time." or a refusal, and they read telemetry in the AI Workbench instead. An operator sees only the units assigned to them, under the same rule as in the Scheduler: runs, readings and actions of other units are not shown, and a reading that carries no zone is never shown to an operator.

Each card shows the unit code and type, the run number, the recipe, the status and the start time, with three panels:

  • Steps: each step of the recipe, its status and the cycles completed out of the total. The current step is highlighted.
  • Live Measurements: the latest value of each sensor reading for the unit. Air Temp, CO2, Humidity and Substrate Temp are drawn as bars against the current step's limits, which are shown in brackets. A bar marks whether the value is inside or outside the limits, and "no limits set" means the step has none for that reading.
  • Actions: the latest command sent to each actuator, for example a light or a fan, with its time.

The page refreshes itself every 30 seconds. Click ↻ Refresh to refresh at once. "No active runs at this time." means that no run you may see is open.

Database Management

Database Management exports database objects as an SQL file, for evidence, for an inspection or for a copy kept outside the platform. Only qa, qp and auditor can open it. It always enforces the policy, whatever IAM_ENFORCE says, because an export takes data out of the platform.

The page lists only the objects in the export groups your role holds, and states them in a line that begins "You may export:".

  • qa and auditor may export master data, devices, device history, recipes, controllers, controller state, runs, schedules, scheduler settings, telemetry and the audit histories.
  • qp may export runs, telemetry and the audit histories.

The page offers only the platform's own data tables and views. The account records, unit assignments and access policy that belong to the same groups are not offered here, and the audit histories offered are the platform's approvals, pending changes and row-change log.
