Sequences and types hold no rows and are offered as definitions to everyone who can open the page.

  1. In DDL & Data Export, tick DDL (the table definition) and/or Data (the rows) for each object you need. All DDL, All Data and Clear set or clear every box. For views, sequences and types the Data box is disabled; only their definition is exported.
  2. Keep or change the File Name.
  3. Under Save To, choose one destination:
    • Download locally puts the file on your computer.
    • Save to platform (apps/database/exports/) writes it on the platform host. It refuses a name that already exists there.
  4. Click ↓ Export SQL. The result line shows the file name, its size, the export number and the start of its sha256 checksum.

The file begins with a header naming:

  • the export number
  • when it was generated, in UTC
  • who exported it, and with which role
  • the groups it covers

If one selected object is outside your groups, the whole export is refused and nothing is logged.

Every export writes one row to the export log (iam_policy.export_log). The row holds the time, the person, the groups, and each object with whether its DDL or how many rows were exported. The row is written before the file is handed over, and nobody can change or delete it afterwards. The full sha256 of the file is written to the Database Management service log; keep the checksum with the file, so you can show later that the file is the one exported.

User Manager

The User Manager is where access to the platform is decided and checked:

  • access requests
  • accounts and their roles
  • the decisions qa must confirm
  • the e-mail the platform sends
  • the quarterly access review

The roles admitted are admin, qa, qp and auditor. Each person sees only the tabs their role may use:

TabWhat it is forWho sees it
RequestsApprove or reject access requests (Pending, Approved, Rejected, All)admin
UsersAccounts (Active, Inactive, Awaiting approval, All): deactivate, activate, change role, set the personadmin
DecisionsAccount decisions (Waiting for qa, To review, All)admin and auditor read them; qa confirms, refuses and reviews them
E-mail settingsThe sending mail accountadmin
TemplatesThe texts of the e-mails sent to applicantsadmin
Access reviewThe quarterly review of who holds whatqa creates and signs it off; qp and auditor read it
Change logChanges to the mail settings and templatesqa, qp, auditor

The full access process, including what the applicant sees, is in the access chapter; dual control and the review are explained in the compliance chapter. The main tasks are below.

Deciding an access request (admin)

  1. Open Requests, tab Pending, and select a request.
  2. Under Decision, choose Approve or Reject. For an approval, check Role to grant. It starts as the requested role, and you may reassign it.
  3. Write a note. A rejection needs one. Check the e-mail to the applicant, which is pre-filled from the template; untick Send e-mail to the applicant's address to send nothing.
  4. Confirm. An approved request waits for qa: the account stays disabled, with no role, until a qa person confirms the decision, and the approval e-mail goes out only then. A rejection takes effect at once: the e-mail goes out, and the pending account is deleted so the person may apply again. The request record keeps who asked, for what, and why it was refused.

Changing an account (admin)

In Users, select an account. Deactivating, changing the role, proposing a person and withdrawing a decision each need a reason; for an activation the reason is optional. The reason is recorded with the decision. You cannot change your own account.

  • Deactivate account acts at once. The person is signed out everywhere and their MQTT credentials are revoked. Their data, role and history are kept.
  • Activate (waits for qa) waits for confirmation, also for an account with no role.
  • Change role acts at once when the new role holds less, a narrowing; it takes effect within a few minutes, without signing the person out. When the new role adds any right, the button reads Change (waits for qa) and the page lists the rights it adds.
  • Person: enter the New person_id the account belongs to and click Propose (waits for qa). This always waits for qa.
  • While a decision about the account waits for qa, the role and the person cannot be changed until it is decided or withdrawn with Withdraw decision. Deactivating the account is always possible and withdraws the waiting decision.

Confirming decisions (qa)

  1. Open Decisions, tab Waiting for qa, and select a decision. The detail lists the effect and the access it adds. For a new applicant it also lists the existing admin and qa accounts, flagging any that may be the same person.
  2. Write a note (required to refuse) and click Confirm or Refuse.
  3. Decisions that acted at once (deactivations, narrowings) appear under To review. Click Mark reviewed after checking them.

You cannot check a decision that you made, or one about your own account. The page says why when that is the case.

E-mail settings and templates (admin)

Until e-mail is set up, a banner at the top says that nothing is sent and messages go to the service log.

  1. In E-mail settings, enter the Gmail address, the App password and the Sender name. The panel beside the form explains how to get an app password. Use Another mail server for a different SMTP host.
  2. Click Verify and propose (waits for qa). The login is checked first. The change is used only after a qa person confirms it, because the same account sends the password-reset e-mails.
  3. Send test e-mail to me checks the current account.

In Templates, select a template, edit it and click Save template, or Reset to default. A template change applies at once.

Every request, decision, confirmation, refusal and review is kept in append-only histories with who and when. Mail settings and template changes are listed in the Change log (when, what, change, by).

The quarterly access review (qa)

  1. Open Access review and click Create this quarter's review. The page takes a snapshot of:
    • accounts, roles and units
    • decisions waiting and decisions made since the last review
    • waivers and switches
    The snapshot never changes afterwards.
  2. Read the review, including Unexplained at the time of the review.
  3. Optionally write a note and click Sign off. Signing off cannot be undone, and it accepts any unexplained roles listed.

Access Policy

The Access Policy shows who may do what on the platform: the access policy that is applied now, its earlier versions, and its history. Open it from the portal's Access Policy card or from the User Manager's Access Policy link. Since slice 4.4 (November 2026) an admin drafts a change there and a qa person approves or returns it; a qa person also confirms (or disputes) a version that was applied without approval beforehand. An approved version waits, with status approved, until the host operator applies it with apply.sh (slice 4.5, not built yet). See Changing the access policy for who does what.

It always enforces its own rules: your account needs a person_id and exactly one platform role, and if your account changed since you signed in (a role, a deactivation, a decision), every request answers "your access changed since you signed in: reload the page".

TabWho sees itWhat it shows
Matrixadmin, qa, auditorOne row per resource group, one column per role; a cell lists the levels the role holds (a dashed letter is limited by a constraint). Every role, group and level name shows its explanation when you point at it. Red: the policy's checker refuses the cell; amber: a recorded waiver allows it; "not granted": no role holds anything on the group in this version. Click a cell for what it gives: the database objects, the routes, the sign-ins, the MQTT topics that only this cell gives the role, the group's members, the constraint's meaning and any waiver. A version selector shows every version with its kind and status. On a skeleton version a banner says its entries are the applied ones and what changes is the platform skeleton (see its diff under Approval). On a draft an admin edits cells here (below).
Changesadmin, qa, auditorOne row per version: kind, status, its makers (for a skeleton version the person apply.sh --by claimed, labelled "claimed by apply.sh --by"), its checkers, the reason, a qa person's return note, and the git commit of a skeleton version. An admin also sees New draft (from the applied version, from the factory file or from an earlier version, with a required reason; disabled while another version is open, naming it), and on the open version Submit for approval and Withdraw…, each with a reason and a confirmation.
ApprovalqaThe submitted versions, each with the diff to approve or return (below).
Who can…admin, qa, auditorPick an object (a table, a function, a route, a host, an MQTT topic, a tool, or a whole resource group) and, optionally, a level: the roles that reach it in the chosen version, with the levels and constraints that give it, and the service identities that reach it.
Service identitiesadmin, qa, auditorEach service's database role, MQTT login and ceiling (the most it may ever hold), and the name of the config.env key holding its password (never the password).
Break-glassadmin, qa, auditorThe break-glass identities the policy names, who uses them and where their use is logged, and every policy break-glass still open (event, time, components). The reason is in History.
Historyqa, qp, auditorEvery event of the policy's record, newest first (at most 500; choose a version to narrow it): who, when, the reason and the details. Above it, whether the record is intact (the hash chain: green "Record intact", red with the first broken event, grey "cannot verify"), and what the version audit finds.
ConfirmqaThe bootstrap and rollback versions waiting for confirmation, with when they were applied and the person who ran apply.sh.

Confirming a version

A bootstrap or rollback version is applied at once by the person who runs apply.sh. A qa person then confirms that it is what was intended, or disputes it:

  1. Read the version in the Matrix tab and its events in the History tab.
  2. In the Confirm tab, tick "I did not run apply.sh for this version.", write a note, and press Confirm. To dispute it instead, write a note and press Dispute…: a dispute is permanent, the version stays disputed until a later version supersedes it, and the health check reports it as a failure.

The confirmation goes to the database with your own sign-in token, and the database checks it: it refuses a person who ran apply.sh for that version ("You applied version N yourself"), a missing note or statement, a version already confirmed or disputed, and a request that did not come through the Policy UI's sign-in. A confirmation is an attributable record (your account, your person, the time, your note), not an electronic signature. It needs IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES to be true; otherwise the page says confirming is not possible yet.

Drafting a change (admin)

  1. In Changes, under New draft, choose what it starts from (the applied version, the factory file or an earlier version), write the reason and press Create the draft. Only one change or skeleton version can be open at a time: "version N is still open: take it through or withdraw it first".
  2. Open the draft in Matrix; the page is in edit mode. Click a cell. The panel has one box per level and, per level held, a constraint (none by default). Levels a held level implies are ticked for you. Write a reason and press Save the cell. Each save replaces the whole cell and records the checker's verdict on the whole draft; a cell the checker refuses still saves and turns red, with the rules it breaks.
  3. Back in Changes, press Submit for approval. It stays disabled while the checker lists errors; iam-api runs the checker again on what is stored before it submits ("version N fails the checker now" otherwise). A submitted version is frozen: nobody edits it.
  4. To give up, press Withdraw… on the open version (any admin, whatever its status, approved included). A withdrawn version stays in the history and is never applied.

A version qa returns becomes a draft again, with the return note shown as "Returned by qa"; edit and submit it again, or withdraw it.

The Approval screen (qa)

Open a submitted version under Approval. The page shows, in this order:

  • The header: version, kind, reason, makers, and which version it was drafted on against the applied one. For a skeleton version: "a git change", the commit, and the person apply.sh --propose-skeleton --by named, as a claim (the reason also names the username and the OS account that ran it).
  • "This version widens a role you hold.", first: any role it gives more to that you hold, directly or through the roles yours includes (qa includes auditor). You may not approve such a version, and Approve stays disabled. This covers the role you signed in with only; your other accounts are checked when it is applied (4.5). A version that adds to qa or auditor therefore cannot be approved on this screen at all.
  • The diff: per role what it adds and removes (database privileges, routes, hosts, MQTT topics, tool settings, levels), the changed cells (before → after), the waivers, what changes for services and anonymous callers, and for a skeleton version the sections of the skeleton that change.
  • "Approval — an attributable record, not an electronic signature", a required note, and for a skeleton version the box "I did not run apply.sh --propose-skeleton for this version."

The page hashes the diff exactly as it received and shows it (SHA-256) and sends that digest, the version's entries digest (taken from the same diff) and the skeleton the diff was computed on with the approval. iam-api then computes the diff again (a change) or compares it with the one the host stored (a skeleton version) before it forwards your approval, with your own sign-in token, to the database, which checks it all again. So what is approved is exactly what you saw, on the skeleton you saw it on. If the diff did not arrive intact ("The diff did not load intact: reload the page."), or the Policy service runs another skeleton than this host accepted, the page says so and Approve stays disabled.

Refusals you may meet: you are one of its authors ("another qa person approves it"); it changed since you opened it ("reload the page"); it is no longer submitted; another version was applied since it was drafted ("return it"); a policy break-glass is open ("approvals wait until a normal deploy … ends it"); a skeleton version proposed against an older accepted skeleton ("return it and propose again"); a version that widens your role. Return… needs a note and a confirmation; returning a skeleton version withdraws it, and the host operator proposes again. After an approval the page says "Version N approved. It is applied when the host operator runs apply.sh (IAM 4.5)."

MQTT Explorer

MQTT Explorer is a browser tool that shows the messages the boards and the platform exchange on the message broker. Only engineer can open it. It is meant for commissioning and fault finding.

  • The left column lists every topic under the platform's topic root (medcea/ by default) that you may read, seen since the page opened, with its last value.
  • Click a topic to see its latest messages; the page keeps the last 100 for each topic.
  • The status at the top reads Connected, Reconnecting, Waiting for the broker to accept your credential, or an error.

How the page connects depends on the switch IAM_MQTT_PERSON (see IAM switches).

With your own credential (IAM_MQTT_PERSON=true). When the page opens it asks the platform for a broker credential that is yours alone, made from your role, and connects with it. You see nothing of this: no password is shown or stored in the browser.

  • The first connection can take up to 15 seconds: the broker picks up a new credential at its next check.
  • The credential lasts 8 hours and is renewed in the background before it ends. Signing out of the platform does not end it at once; deactivating your account or changing your role ends it within about 15 seconds.
  • The Your rights note under the Publish box lists the topic patterns your role may read and send to, for every unit (for engineer, send: medcea/+/+/control/#). An engineer reads every unit's sense, control and control_setpoint topics, and may send only to a unit's control topics while that unit is flagged for commissioning and has no run in progress. The broker checks this every 15 seconds: when the flag is cleared or a run starts, sending to that unit stops.
  • A unit is flagged for commissioning in the Configuration UI's Units tab (see commissioning a unit): an engineer proposes it and a qa person approves it. The broker follows the flag within about 15 seconds, without a reload. The Your rights note does not change with the flag: it shows your role's patterns, not which units are flagged. Send is enabled whenever your role has a send pattern, and stays disabled for a role without one.
  • A message the broker does not allow is dropped silently: the page shows no error, and nobody receives it.
  • The topics in Your rights are patterns (+ is one level, # the rest). Send to one topic they match, for example medcea/UNIT01/ZONE01/control/<tag>. A topic with + or # is refused by the page before it is sent: the broker would close the connection instead.
  • If the page says your role has no MQTT rights, or that your access changed since you signed in, reload it after your administrator has sorted out your role.

With IAM_MQTT_PERSON=false the page connects to nothing. It says "The MQTT Explorer needs IAM_MQTT_PERSON=true" and shows no topics. The shared account it used to connect with was removed on 1 November 2026, after the personal credentials had run for a trial period. Ask an administrator to turn the switch back on.

To send a message:

  1. Click a topic, or type one in the Publish box. The page refuses a topic with + or # ("Not sent: + and # only match topics") instead of sending it.
  2. Enter the payload, as text or JSON.
  3. Click Send.

A message sent to a control topic switches real equipment. The broker accepts it only for a unit flagged for commissioning with no run in progress, but that unit is still a live chamber.

Node-RED

Node-RED runs the flow that receives telemetry from the broker and writes it to the database. Its editor is where that flow, and any other integration flow, is changed. Only engineer can open it.

After the platform sign-in, the editor signs you in a second time with the same account. You normally see no form: the page reloads once and the editor opens. Your rights in the editor come from the access policy. engineer may change and deploy flows, use inject and debug, and edit context and the library. Nobody installs palette modules from the editor: the modules the platform needs are listed in the repository and installed by ./apps/node-red/deploy.sh. Every deploy you make is logged with your username.

The editor session lasts one hour from when you opened the editor. Then the editor signs you in again, without a prompt while your platform sign-in lasts, as whoever is signed in on that browser now. If you have changes you have not deployed at that moment, the browser first asks whether to leave the page. Leaving loses those changes. If you stay, the editor cannot deploy until it is reloaded, so export your flows first (menu → Export), reload, and import them. Deploy or export before the hour is up. The platform asked for about five minutes, but that would interrupt editing far too often, so the editor keeps one hour. If your role changes, your editor rights change at your next editor sign-in. If your account is deactivated, the platform sign-in ends your access within about four minutes.

The editor keeps your sign-in in the browser. If someone else signs in to the platform on the same browser within that hour, the editor still acts as you, and deploys are logged with your name. This is a known gap: sign out and close the browser before you hand it over. Editor sessions started before 1 November 2026 were set to last a week; the deploy of that day ended them.

The flows in the repository are the reference. The health check compares the flows Node-RED runs with them every time it runs: a flow from the repository that was changed or deleted in the editor shows as a failure, and a flow built only in the editor shows as a warning. To make an editor change permanent, export the flow and commit it to the repository; to undo an editor change, run ./apps/node-red/deploy.sh.

The flows connect to the message broker as the node-red service account, which reads every topic under the platform root, and to the database with the ingest login svc_ingest, which may read master data and insert telemetry rows. Code in a flow runs with both. A broken or deleted ingest flow stops telemetry from being stored. Export a copy of the flow before you change it. The flow kept in the repository is apps/node-red/flows/message-processing.json.

Grafana

Grafana shows telemetry as dashboards. The portal's Grafana card opens it, and you are signed in with your platform account without a further prompt. The provided dashboard, IoT Environmental Monitoring, has these panels:

  • live readings of air temperature, relative humidity and CO2
  • historical trends of the same readings
  • a summary of the latest readings by zone and device

Grafana gives each person one of its own roles, from the access policy's Grafana dashboards row:

  • Editor, for engineer: edits dashboards and opens Explore;
  • Viewer, for production_manager, maintenance, qc, qa, qp, auditor: views dashboards;
  • no sign-in for every other role (operator, admin, bio_scientist, control_scientist) and for an account without a platform role.

A person Grafana refuses sees Grafana's own sign-in page with a message that the login was denied, not the platform's "No access" page. Nobody gets Grafana Admin through the platform sign-in: only Grafana's local break-glass account holds it (see Credentials).

A Grafana session ends after 5 minutes without use, and at the latest one hour after you signed in to Grafana, even while you use it. A tab you keep using is not interrupted before the hour, so a panel you are building is not lost every 5 minutes. A restart of Grafana (a deploy) does not end it. When the session ends, the page reloads once by itself, and Grafana sends you through the platform sign-in again without asking for your password while that sign-in lasts. You come back to the same dashboard, as whoever is signed in on that browser now, and your role is checked again. A dashboard that refreshes itself keeps refreshing after that reload. Dashboard changes you have not saved are lost at the reload, so save as you go. Grafana's live updates keep their connection until you close the tab.

The platform's dashboards change only through the repository (apps/report/dashboards/) and the report deploy. An Editor who changes one of them in the browser cannot save it. An Editor can create a new dashboard, or copy one with "Save as", and keep it: Grafana keeps it, with folders, annotations and alert rules, in its own volume across restarts, and other Grafana users can see it. Such a dashboard is not part of the record and is not reviewed: it changes whenever an Editor saves it, and a reinstall of the platform deletes it (the uninstall lists Grafana's volume before it asks). The nightly backup copies Grafana's whole database, so an administrator can put it back after a loss, but only as a whole and only to the state of a night within the last two weeks (Backup and restore). Put a dashboard the platform should always have in the repository; to move one there, export it (Share → Export → Save to file) and add the file to apps/report/dashboards/.

Dashboards read through Grafana's own database account, so a viewer sees telemetry of every unit; that is why an operator, who sees only their own units in the Run Monitor, has no Grafana. A viewer can also send Grafana any query that account may run, even though the Explore screen is hidden from Viewers: what limits a viewer is the database account's read access (master data, measurements and runs), not the dashboards.

AI Workbench (JupyterHub)

The AI Workbench is JupyterHub: notebooks for data analysis and for developing control algorithms. It is meant for bio_scientist and control_scientist work.

  • Each person gets their own workspace with persistent storage (a home folder of its own). It starts when you sign in and is stopped after an hour without activity; your files are kept, and copied every night into the platform's backup. Keep your work in git all the same: see Saving your notebooks to git.
  • The workspace opens in JupyterLab, and VS Code is available inside it.
  • The first start of a new workspace takes a few minutes: real-time collaboration and VS Code are installed into your home folder before the workspace opens. The very first workspace on a newly installed host also downloads the notebook image (about 2 GB) first; the install does not wait for that download. Later starts are fast. Wait on the start page; the hub waits up to 15 minutes.
  • The workspace runs as the container's own account, jovyan, so file paths start with /home/jovyan. You are still shown as yourself: a terminal's prompt reads <your username>@workbench, and your commits carry your username as the author name (see Saving your notebooks to git).
  1. Click the JupyterHub card on the portal. You go straight to the platform sign-in.
  2. Wait while your workspace starts, then work in JupyterLab.

Who may sign in comes from the access policy: admin, bio_scientist, control_scientist, engineer, qa. Anyone else sees the workbench's "No access to AI Workbench" page. Every person admitted gets a full workspace that runs code; the workbench has no read-only mode.

The administrator (admin) is the hub's administrator, through a hub role made for this (workbench-admin), not JupyterHub's built-in administrator. From the hub's admin page the administrator can list the people and their workspaces, start and stop workspaces and remove a person from the hub. The administrator cannot open your workspace or your notebooks, cannot create a token in your name, cannot share your workspace with anyone and cannot change your hub account. If the administrator follows a link to your workspace, the hub answers "403 Forbidden"; it never offers an "Authorize" button for someone else's workspace. Removing a person from the hub, or a role that no longer includes the workbench, keeps your home folder: your files come back if access is restored.

Your workbench sign-in lasts 5 minutes. The next workbench page then signs you in again without a prompt while your platform sign-in lasts, and checks your role again. A JupyterLab tab that stays open does not do this by itself. About 5 to 10 minutes after the tab was loaded, saving, opening files and starting kernels answer "Forbidden". Reload the tab, or open the workbench in another tab, and everything works again. Your unsaved changes stay in the open tab and save once it works again. Running kernels and their open connections are not affected, and your workspace is not stopped any sooner. If a different person is now signed in to the platform on that browser, they are refused your workspace ("403 Forbidden"); they never see it.

File → Log Out (and the hub's Logout) signs you out of the whole platform, like Sign out in the user menu: the workbench ends its session, the platform sign-in page asks "Do you want to log out?", and you land on the portal's sign-in page. Your workspace keeps running until it is stopped for inactivity.

Reading platform data from a notebook

Every workspace has a read-only folder medcea-examples in its home folder, kept up to date by the platform. If you do not see it, your workspace was started before the folder was added: choose File → Hub Control Panel, Stop My Server, then Start My Server.

  • 01-connect.ipynb: signs you in and lists the units and zones your role may see.
  • 02-air-temperature.ipynb: a line chart of one zone's air temperature over the last minutes. The unit, the zone and the number of minutes are set at the top.
  • SAVE_TO_GIT.md: how to keep your notebooks in the research repository (below).
  • medcea_data.py: the small library the notebooks use (import medcea_data as md works in any notebook).
  • nb_output_guard.py: the check git runs before each commit (see Saving your notebooks to git); you do not run it yourself.

To change a notebook, copy it into your home folder first (right-click, Copy, then Paste in your folder, or File > Save Notebook As).

  1. Open 01-connect.ipynb and run the first cell. It shows a link and a short code.
  2. Open the link in a browser tab, sign in if asked, and confirm the code. The cell finishes once you have; the code is valid for about ten minutes.
  3. Run the other cells. md.units(), md.zones() and md.measurements(unit, zone, tag="air_temperature", minutes=60) return tables (pandas DataFrames). md.measurements returns at most 50,000 readings, the most recent ones; when the window holds more, it warns, and the chart's title says "latest N readings". Shorten the minutes, or pass a larger limit=.

The notebook reads as you: every request carries your own sign-in, and the database applies your role and its row rules. You read exactly what your role may read in the database, nothing more: bio_scientist and control_scientist read master data, recipes, controllers and telemetry (no runs, no export). A notebook only reads: the database refuses any change made with a notebook sign-in ("may only read"), whatever your role may change in the Configuration UI. A refusal because your role may not read a table says so ("your role may not read this"); signing in again does not change it. The sign-in lives only in the notebook's memory; it is renewed while you work and is gone when the kernel stops or your platform session ends (deactivation ends it within five minutes). Then run md.sign_in() again. Nothing in your workspace holds a platform password.

The notebooks reach the platform only while the platform verifies each person's sign-in at the database (IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES both true). Otherwise md.sign_in() says it cannot reach the platform. A workspace started before the administrator turned this on must be stopped and started again (File > Hub Control Panel).

Telemetry is regulated data, and the scientist roles may read it, not export it. Keep it on the platform: do not copy values into a code or text cell, a file you download, or a message. When you commit a notebook to git, its outputs are removed automatically (below); code and text cells are not checked.

Saving your notebooks to git

Your home folder is kept when your workspace stops, when the administrator removes you from the hub and when your role loses the workbench, and since 1 November 2026 the platform copies it every night into a backup on the same machine, kept for two weeks by default. That copy is for an administrator to restore after a loss (the whole home, or one file, as it was on a night), not a history of your work, and uninstalling the platform deletes the home itself. Keep work that matters in the research repository on GitHub that the administrator set up (WORKBENCH_NOTEBOOK_REPO; a terminal in your workspace prints it with echo "$MEDCEA_NOTEBOOK_REPO"). SAVE_TO_GIT.md has the details.

  1. Once: set your e-mail for commits (git config --global user.email "you@example.org", preferably your GitHub account's). The author name is already your username; set user.name yourself to use your full name, and the workspace keeps it. Then create a fine-grained personal access token on GitHub for that repository only (Contents: Read and write, with an expiry date), and clone the repository into your home folder from a terminal. Git asks for your GitHub username and the token, and keeps the token in your home folder only (~/.git-credentials).
  2. Save your notebooks inside the cloned folder.
  3. In the Git panel on the left: stage the changed files (+), write a message, Commit, then Push. Pull brings in your colleagues' work.

Each workspace has nbstripout set up as a git filter at every start: a committed notebook keeps its code and text but none of its outputs (tables, charts, printed values), while the notebook on disk keeps them. If nbstripout could not be installed, git refuses to commit a notebook rather than store its outputs. Behind the filter, a check runs before every commit and refuses it while a notebook still holds outputs (for example in a repository whose own settings turn the filter off): the commit fails with "Commit refused" and the file names. Clear the outputs (Edit > Clear Outputs of All Cells), save, stage the file again and commit. If you set your own git hooks folder (core.hooksPath), the workspace leaves it alone and this check does not run. Commits carry your own name and GitHub account; the platform holds no GitHub credential.

pgAdmin

pgAdmin is a database browser for the administrator, who uses it to see the database schema and the configuration. Only accounts holding admin can sign in; the access policy decides this. pgAdmin sends you straight to the platform sign-in; its MedCEA sign-in button appears only after you used pgAdmin's own Logout. Anyone else is sent back to pgAdmin's login page with a message that they are not authorized. pgAdmin checks the role only at sign-in. Open it from the pgAdmin card on the portal's Platform Management page (every role sees the card; only admin gets in), or at https://pgadmin.<domain>.

After signing in you find the server IoT Postgres (group MedCEA) in the browser tree. It is your own copy, loaded when pgAdmin starts for every enabled account that may sign in and has an e-mail address in Keycloak; it connects without asking for a password. If you were given the admin role after pgAdmin last started, the server is missing until an administrator runs ./deploy-infra.sh. Server entries, saved queries and settings you add in pgAdmin are lost when it restarts: it keeps no data between starts.

pgAdmin uses one of two database logins:

  • While the services use their own database roles (IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES=true), it connects as rr_admin_login. That login owns nothing and reads only what the admin role may read. It cannot change regulated data or the histories.
  • While that switch is false, it connects as the database superuser.

pgAdmin signs you out after 60 minutes without use, and a pgAdmin session lasts at most one day. Then pgAdmin signs you in again without a prompt, as whoever is signed in to the platform on that browser now. pgAdmin cannot be given a shorter session (its start-up accepts only whole days), so it does not follow Sign out or a change of person at once. This is a known gap: use pgAdmin's own Logout before you hand over the browser.

Do not change the database schema here. Schema changes are applied by the database deploy, as described in the administration chapter.

Edge devices

The boards in the growing chambers are ESP32 microcontrollers. A SENSE board reads the chamber and publishes measurements. A CONTROL board receives the scheduler's commands and switches equipment through relays. Each board fills one slot: one unit, one zone, one role. A board joins the fleet through onboarding. Someone plugs it into the platform host over USB, signs in, picks a free slot and says what is wired to it. The onboarding run then erases and flashes the board, joins it to WiFi, gives it its own broker credential and registers it in that slot. This chapter covers who may do that, the steps of a run in the browser and in a terminal, how to replace or move a board, how board credentials work, and what the platform records.

SENSE and CONTROL boards

Every board runs the same application. What makes it a SENSE or a CONTROL board is a small configuration file that onboarding writes onto it. That file holds the role, the slot, the broker credential and, for a SENSE board, its sensors and their pins. A CONTROL board also gets a pin map file with the relay channels chosen during the run.

RoleWhat it doesMQTT topics
SENSEReads its sensors at a fixed interval (30 seconds by default) and publishes one message per measurement tag. A SENSE board may carry several sensors.Publishes to medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>/sense/<tag>
CONTROLDrives a relay board. It receives the scheduler's commands for its zone, switches the relay channels and reports what it actually did.Receives medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>/control/#; reports on medcea/<UNIT>/<ZONE>/control_setpoint/...

The topic root is the platform's project name, medcea. The unit and zone segments are the codes as the Units tab shows them.

The sensors the SENSE template offers today:

SensorTags it publishes
Sensirion SCD40 / SCD41co2, air_temperature, air_humidity
Sensirion SCD30co2, air_temperature, air_humidity
DS18B20 substrate probeground_temperature
Analogue water level probewater_level

The CONTROL template describes an 8 or 16 channel relay board. Its equipment is the red, blue and UV grow lights, the air heater pad, the air cooler, the humidifier, the air flow fan, the CO2 fan, the CO2 flow steriliser and the substrate heater.

Slots

A unit is a physical chamber and a zone is one area inside it. Both are created in the Units tab of the Configuration UI before any board is onboarded. The onboarding wizard reads units and zones from the database and never creates them.

Each active zone has exactly two slots, one per role:

UNIT01 / ZONE01  ->  unit01-s-zone01   SENSE: reads the chamber
                     unit01-c-zone01   CONTROL: drives the chamber
  • A slot is free when no active device of that role is in the zone. Only free slots are offered.
  • A slot is taken when an active device of that role is in it. The wizard lists taken slots with their board, but you cannot pick them.
  • A slot is not offered when its unit and zone codes cannot make a legal board name. The wizard says why. See Device names.
  • Slots in an inactive unit or an inactive zone are left out.

This rule is read from the live device table on every run. To free a slot, mark its device Inactive in the Devices tab. Onboarding a board into a slot that already has a device record updates that record in place. It never creates a second one.

The one-SENSE, one-CONTROL rule also limits harm. A board can publish only under its own slot's topics, and a second board cannot quietly start driving a chamber that already has a controller.

Device names

A board has one name, built from its slot:

lower(unit code) - s|c - lower(zone code)

UNIT02 + CONTROL + ZONE02  ->  unit02-c-zone02

The same name is the board's DHCP hostname, its Hostname (DHCP) in the Devices tab, the device_dns field in every MQTT message, its broker user name, and the file name of its provisioning record. Lowercasing is the only change made to the codes, so the name stays readable and is the same every time the slot is onboarded. Unit codes are unique ignoring case, and zone codes are unique within their unit ignoring case, so two slots can never get the same name.

The codes must produce a legal hostname:

  • letters, digits and hyphens only, with no hyphen at the start or the end;
  • at most 32 characters, the longest hostname a MicroPython board accepts.

A slot whose codes break either rule is listed as not offered, with the reason, for example a code containing an underscore or a space. There is no fallback name. To use the slot, rename the unit or zone code in the Units tab. After the board boots, the run reads the hostname back from the board and warns if the board kept a different one.

A board onboarded with --unregistered is named cea-un-<mac6>, where <mac6> is the last six hex digits of its MAC address. The un shows at a glance that it has no slot.

The board does not answer .local names: it runs no mDNS responder. Use the IP address printed at the end of the run and kept in the provisioning record. A DHCP reservation on the router keeps that address stable.

Who may onboard

The access policy (apps/iam/policy/defaults.yaml) gives these rights on boards:

RoleBoards and onboardingSlot history
engineerOnboards boards into any free slot, replaces and moves boards, edits device records.Read
maintenanceRe-onboards a board into a slot that already exists: a replacement for an inactive board, or a re-flash in place. Can change a device record that stays in its slot, for example to mark it Inactive. Creating a new slot is refused, with the message that a new slot is an engineer's change.Read
qaReads and exports devices, and reviews (approves) a device change made by someone else. qa may not review their own change.Read and export (qa holds everything the auditor holds)
auditorReads and exports devices.Read and export
operator, production_manager, adminRead devices only.None
qc, qp, bio_scientist, control_scientistNo access to device records.None

The platform database enforces these rights (with IAM_DB_ROLES=true, as on this platform). The wizard reads and writes the platform database as the person who signed in. Only engineer and maintenance can sign in to the wizard; the access policy decides this. Anyone else who signs in gets a page saying that one of these roles is required to provision hardware, and is never offered Start. A terminal run refuses them in the same way, before anything is touched. For maintenance the wizard does not itself check that the slot already exists: if a maintenance person chooses an empty slot, the board is erased and flashed, and only then does the database refuse the registration. Choose an existing slot.

Before the first onboarding

These are one-time tasks for the administrator of the platform host.

  1. Serial port access. The account that runs the wizard must be in the dialout group. ./deploy-infra.sh adds it, as its last step. The wizard service keeps its old group list until its user service manager restarts, and that restart closes every desktop program of the account, so ./deploy-infra.sh restarts it only when it runs over SSH or on a text console with no desktop session of the account open. On a desktop it says so instead, and ./install.sh repeats it at the end: reboot the host once after the install. To do it all by hand, signed out of the desktop (from SSH or a text console): 
    sudo usermod -aG dialout "$USER"
sudo systemctl restart "user@$(id -u)"
    Logging out of the desktop is not enough: the wizard service keeps the user service manager running (linger). Confirm with id -nG in a new login.
  2. The wizard service. ./install.sh and ./deploy-apps.sh --all run ./apps/edge-wizard/deploy.sh. It installs the cea-edge-wizard service on the host and publishes it at https://onboard-device.<domain>. If the service is not running, the Onboard Device button gives a connection error. ./health-check.sh checks that it answers. It restarts the service, so it refuses while an onboarding run started from the wizard is in progress; run it again when the run has finished. It prints the roles the wizard admits.
  3. Sign-in. ./deploy-infra.sh creates the Keycloak client edge-wizard and writes EDGE_WIZARD_SECRET into config.env. Without it the wizard cannot sign anyone in.
  4. Units and zones. Create them in the Units tab, with codes that make legal names.
  5. Firmware. Nothing to do. edge/firmware/ may start empty: the run offers MicroPython images for the detected chip and downloads one on request.

The same tasks are in ./guide.sh under Edge devices: flash and register a board, show flash-device.sh's options, and install or update the browser wizard service.

The provisioning records in edge/provisioned/ hold each board's WiFi passphrase, WebREPL password and MQTT password in plain text. The files are readable by their owner only and are not in git. Keep them that way, and include the folder in host backups only if the backup is protected.

Onboarding from the browser

This is the normal way. Onboarding always happens over USB on the platform host: the cable proves the board is in your hands, and the sign-in says who you are.

  1. Plug the board into a USB port of the machine that runs the platform.
  2. Open the Configuration UI at https://config.<domain>, go to the Devices tab and press Onboard Device. It opens https://onboard-device.<domain> in a new tab.
  3. Sign in with your platform account.
  4. The page "Onboard a device" shows one tile per device type. Pick ESP32 / ESP32-S3. Tiles marked planned or unavailable cannot be started.
  5. Answer each question as the page shows it. The page also shows the live log and the current step. The questions are described in The steps of a run.
  6. At the end, read the summary card: IP address, hostname, unit and zone, role, broker, topics, and whether the device record was written. Copy the WebREPL password if you need it.
  7. Return to the Devices tab. The new board appears under its unit and zone.

The service runs one onboarding at a time. A second start while a run is in progress is refused with "a device is already being provisioned".

Do not use the Add Record button to add a board. It creates an inventory entry only, with no flash, no slot check and no broker credential.

Onboarding from a terminal

The same run works in a terminal on the platform host, for example over SSH:

./edge/flash-device.sh

Before it reads the free slots, the script shows a sign-in box:

This run reads and writes the platform database as you.
On any device with a browser open:  https://keycloak.<domain>/realms/medcea/device
and enter the code   XXXX-XXXX

Open that address on any phone or computer, sign in, and enter the code. The code expires after about ten minutes. Once you are signed in, the script prints "signed in as <name> (<user>, <role>)" and every database call in the run acts as you. Press Ctrl+C while it waits to stop without writing anything. The device code appears only in the terminal and is never shown on a web page.

Only an account holding engineer or maintenance may onboard. Any other account is refused right after the sign-in with a message naming those roles, and nothing is touched.

Useful options (./edge/flash-device.sh --help lists all of them):

OptionEffect
--role SENSE or --role CONTROLSkip the role question.
--unit CODE, --zone CODELimit the slot menu to one unit or zone. Type the code exactly as the Units tab shows it; case matters. Only free slots are still offered.
--advancedAlso ask the parameters the template marks as advanced, such as I2C frequency and UART pins.
--port PATHUse this serial port instead of autodetecting.
--offlineUse only firmware already in edge/firmware/.
--no-eraseSkip the full erase. Only for a board you have already erased once.
--uiAlso serve this run as a web page and print its address. Use the printed link, which carries the run's token. It needs port 8088 free, so it cannot run while the wizard service is up; use the wizard service instead.
--no-registerFlash and configure the board for the slot, but do not write the device record. The slot stays free.
--unregisteredBench mode. See Bench mode.

With -y the script does not ask before erasing. It also refuses to move a board that is registered in another slot.

The steps of a run

The questions that only the person at the chamber can answer (role, slot, wiring) come first, before the board is touched, so a board is not left half onboarded because someone walked away. Two questions come later: the go-ahead to erase, and the WiFi network, which the board can only scan once it runs MicroPython. The browser page and the terminal ask the same questions in the same order.

  1. Sign in. In the browser you are already signed in. In a terminal, enter the device code. A run that is not registering a board (--unregistered) skips this.
  2. Role. "What is this board for?" Pick SENSE or CONTROL.
  3. Slot. "Which unit and zone is this board going into?" Only free slots of that role are listed, each with the name it will give the board. If there is exactly one, it is chosen for you. If none is free, the run stops and says to mark the existing device Inactive or add a zone.
  4. What is wired. For a SENSE board, "Which sensor is wired to this board?", one sensor at a time, then each sensor's pins, for example the 1-Wire data pin of a DS18B20. Pick Done when every sensor is listed. For a CONTROL board, the run shows the reference wiring, equipment against GPIO pin, and asks "Use this reference wiring?". Answer no to set the pins yourself or to remove equipment the chamber does not have. See The channel-map editor.
  5. Interval. For a SENSE board, the publish interval in seconds (30 by default). A CONTROL board's command poll interval (100 ms) is asked only with --advanced. The run then prints what it inferred without asking: broker address and port, topics and the board's name.
  6. Serial port. Detected automatically. If the account cannot open the port, the run says so and points at the dialout fix.
  7. Board check. The run reads the chip type and MAC address from the board. Two checks happen here, before anything is erased:
    • If this board is already registered in another slot, the run asks the move question. See Moving a board.
    • If the pin map was checked against a different chip than the one plugged in, the run checks it again against the real chip. If the map cannot drive that chip, the run stops and tells you which board type to pick.
  8. Firmware. The newest local image for this chip is the default. Images from micropython.org are offered for download. Only images that match the detected chip are listed.
  9. Erase and flash. "Erase and flash this board?" Yes wipes the whole chip, then writes MicroPython.
  10. WiFi. The board scans the air with its own radio. Pick a network, or enter a hidden one by name, then type the passphrase. A network missing from the list is usually 5 GHz only: these boards hear only 2.4 GHz.
  11. Files. The run generates a WebREPL password for this board only, writes the board's configuration file, its pin map (CONTROL) and the application, then restarts the board and waits for it to report an IP address.
  12. Registration. If the board came online, the run writes its device record, one component record per sensor (or one for the relay board), and the hash of its broker password. If the board never reported an IP address, it is not registered and the slot stays free.
  13. Record and summary. The run writes edge/provisioned/<name>.json and prints the summary.

If a registration is refused, the board is still flashed and running. Fix the cause and onboard the same slot again. The slot's record is updated in place.

The board in download mode: most boards reset themselves for flashing. If the run cannot talk to the board, hold BOOT, tap RESET, release BOOT, and try again.

The channel-map editor and the board check

When you answer no to the reference wiring in the browser, the page shows the channel map itself: a table of relay channels, every command the scheduler can send, and the pin map file being built as you type.

  • Board selector. It defaults to esp32s3, what this fleet runs. ESP32, S2, S3, C3, C6, H2 and P4 are offered. Changing it checks the map you have against that chip, without rewriting it.
  • Pin reference. Opens the table of every GPIO on the selected chip and what else already uses it.
  • Each row shows its pin's type as you type the number. Pins that cannot work (input-only pins, pins used by the SPI flash) are errors. Strapping, PSRAM, USB-JTAG and console pins are warnings.
  • Missing equipment. Clear the equipment the chamber does not have. Every command that needed it is then left out of the board's table, rather than shipped as a command that does nothing and still reports success.
  • Reset to reference wiring puts the reference layout back.

The page refuses to submit a map with errors. The run checks the map again when it gets it, and a third time against the real chip after probing, before the erase. In a terminal the same step asks for one GPIO per piece of equipment. Answer blank or - for equipment the chamber does not have.

The same editor exists as a standalone page for inspecting or correcting a map away from the platform:

./edge/tools/build-editor.sh        # writes edge/tools/actuator-config-editor.html
python3 edge/src/host/device_template.py pins CONTROL esp32c3   # the pin table in a terminal

Testing a CONTROL board: commissioning

After a CONTROL board is onboarded, an engineer can check its wiring by sending commands to it by hand from the MQTT Explorer. The broker allows that only while the board's unit is flagged for commissioning (test mode) and has no run in progress:

  1. Make sure no run is running or scheduled on the unit; flagging is refused while one is.
  2. In the Configuration UI's Units tab, click Flag for commissioning… on the unit, give the reason (for example "relay test of the new CONTROL board in zone 2") and click Propose.
  3. Ask a qa person to approve it. Within about 15 seconds of the approval, the broker accepts your messages to the unit's control topics. The MQTT Explorer's Your rights note does not change: it lists your role's pattern, medcea/+/+/control/#, for every unit.
  4. Send the test commands to one concrete topic, for example medcea/UNIT01/ZONE01/control/<tag> (never the pattern itself: the page refuses + and #), and watch the board's control_setpoint reports. Clicking the topic in the left-hand list shows your message as the broker delivered it.
  5. Click End commissioning… on the unit when you are done. It applies at once; runs can start on the unit again, and qa reviews it afterwards.

The full procedure, the refusals and who sees what are in commissioning a unit.

Replacing a board

Use this when a board fails and a new board takes its place in the same slot. maintenance and engineer may do it.

  1. In the Devices tab, press Edit on the old board, set Status to Inactive and save. The slot becomes free, and within about 15 seconds the broker refuses the old board.
  2. Plug the new board into the platform host and start onboarding.
  3. Pick the same role and the now free slot. Wire it, flash it and let it register as usual.

The registration updates the slot's existing record in place. The record keeps its id, gets the new board's serial number, becomes Active again, and its components are replaced with the ones just chosen. The board gets the same name as before and a new broker password.

To re-flash the same board in its own slot (a firmware update, new pins), onboard it again without deactivating it. The run sees that the board is already in this slot and updates its record in place. The broker password changes.

Replacement is recorded in the slot history as replaced, with the old and the new serial number and the person who did it. Changing the serial number also deactivates the old board's broker credential.

Moving a board

A board's record follows the board, because its serial number (the MAC address) is unique. To move a board to another slot, start onboarding with the board plugged in and pick the new slot. When the run reads the board's MAC address, it finds the existing record and asks:

this board is already registered as unit01-s-zone01 - UNIT01 / ZONE01 (SENSE).
Move this board from unit01-s-zone01 to UNIT01 / ZONE02?
  • Yes moves the record to the new slot. The board takes the new slot's name, its components are replaced, and the old slot becomes free. If the new slot holds an older inactive record with the same name, that record is renamed <name>-<id> and kept inactive.
  • No stops the run before anything is erased or written.

The question comes after the board is probed and before the erase, because that is the first moment both the board and the target slot are known. A run started with -y never moves a board: it stops and asks you to onboard interactively.

maintenance may move a board only into a slot that already has an inactive record. Moving a board into a slot that never had one is an engineer's change.

A move is recorded in the slot history as moved, with the old and new zone, role and name.

MQTT credentials per board

The broker admits nobody anonymously. Each registered board has its own credential:

  • User name: the board's name, for example unit02-c-zone02.
  • Password: a fresh random password minted at every onboarding, so re-flashing a board changes it. The password goes onto the board and into its provisioning record. The platform database stores only its hash.
  • Access by role. The broker lets a SENSE board publish only under its own slot's sense/ topics. It lets a CONTROL board receive only its own slot's control/ topics and publish only its own control_setpoint/ topics.

A sidecar next to the broker rebuilds the broker's password and access files from the database every 15 seconds. Two things follow from that:

  • Deactivation revokes within about 15 seconds. When a device is marked Inactive, or its record is deleted, its credential drops out at the next rebuild. The broker then refuses its next connection and denies its publishes.
  • The first connections of a new board may be refused. The board boots before it is registered. Until the registration lands and the next rebuild picks it up, which takes up to about 15 seconds, the broker refuses the board. The board restarts after 10 seconds and tries again, so this clears by itself.

A board that is not registered (the run was refused, the board had no IP, or --no-register) has a password that the broker does not know, so it cannot publish. Onboard it again once the cause is fixed.

Boards flashed before broker authentication existed have no credential. Re-onboard them.

Boards and the platform's services connect on the broker's LAN port (31883 on the server; 1883 inside the cluster). With IAM_MQTT_PERSON=true that port accepts no person's credential: the MQTT Explorer's personal credentials work only on the broker's WebSocket listener, behind the Explorer's sign-in (see MQTT Explorer). The shared mqtt-explorer account was removed on 1 November 2026 and is refused on both ports. Watch the board in the MQTT Explorer instead (the onboarding summary says so while IAM_MQTT_PERSON is true).

The WiFi passphrase and the MQTT password sit in plain text on the board's flash. Anyone holding the board can read them. WebREPL also stays on after onboarding: anyone who reaches port 8266 with the board's WebREPL password gets a full Python prompt and the board's files. Treat a lost board as a lost credential and mark it Inactive.

Slot history and what gets recorded

The database keeps an append-only history of where every board has been. A database trigger writes it on every change to a device record, whoever makes the change: onboarding, the Devices tab or a direct database change. Nobody can edit or delete the history, and it outlives a deleted device record.

EventWhen
placedA device record was created.
movedIts zone or role changed.
renamedOnly its name changed, for example after a unit or zone code was renamed and the slot re-onboarded.
replacedA different board (serial number) took over the record.
removedThe record was deleted.

Each event holds the board's serial number, the zone, role and name before and after, the time, and who made the change: their account, person id, name, platform role and source. A re-flash in place with no change of slot, name or board adds no event.

For each onboarding the platform records: the device record and its components, stamped with the person whose sign-in the run used; the slot history event; the board's credential hash; and on the host, edge/provisioned/<name>.json with the MAC address, chip, firmware file and its SHA-256, MicroPython version, tool versions, the IP at onboarding, the host login that flashed it and the Keycloak account that registered it. Every stored measurement is stamped with the zone its board was in when the reading arrived.

maintenance and engineer may read the slot history. auditor and qa may read it and export it from Database Management.

Adding a sensor type

The wizard's questions are data, not code. The SENSE questions come from edge/templates/esp32-sense.json and the CONTROL questions from edge/templates/esp32-control.json. Each sensor entry gives its name, the tags it publishes, and the parameters to ask, with the reference build's wiring as defaults. Neither flash-device.sh nor the wizard page changes when an entry is added.

A sensor also needs code on the board to read it: a reading function in the board application (edge/src/app/cea_sensors.py). If that function already exists, the template entry is all that is needed. A new kind of sensor needs a new reading function, which is a developer's change made through the normal review.

New device types in the onboarding page work the same way. Each tile is an entry in edge/catalog.json. An entry whose script is missing or not executable is shown as unavailable.

Bench mode

./edge/flash-device.sh --unregistered

This brings a board up on WiFi with no platform identity, for a bench with no platform deployed. It asks no slot, ships no application, writes nothing to the database and needs no sign-in. The board is named cea-un-<mac6> and has no MQTT credential, so it cannot connect to the broker. It joins WiFi and keeps WebREPL open, so the warning in MQTT credentials per board applies to it too. To give it a slot later, onboard it normally. Nothing has to be undone.

A registered run also offers to continue unregistered when the platform database does not answer or no sign-in is possible. If you accept, the board ends up the same as with --unregistered.

--no-register is different: it flashes the board for a slot with its application and pins, but does not write the device record. The slot still reads as free, and the broker refuses the board until it is onboarded again with registration.

When onboarding is refused

The wizard explains a refusal from the platform database in plain words:

The wizard saysWhat to do
this role may not onboard boards (permission denied)Ask an administrator for the engineer or maintenance role.
no platform role, or two own rolesAsk an administrator to fix the account in the User Manager.
anonymous request refused, or the sign-in token was refusedSign in again and re-run.
the token expired during the requestRe-run. The slot's record is updated in place.
config-api has no JWT keyAdministrator: run ./deploy-infra.sh, then ./apps/frontend/deploy.sh.
the code expired before the sign-in was finishedRe-run for a new code. If Keycloak said the account is disabled, the access request is still pending or the account was deactivated.
no free SENSE (or CONTROL) slotMark the existing device Inactive, or add a zone in the Units tab.
not offered: ... is not a legal hostnameRename the unit or zone code in the Units tab.

The run checks your sign-in again when it checks the board, just before the erase and just before the registration. If your session has ended (you signed out, or your account was deactivated), the run pauses and asks the same person to sign in again: the browser page sends you to the sign-in page, and a terminal run shows a new code. The run waits up to 15 minutes. If nobody signs in by then, a run that has not yet erased the board stops with "Nothing was erased".

If you no longer hold engineer or maintenance (the roles the access policy admits to onboarding), the run stops before the erase. After the erase it skips the registration instead, and the board stays unregistered.

Access control and records

This chapter is for quality staff (qa, qc, qp), auditors and thesis examiners. It describes the one access policy that says who may do what, the layers that enforce it, the decisions that need a second person, and the records the platform keeps of every change. It ends with the known gaps and the decision record. It describes the platform as deployed on 31 October 2026, including the Approvals tab and unit commissioning (branch feat/approve-commission). The current value of each switch is shown by ./health-check.sh and in each access review.

The access policy: one source

Every access right on the platform comes from one policy. Its factory file is apps/iam/policy/defaults.yaml; since IAM 4.2a the policy in force is the database's applied version (version 1 is that factory file), which every deploy reads through state/policy-applied.yaml (see The applied access policy). A compiler reads it and produces the database grants, the sign-in gate of each web host, the route table every service checks and the network policies. Grafana, the AI workbench, the device onboarding wizard and pgAdmin sign people in themselves; their own sign-in settings are compiled from it too. The compiler refuses to compile a policy that breaks one of its invariants.

The policy is a matrix. Rows are resource groups: a set of tables, API routes, web pages and MQTT topics that belong together, such as "Recipes and recipe-to-unit assignment" or "Telemetry". Columns are the eleven platform roles. A cell lists the access levels a role holds on a group. A role not listed in a row holds nothing there. Every account has exactly one platform role.

Roles

RoleName in the UIWhat the role is for
operatorCultivation OperatorRuns the cultivation day to day in the units assigned to them.
production_managerProduction Manager (Process Owner)Prepares and starts runs, assigns approved recipes and parameter sets to units. Holds everything an operator holds, in every unit.
maintenanceMaintenance and CalibrationReplaces and calibrates boards and sensors in existing slots.
qcQuality Control AnalystTests product and records lab results. Offered, but tagged "not yet in use".
qaQuality AssuranceIndependent approver of configuration, access decisions and policy changes. Also holds everything auditor holds.
qpQualified / Responsible PersonReleases the output of a completed run; creates and changes nothing. Offered, but tagged "not yet in use".
engineerAutomation EngineerBuilds master data, devices, recipes, controllers, flows and dashboards as drafts that qa approves.
bio_scientistBio-process ScientistReads recipes, controllers and telemetry (no export); works in the AI workbench.
control_scientistControl and AI ScientistReads controllers, parameter sets and telemetry (no export); develops algorithms in the AI workbench.
adminSystem AdministratorKeeps the platform running and administers accounts and the policy, without access to regulated or history content.
auditorAuditor / InspectorReads and exports records, configuration and audit trails; changes nothing.

Access levels

One scale of letters is used for every kind of resource. Some levels include others ("implies"), and a cell always lists every level it holds, so a reader sees exactly what is granted.

LevelNameIncludesMeaning
NNoneNo access. The default for every cell.
RReadSee the resource, change nothing.
XRead and exportRRead, and take a copy out of the platform through Database Management. Every export is logged.
CCreateRAdd new records; never change existing ones. The level used for appending to regulated data and audit trails.
UUpdateC, RCreate and change records within the group's constraint. Removing a configuration record is an update to "retired", never a delete. Never held by a person on regulated or history data.
AApproveRApprove or release another person's work. In this version an approval is an attributable, time-stamped record, not a 21 CFR Part 11 electronic signature.
OOperateRStart, stop or trigger a run; no change to its definition.
KConfigureAdminister a tool or accounts. Held only by admin. On accounts it means reading and changing account records, which are personal data.
DDeleteRemove platform components on the host. Never data. Held only by admin, on platform administration.

Data classes

Each resource group has one class, and the invariants are written against the classes.

  • Regulated data: records produced while making product, such as runs, telemetry, persisted controller state and run logs. Created once and never changed or deleted by anyone; corrections are new records.
  • Audit history: append-only trails of who did what, including approvals and export logs. Written only by database functions and triggers.
  • Personal data: accounts, names, e-mail addresses, unit assignments and access decisions.
  • Configuration: master data, recipes, controllers, schedules, settings, dashboards and tools, changed under the group's approval rule.

Constraints

A constraint narrows a level in a cell. A person passes when any of their roles holds the level without a constraint, or with a constraint that holds.

ConstraintWhere it appliesRule
own_unitoperator reading telemetry, runs and run logsOnly the units assigned to the person, including every zone of those units and zones added later.
assigned_runoperator executing or stopping runsOnly a run another person created while holding production_manager (or a schedule such a person created), in one of the operator's units.
assign_onlyproduction_manager on recipesAssign approved recipes and approved parameter sets to units; never edit them.
draft_onlyengineer on recipes and controllersEdit drafts only. Once approved, a definition is frozen and a change starts a new draft.
existing_slotmaintenance on devices and onboardingRe-onboard a board into a slot that already exists; creating a new slot is refused.
commissioning_unitengineer publishing on command topicsCommand only a unit flagged for commissioning and with no active run.
not_own_authorevery Approve (A) cellApprove only another person's work. Compares persons, not accounts.
qa_dual_controladmin on accounts and unit assignmentsGrants wait for a qa person; removing access acts at once.

Invariants

Invariants are rules the policy itself must satisfy. The matrix rules are checked on every compile and by python3 apps/iam/compiler/cli.py check. The workflow rules are checked when a change is approved: I17 by the User Manager, I8 by the planned Policy UI. The database rules (I9, I10, I15) are checked by the database deploy, which rolls back if the live grants differ from the policy.

CodeRule
I1No role holds U or D on regulated or history data.
I2admin holds nothing but K on regulated, history or personal data, including through tools.
I3auditor holds only R or X.
I4On a group with an approval rule, no role holds both (C, U or O) and A.
I5The two scientist roles hold nothing on runs, schedules, MQTT topics and controller state, and at most Read on telemetry (never export or write; Read by decision, IAM 3.2b).
I6Production roles hold nothing on the AI workbench; engineer at most R.
I7qp holds no C, U or O anywhere, and holds A on runs.
I8A policy version is applied only after approval by someone other than the submitter, whose own role it does not widen.
I9Nothing in the platform's schemas is reachable without a verified identity.
I10Each service's grants stay within its ceiling; no service approves on its own or rewrites regulated or history tables unless a waiver covers it.
I11A tool cannot widen a role's access to the data it reaches, unless a waiver covers it.
I12At least one role other than admin holds A on the policy and on accounts, and at least one of those roles makes no changes there and is in use; admin never does.
I13Only admin holds K or D; D only on platform administration.
I14, I16Every cell compiles to something real, and cells list every level they imply.
I15The privileges in the database equal what the policy compiles to, and every table belongs to a group.
I17No person holds an admin account and a qa account at the same time.

Two waivers are accepted and stated in the policy with their compensating controls and a review date of 2027-10-27. W1: the Node-RED editor runs with the ingest account, which must insert telemetry. Its controls: the flows are deployed from the repository, the health check compares the running flows with the repository, the editor admits only the roles the policy gives Node-RED flows (at the sign-in and inside the editor), and every deploy in the editor is logged with the person's name. W2: a learning controller updates its Q-table during a run; every update is also appended as an event with the run id.

The Access Policy shows the applied policy and its history, and is where a qa person confirms a bootstrap or rollback version (slice 4.3). Since slice 4.4 an admin drafts a change there and a qa person who is none of its authors approves it, and the host operator proposes a git skeleton change for the same approval (Changing the access policy). Applying an approved version is slice 4.5 and not built: nothing approved changes anyone's access yet. Version 1 exists since slice 4.2a, and every deploy refuses a git change that gives access (the skeleton gate), break-glass included, whether or not a version proposes it, so until approvals can be applied no change that widens access can deploy; a narrowing change in git is accepted at the next deploy. python3 apps/iam/compiler/cli.py check runs the invariants.

How the policy is enforced, in layers

No single check is trusted alone. A request passes five layers, each compiled from the same policy; a message to the broker passes its own, listed last.

  1. Sign-in gate per host. Every web UI signs in through Keycloak. Each gated host admits only the roles that hold some level on a group served there. A signed-in person without such a role sees a "No access" page naming the roles that may open it. For example the policy admits auditor, qa and qp to Database Management, and admin, qa, qp and auditor to the User Manager. python3 apps/iam/compiler/cli.py hosts lists who each host admits. Grafana signs people in itself; its own role setting is compiled from the same policy: engineer is Editor, the other roles holding Read on dashboards are Viewer, and every other role is refused. Grafana Admin is never given through the sign-in. Node-RED's editor signs people in a second time and gives the rights compiled from the policy: engineer may change and deploy flows but not install palette modules, and no other role has any right in it. The AI workbench (JupyterHub) likewise admits exactly the roles the policy admits to its host (admin, bio_scientist, control_scientist, engineer, qa); admin, the only role holding Configure there, is hub administrator without the right to open anyone's workspace (through the hub role workbench-admin; until the fix of 31 October 2026 the platform did not meet this, see the known gaps), and removing a person from the hub keeps their home folder. The device onboarding wizard admits only engineer and maintenance, the roles that may register boards, and checks again at every renewal of the sign-in during a run, so a board is not erased for someone who lost the role. pgAdmin admits only admin, checked at sign-in.
  2. Verified token in every service. The six Python services (scheduler, Monitor, Database Management, the Config UI's platform API, the User Manager and, since IAM 3.5, iam-api, which issues the MQTT Explorer's credentials) check every request against the compiled route table. They trust only a signed access token verified against Keycloak's public keys, never identity headers that anything inside the cluster could send. Each route belongs to one group and needs one level; a route missing from the table is refused.
  3. Network policies. Each of the eight application services (scheduler, Monitor, Database Management, the Config UI, its database API, the User Manager, Node-RED and iam-api, which only the MQTT Explorer's sign-in proxy reaches) accepts connections only from its listed callers, so another program in the cluster or a forged request cannot reach it at all. Since IAM 3.2b the database API (config-api) also accepts the AI workbench's notebooks, which reach nothing else of the platform but Keycloak, and only while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES are true: each request carries the person's own sign-in, and the database applies that person's role and row rules. Both sides of that opening are compiled from the access policy. The portal, MQTT Explorer's page, the message broker, Grafana and the workbench's own hub are not covered.
  4. Database roles and row-level security. While IAM_DB_ROLES is true, a person's requests through the Config UI's database API run as that person's own database role. Each service logs in as its own role. Row-level security (a filter the database applies to every query) limits an operator to the telemetry and runs of their units. Triggers refuse edits to approved definitions and to append-only records. This layer holds even if an application has a bug.
  5. Approvals in the database. Approval functions check the approver's role and that the approver is not an author, using the person from the verified token. A service cannot approve on its own.
  6. Message broker. Every broker client authenticates. Boards and the platform's services use the LAN port; with IAM_MQTT_PERSON=true people use only their own short-lived credential from the MQTT Explorer, accepted only on the WebSocket listener, carrying the topics their role's cells give. A send right exists only for a unit flagged for commissioning without a running run, and the broker's credential sync re-derives it every 15 seconds from the database.

Some constraints are checked only in one layer. assigned_run is checked only by the database's run functions, and own_unit by row-level security and by the Monitor and scheduler reads themselves.

Report-only and enforce

The services can run in two modes, set by IAM_ENFORCE in config.env.

  • Report-only (IAM_ENFORCE=false, the state in which IAM 2.6 was deployed): a request the route table refuses is let through, and the service writes medcea_iam: would refuse … to its log. In the same way the scheduler logs run_logs: would withhold when it shows a run's log to a role that will not see it under enforcement.
  • Enforce (IAM_ENFORCE=true): the service answers 401 (no token or a bad one), 403 (route or role not allowed) or 503 (the service cannot check, for example the route table or keys are missing).

The services enforce since 31 October 2026, 09:54 UTC (IAM_ENFORCE=true; the policy history holds the enforcement_enabled event). Two places always enforce, whatever IAM_ENFORCE says: Database Management (exports) and the User Manager's administration port. The database layer has its own switch, IAM_DB_ROLES, which has been true since 30 October 2026, and the operator unit filter in the Monitor and scheduler applies whenever either switch is true. Only while both are false do those two services log own_unit: would limit … instead of filtering.

The administrator turns enforcement on with ./guide.sh: area "IAM switches (enable / roll back)", the IAM_ENFORCE action, then enable. The guide first lists its preconditions as a checklist the administrator confirms: IAM_DB_ROLES is true, the report-only trial is over, and every operator has a unit in effect. It then takes the deploy lock, runs python3 tests/iam-routes-diff.py --strict, sets the switch in config.env and runs ./deploy-infra.sh, stopping at the first failure. Services read the new value within about a minute without a restart. Rollback sets it back to false and runs ./deploy-infra.sh again.
