Every transition of IAM_ENFORCE or IAM_DB_ROLES appends an enforcement_enabled or enforcement_disabled event, naming the switch, to the tamper-evident policy history. ./health-check.sh (check iamenforce) fails when the value the services read differs from config.env, when IAM_ENFORCE is true while IAM_DB_ROLES is false, or when a service's own fallback copy is still false under true. It warns while IAM_ENFORCE is false.

Dual control: the maker is never the checker

Every approval needs two different persons. The platform compares the person_id carried in each verified token, not account names, so one person with two accounts is still one person. person_id is set to the account's own id when it is first enabled; changing it is itself a decision that waits for qa.

An approval function refuses when the approver is the author, submitter, creator or executor of the record, or, for an account decision, its subject; and when the change would widen the approver's own role. For account decisions the accounts are compared as well.

Rule I17 keeps the administrator and the independent approver apart: no person may hold an admin and a qa account at the same time. The User Manager checks this against Keycloak when a role or person_id change is proposed and again when it is confirmed.

Account decisions

Every change an administrator makes to an account in the User Manager is recorded as an account decision. What happens next depends on whether the change gives access or takes it away.

ChangeEffect
Grant (an approved access request)Waits for qa. The request reads "approved", but the account stays disabled with no role until confirmed.
Activation of an accountWaits for qa, because an enabled account gets back everything its role gives (and, while IAM_GATES is false, the AI workbench even without a role).
Role change that adds any (group, level) the old role did not haveWaits for qa.
New unit assignmentWaits for qa (see Unit assignments).
person_id changeAlways waits for qa.
E-mail settings changeWaits for qa.
DeactivationActs at once, signs the person out and revokes their MQTT credentials; reviewed by qa afterwards.
Role change that only narrowsActs at once, revokes MQTT credentials; reviewed afterwards. A widening role change revokes them when qa confirms it.
Removal of a unitActs at once; reviewed afterwards.

An account has at most one open decision at a time. A deactivation is the exception: it withdraws the account's waiting decisions. An administrator may withdraw a waiting decision in the User Manager, except a unit assignment, which is withdrawn with units.sh remove. The last enabled admin and the last enabled qa cannot be removed.

Confirming, refusing and reviewing

  1. Sign in to the User Manager as a qa person and open the Decisions tab.
  2. "Waiting for qa" lists the decisions to confirm or refuse; "To review" lists the changes that already acted; "All" lists every decision.
  3. Open a decision and read what it will do. For a waiting decision choose Confirm or Refuse; a note is optional to confirm and required to refuse. For an applied one choose Mark reviewed. Confirming applies the change in Keycloak in the same step. Refusing a grant deletes the requested account, so the person can apply again. If you cannot check a decision, for example because you made it or it concerns your own account, the page says why.

A red banner at the top of the Decisions tab ("Keycloak differs from the decisions") lists any account whose live role or state is not explained by a recorded decision, for example a role granted directly in the Keycloak console.

Each decision row keeps the maker, the subject, the change, the reason, and the checker with time and note. Once checked the row is final: a database trigger lets only the check columns be filled, once. Confirmations, refusals, reviews and withdrawals are also appended to the hash-chained policy history. Status and role changes are kept in the append-only account status history.

./health-check.sh (check iamdecisions) warns when a decision has waited more than 7 days, or when an e-mail settings change is pending.

Unit assignments

Operators are assigned units, not zones. An operator with no unit in effect sees no telemetry and no runs and can start none. Until the planned Policy UI (Phase 4) brings this into the web interface, units are assigned on the platform host with apps/iam/units.sh, which takes the deploy lock.

./apps/iam/units.sh list
./apps/iam/units.sh assign <username> UNIT01 --by <your username>
./apps/iam/units.sh remove <username> UNIT01 --by <your username>

--by names the person running the command, who is recorded as the maker. An assignment is only proposed: nothing takes effect until a qa person who is neither the maker nor the subject confirms it in the Decisions tab. A refusal applies nothing. A removal acts at once. list shows the units in effect and those still waiting for qa.

The script cannot prove who ran it, since whoever runs it controls the host. So every assignment made by units.sh can be confirmed only after the qa person ticks the box "I did not run units.sh for this assignment." The attestation is stored with the confirmation note.

--bootstrap applies an assignment at once, before any check, and is allowed only while no other enabled qa account could confirm it. The decision still waits for qa; the Decisions tab marks it "in effect before the check", and a refusal removes the unit.

Every unit that takes effect or is removed appends unit_assigned or unit_removed to the hash-chained policy history, with the qa person as actor for a confirmed assignment.

The quarterly access review

The Access review tab of the User Manager holds one review per quarter. A qa person creates it and signs it off; auditor and qp can read it.

  1. Open Access review and choose "Create this quarter's review". The snapshot is taken at that moment and never changes.
  2. Read the snapshot: every account with its role, state, person_id and last login; the units; anything unexplained at the time of the review; decisions waiting for qa; decisions since the last review; the waivers; the IAM switches; and the policy version and hash-chain verdict.
  3. Add an optional note and choose "Sign off" followed by the quarter. Signing off cannot be undone.

Signing off a review that lists unexplained roles accepts them: the drift banner in the Decisions tab stops showing them until a later change to that account.

Approvals of configuration

Groups with an approval rule are either blocking (nothing is used until approved) or review (qa confirms after the fact).

WhatRuleWho approves
RecipesBlocking. Status draft, approved or retired. While IAM_DB_ROLES is true, the database refuses to assign a recipe that is not approved (in the Configuration UI's Recipe ↔ Unit Permissions) or to run one, and an approved recipe cannot be edited; while it is false these are warnings.qa
Control algorithms and parameter setsBlocking. A run uses only approved algorithms and approved parameter sets.qa
Scheduler settings (log retention)Blocking. A new value waits as a pending change and applies only after a qa person other than its author confirms it. While one waits, only its proposer may replace it; anyone else's new proposal for that setting is refused until qa approves or returns it or its proposer withdraws it. The database also enforces minimums (compress after at least 1 day).qa
Unit commissioning flag (test mode)Blocking to turn on: an engineer proposes it with a reason in the Units tab, and it takes effect only after a qa person other than the proposer approves it, because it opens MQTT command rights to the unit. It is refused while the unit has a run running or scheduled, at the proposal and again at the approval, and no run can be scheduled or start on a flagged unit. Turning it off is a narrowing: it applies at once and qa reviews it afterwards. qa may return a waiting proposal with a reason, and its proposer may withdraw it.qa
Master data and devicesReview. Each change may be confirmed after the fact; an unconfirmed one blocks nothing. These reviews are not listed in the Approvals tab (a known gap).qa
Persisted controller state (Q-tables)Blocking. Retiring is an approved, recorded event; the next run starts cold.qa
Run releaseRelease of a completed run, in the Configuration UI's Approvals tab, by a person who neither created, changed nor executed it (any attempt), with an optional note. Refused while a change to the run has no attributed person, when no recorded person created or started it (a run from before attribution), and for a run that is not completed or already released. There is no hold or refusal: a run that is not released stays unreleased. A released run cannot be set back for a retry.qp

Editing an approved recipe, algorithm or parameter set is refused while IAM_DB_ROLES is true; a change starts a new draft. An approval is also refused while the record carries a change with no attributed person since its last approval.

To retire a Q-table, a qa person opens the Control Models tab of the Config UI, finds the unit in the Production Units table, chooses Retire… next to the algorithm under Learned State and gives a reason, which is recorded. Nothing is deleted.

Each approval writes a row to medcea.approval with the record, a hash of its content, the persons who authored it, and the approver's account, person, name, role and source. Returning a draft or a proposal (action return, with the reason), withdrawing a proposal (action withdraw) and reviewing a change that applied at once (action review) are rows there too; no approval rule counts a return or a withdrawal as an approval. A commissioning proposal is a row in medcea.pending_change (proposer account, person, name, role, source, reason, time, and when it applied at once); the flag change itself is in medcea.row_change, on the unit's updated_by_* columns and in the tamper-evident policy history. A retired Q-table keeps its full state, and each of its channels gains an approval row with action retire and the reason. A release is a row in medcea.run_release (one per run) and an approval row with action release, both with the note.

Attribution: who did what

The 14 attributed tables (master data, devices, recipes, controllers, runs, schedules and others) carry created_by and updated_by fields: account, person, name, role and source. A database trigger fills them from the verified actor of the transaction and overwrites whatever a client sends. A service acting for a person records that person, with source person via followed by the service's database login; a schedule firing acts as system:scheduler on behalf of the person who created the schedule.

Every change to those tables is also appended to medcea.row_change. The approval functions read it to decide who counts as an author.

The tamper-evident policy history

iam_policy.history is a hash chain: each event stores the hash of the previous one, so a changed or removed event breaks every hash after it. Triggers refuse UPDATE, DELETE and TRUNCATE on it for every role, the owner included. It records:

  • enforcement_enabled and enforcement_disabled, with the switch (IAM_DB_ROLES, IAM_ENFORCE or IAM_MQTT_PERSON);
  • mqtt_credential_issued: every MQTT Explorer credential, with its user name, the person, their role, the topics and the expiry (never the password);
  • unit_assigned and unit_removed;
  • account_decision_confirmed, _refused, _reviewed and _withdrawn;
  • mqtt_credentials_retired: MQTT Explorer credentials ended by ./deploy-infra.sh because the MQTT policy or the IAM_MQTT_PERSON switch changed, with the number and the reason;
  • commissioning_enabled and commissioning_disabled: a unit's commissioning flag turned on (by the qa person who approved it) or off (by the engineer, at once), with the unit, the proposal, the proposer and the reason;
  • break_glass_seen: ./deploy-infra.sh found Node-RED running without its own sign-in (NR_ADMIN_AUTH=off) and turned it on again, with the time Node-RED started that way;
  • since IAM 4.2a, the access policy's own versions: skeleton_accepted (with the skeleton it replaced) and skeleton_synced, draft_created, status_changed, apply_started and bootstrap_applied (version 1, with the person who claimed it, the git commit and the digest of every compiled output);
  • account_seeded: an account ./deploy-infra.sh created or gave a person_id, and, once, the qa and engineer accounts of the install as they were found (observed); username, account id, role and person_id only;
  • break_glass_seen with source: file and break_glass_ended: a deploy compiled a policy file of its own (see policy break-glass), with the file's SHA-256 and the reason, and its end per component;
  • older zone_assigned and zone_removed events from before units replaced zones.

./health-check.sh runs iam_policy.verify_chain() (check iamchain) and reports "hash chain intact, N event(s)" or the first broken event.

Known limit: the chain catches any edit that does not rebuild it. It does not catch a database superuser who disables the triggers, drops the newest events or re-hashes the chain from an edited event onward. That needs the head of the chain anchored outside the database, which is not built.

Exports and the export log

Database Management lets a person holding X take a copy of tables and views of schema medcea, covering exactly the groups where their role holds X. An object outside those groups refuses the whole export and nothing is logged. A file saved on the platform goes to apps/database/exports/ and never overwrites an existing file.

Each export writes one row to iam_policy.export_log naming who exported which groups and objects (with row counts) and when, before the file is produced. The file header carries the export number; the page shows the export number and the start of the file's SHA-256, and the service log records the full hash. The export log is append-only for every role.

What is never deleted

  • Audit histories: the policy history, export log, approvals, row changes, account status history, access request history and the User Manager's change log are append-only: database triggers refuse changing or deleting their rows for every role, the owner included.
  • Device placement history: every placement, move and removal of a board.
  • Run logs: kept for good. The scheduler's own job sys_log_retention (daily at 03:15 UTC) compresses the log of a finished run after log_compress_days. It never deletes one, and no person can delete or compress logs. log_delete_days remains a setting but nothing reads it.
  • Q-tables: retired, never deleted.
  • Configuration: recipes, schedules and similar records are retired, not deleted. No person holds a DELETE grant on data.

While IAM_ENFORCE is true, a run's log is shown only to roles that hold Read on run logs; maintenance, qc and qp see the run without its log.

Accounts on this install are test accounts

This install is a research prototype run by one person. Every platform account on it is a test account used by that person to exercise a role; none belongs to cultivation, quality or audit staff. Read every record that names these accounts with that in mind: a maker and a checker shown as different accounts can be the same human.

The table below and the evidence this chapter cites (decision, approval and pending-change numbers, health check results, hub logs) are those of the install that ran until 1 November 2026. A reinstall starts with an empty database and a new Keycloak realm: its accounts are the administrator, qa and engineer named at its setup (ADMIN_USER, QA_USER, ENGINEER_USER), its IAM switches start with only IAM_GATES and IAM_NETPOL on, and its records are numbered from 1 again, so a number cited here may name an unrelated record there. The earlier records survive only in a backup folder copied off the host before the reinstall (see Backup and restore); if none was copied, they are gone. The revision history records the date of any reinstall; update this table after the new install has run Bring IAM to full enforcement.

AccountRoleStateNote
adminadminenabledThe platform administrator account made by the install.
svieiraqaenabledThe researcher's own account; confirms decisions and signs off the access review.
engengineerenabledTest account for the engineer role.
viewerauditorenabledDemo account made by older installs; kept for testing.
sergio_qaqadisabledTest account of the same person as svieira. It confirmed one unit assignment (decision 3, UNIT02) that therefore was not independent; recorded, not undone.
developeroperatordisabledDemo account made by older installs. Deactivated on 31 October 2026 as a test of the retire path: its units were removed with units.sh, then the account was deactivated in the User Manager (decision 12).

New installs no longer create the developer and viewer demo accounts (IAM 3.1). Before real staff use the platform, give each person their own account through Request access, and deactivate the test accounts in the User Manager.

Known gaps and single-person controls

  • Zone moves. Telemetry is filtered by the zone's current unit. Moving a zone to another unit, which engineer may do, gives that unit's operators the zone's whole history without an account decision. It is recorded only in medcea.row_change. Deferred.
  • Open AI workbench sessions. The workbench checks a person's role at each sign-in, not continuously. After a role removal or a deactivation, a workbench session and an open notebook keep working until the sign-in runs again, at the latest after 5 minutes (an open notebook tab after about 10 minutes; until 1 November 2026, an hour). Personal API tokens a person created on the hub keep working until the administrator removes that person from the hub (which keeps their files). Notebooks run code; of the platform's services they reach only Keycloak and the database API, with the person's own sign-in, for reads only, which follows a role change or a deactivation within five minutes (IAM 3.2b).
  • Fixed 31 October 2026: the workbench administrator could open workspaces. Until then a hub administrator held JupyterHub's built-in administrator role, which includes creating a token in any person's name and opening their workspace. The setting the platform relied on to prevent it (admin_access: false) has done nothing since JupyterHub 2.0, and the health check trusted it. The live test tests/iam-workbench.sh found it on 31 October 2026 (step W4: the hub created a token for the test scientist, and the scientist's workspace accepted it; step W3 passed only because the test did not press "Authorize" on the page the hub showed). The fix gives administrators the narrower hub role workbench-admin and nobody the built-in role, and the test now requires the hub to refuse (403) both the token and the authorize step. The database showed no token created by an administrator for another person; deploying the fix removes the built-in role from the accounts that held it (admin and two earlier test administrators).
  • Node-RED editor tokens in the sign-in proxy's log. Node-RED's sign-in returns the editor token in the address it redirects to, and Node-RED's sign-in proxy logs each request's full address, so every editor sign-in since the Phase 3 deploy left its token in that proxy's log (found 31 October 2026). Someone who can read that log and also passes the engineer gate could use another engineer's editor session until it expires, and Node-RED's audit log would name the other engineer. The 8 hours the platform had set were never in effect (Node-RED ignored the setting where it stood), so a session lasted Node-RED's default of a week; since 1 November 2026 it lasts one hour, and the deploy of that day ended every longer one. Fixed the same day: the proxy no longer logs requests to /, the path that carries the token (exclude_logging_paths), and the logged sessions were ended by clearing Node-RED's session file. Editor sessions survive a Node-RED restart (they are kept on its volume), so ending them takes clearing /data/.sessions.json; see troubleshooting.
  • Telemetry can leave through a notebook. A scientist holds Read on telemetry, not export, but a notebook runs the person's code with internet access (package installs, GitHub). When a notebook is committed to git its outputs are removed automatically (nbstripout, set up in every workspace at each start), and a check behind the filter refuses a commit whose notebooks still hold outputs, also when a repository's own settings turn the filter off or it could not be installed; a person who deliberately skips git's checks (git commit --no-verify) with the filter off is not stopped. Code and text cells, other files in the repository, downloads from the browser and anything a person's code sends elsewhere are not checked. What is read is attributable (each request runs as the person); what leaves afterwards is not recorded. Mitigated by the instructions in the workspace and the manual.
  • Workbench home folders: one nightly copy on the same host (until 1 November 2026, none). They are kept when a workspace stops, when a person is removed from the hub or loses the workbench, and across every workbench redeploy. Since 1 November 2026 the nightly backup copies them (see the next item); before, nothing copied them anywhere. Deleting the namespace or MicroK8s (--uninstall-infra, --uninstall-all, ./deploy-infra.sh --reset or --uninstall, or a yes to "Fully wipe MicroK8s and reinstall?") deletes them; those commands list them, with the newest backup, and say how to keep them before they ask. The research repository stays the copy of record for notebooks.
  • Backups on the same host (1 November 2026). The nightly backup (Backup and restore) writes to BACKUP_DIR on the platform host itself, by default on the same disk as the data. A disk failure, a lost or stolen machine, or anyone with the host's administrator rights can therefore take both the data and its backups; the deploy warns when they share a disk. Until BACKUP_DIR is on another disk, or the folders are copied off the host, it protects against mistakes, a failed upgrade and a reinstall, not against losing the machine. The backups are not encrypted (mode 700 for the deploying account only) and hold Keycloak's password hashes and client secrets and people's git tokens. A restore is a manual procedure and writes nothing into the platform's records. No restore has been tested on production yet (exercise E12).
  • Onboarding into a new slot by maintenance. The onboarding wizard admits maintenance, which may only re-onboard into a slot that already exists, but the wizard does not check this itself. If a maintenance person chooses an empty slot, the board is erased and flashed before the database refuses the registration, and the board is left unregistered. No account on this install holds maintenance today.
  • Onboarding from the host shell without a platform identity. Whoever has a shell on the platform host and serial port access can flash a board without signing in: flash-device.sh --unregistered, declining the sign-in when a terminal run offers to continue unregistered, continuing when the slots cannot be read, and --ui-insecure. None of these registers the board or gives it a credential. They stay as the host operator's break-glass path (see the decision record).
  • Node-RED flows outside the repository. A flow built in the editor is reported by the health check as a warning, not blocked. Code in a flow runs inside Node-RED and can read what Node-RED holds: the ingest database password, the broker account, Node-RED's sign-in secret and the deploy scripts' token. An engineer who means to can therefore get round the rule that palette modules come only from the repository. The audit log and the health check make such use visible; they do not prevent it.
  • Open Grafana sessions. Grafana checks a person's role only at sign-in. A deactivation or a role removal does not end a Grafana session that is already open: the person keeps their Grafana role, and with it every unit's telemetry, until the session ends, 5 minutes after it was last used and at the latest one hour after its sign-in; the next sign-in checks the role again (a deactivated account cannot sign in). Until 1 November 2026 it was one hour whether used or not. A change to who gets which Grafana role (the access policy) reaches open sessions the same way: Grafana keeps its sessions across the restart that change causes, since it keeps its database (decision record, "Grafana keeps its data"); until then that restart ended every session at once.
  • Editors' own Grafana dashboards are not reviewed, and can be restored only whole (1 November 2026). A Grafana Editor (engineer) may create dashboards, or "Save as" copies of the platform's, and Grafana keeps them, with folders, annotations and alert rules, until an Editor deletes them. They are not in the repository, no second person reviews them, and Grafana records only who saved one last, in its own database. Since 1 November 2026 the nightly backup copies that database, but an administrator can put it back only whole, to the state of a night; a reinstall deletes it (after a warning that names it). They read through Grafana's own database account like every dashboard, so they show nothing a Viewer could not query anyway. The platform's own dashboards stay read-only and change only through the repository.
  • Sign out and switching accounts in one browser (1 November 2026). Sign out ends, at once, the sign-in gate of the app it is chosen in, the platform sign-in and the AI Workbench's own session. The other apps keep their own sessions for a short while, so after a sign-out, or when another person signs in on the same browser, an app can still show the previous person:
    • the sign-in gates of the other hosts: up to 4 minutes;
    • the AI Workbench hub: up to 5 minutes; an open JupyterLab tab: about 10 minutes, though never another person's workspace;
    • Grafana: up to 5 minutes after its last use, and up to one hour after its sign-in while a tab keeps it in use (a dashboard with auto-refresh does);
    • the Node-RED editor: up to one hour after it was opened, still acting, and logged, as the previous person (sessions issued before 1 November 2026 lasted a week; Node-RED drops every stored session longer than the hour at each start, so the deploy of that day ended them);
    • pgAdmin: up to 60 minutes without use, at most one day in use. pgAdmin does not follow the platform sign-in at all: it is bound only by those limits, its own Logout and a restart. A shorter session is not possible: pgAdmin's start-up passes the session length in days to its web server as a whole number of seconds, and a fraction of a day stopped pgAdmin from starting (1 November 2026).
    Live connections already open stay connected until their tab is closed: JupyterLab kernels, MQTT Explorer's broker connection (with the person's own credential until it expires or is revoked) and Grafana's live updates. If the AI Workbench does not answer, Sign out stops on its error page after the platform sign-in has ended; the workbench's own session then ends within its 5 minutes and the portal's gate within 4. The portal's own Sign Out buttons still end only the portal's gate session. The manual tells people on a shared computer not to tick Remember me, to sign out and close the browser, and to use pgAdmin's own Logout.
  • Grafana reads every unit. Grafana is gated by role since Phase 3.1, but its dashboards read telemetry of every unit through Grafana's own database account, which is why operator has no Grafana. A Viewer can send Grafana any query that account may run (master data, measurements and runs); hiding Explore from Viewers is not a boundary.
  • Grafana's local admin. One break-glass account holds Grafana Admin, with its own generated password in config.env and the credentials file. Whoever operates the host can read it; its use is logged only by Grafana.
  • Shared MQTT Explorer account (closed 31 October 2026). Until IAM_MQTT_PERSON was turned on, MQTT Explorer connected with one shared account that read and wrote every topic, also on the LAN port, so commands sent from it named no person. Since then each person gets a short-lived credential of their own, accepted only on the WebSocket listener, and the broker refuses the shared account on both ports (tests/iam-mqtt.sh M5). Since 1 November 2026 the shared account no longer exists: a rollback of the switch leaves the Explorer without a credential instead of bringing it back.
  • MQTT Explorer credentials outlive a sign-out. Signing out of the platform does not end a credential; it ends after at most 8 hours. Deactivation, a role change, clearing a unit's commissioning flag and a run start take effect at the broker's next check, within about 15 seconds; a connection already open stays open, but each message is checked. If the broker cannot read the credentials from the database, it stops accepting changes to them for at most 30 seconds and then admits no person credential until it can read them again.
  • The broker's WebSocket listener is reachable inside the cluster. No network policy covers the broker yet, so a program in the cluster that holds a leaked Explorer credential could use it directly, not only through the Explorer's sign-in, until the credential expires. Deferred (see the decision record).
  • Approval and commissioning screens (closed 31 October 2026). Until then qa could approve drafts and pending changes only through the database functions, and nothing in the Configuration UI flagged a unit for commissioning, so an engineer's MQTT Explorer could only read. The Approvals tab and the Units tab's Commissioning column now do both (see commissioning a unit); a walk-through on production the same evening used both (see the decision record).
  • Fixed 31 October 2026: the Configuration UI's deploy failed. The page had grown to 254 KB, and kubectl apply keeps a copy of the whole ConfigMap in an annotation that holds at most 256 KiB, so the frontend deploy stopped with "metadata.annotations: Too long" and the old page kept running. Found at the deploy of the approval screens. The deploy now replaces or creates that ConfigMap instead of applying it, as the scheduler deploy already did (commit 15e8d0b); see troubleshooting.
  • Fixed 31 October 2026: the MQTT Explorer sent wildcard topics. In the walk-through the engineer copied the pattern from Your rights (medcea/+/+/control/#) into the topic box. Sending to a topic with + or # is a protocol error, so the broker closed the connection and the page showed "Reconnecting…"; nothing was delivered. The page now refuses such a topic before sending and names an example, and the rights note says its topics are patterns (commit a294b06).
  • pgAdmin's secrets in plain text (closed 1 November 2026). Until then pgAdmin's sign-in client secret and its first-start password (the platform administrator's) were plain values in the pgAdmin deployment, readable by anyone allowed to read deployments in the cluster. They are now in a Kubernetes Secret, pgAdmin's deployment keeps no older copies (ReplicaSets) that still hold them, and the health check (pgadminroles) fails while the deployment or such a copy still carries them as plain values. On the install of that date the client secret is to be regenerated once in Keycloak after the deploy, since it was readable until then; an install made after that date never had it in plain text and needs no regeneration.
  • Run release screen (closed 31 October 2026). Until then the database could record a release, but no screen called it, so qp could not release a completed run from the platform. The Approvals tab now lists the completed, unreleased runs for qp and releases them (see releasing a completed run); the Scheduler shows a Released badge. No account on this install holds qp yet.
  • No hold, deviation or CAPA record for a run. qp can release a completed run or leave it unreleased; the platform records no hold, refusal or deviation, and nothing says why a run stays unreleased. Deviations and CAPA (Spec RACI 10) are not built. Decided 31 October 2026.
  • Runs completed before attribution cannot be released. A run completed before migration 008b is recorded as created by migration:008, with no person and no change history, so the platform cannot tell who created or executed it. Such a run is listed for qp with the reason "no recorded person created or started this run" and the database refuses to release it, because the rule that the releaser neither created nor executed the run cannot be checked. These runs stay unreleased in the queue, and they are not counted on the tab. They are never released: decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026 (see the decision record).
  • After-the-fact reviews of master data and devices are not in the Approvals tab. qa can review them only through the database function; an unreviewed change blocks nothing.
  • A few minutes of a differing route-table digest (IAM 4.2a). Between the database deploy and ./deploy-infra.sh of slice 4.2a the database and the route table disagree about the route table's digest, for minutes. No refusal by the User Manager follows from it; iamenforce and tests/iam-schema.sh are red until ./deploy-infra.sh has run.
  • The host policy file's digests are not keyed (IAM 4.2a). The account that deploys the platform can rewrite state/policy-applied.yaml with hand-recomputed digests. The health check finds it (it compares the whole body with the database) and the next deploy overwrites it; the onboarding wizard and flash-device.sh compile it as it is in between.
  • A queued account fact is not read again from Keycloak (IAM 4.2a). A fact that waited in state/seed-facts.jsonl is recorded as it was written. It grants nothing (who may approve is read from Keycloak at the time), and it cannot take the place of the genuine fact, whose content hash differs.
  • Chain head not anchored outside the database (see the policy history).
  • Approvals are records, not electronic signatures. Re-authentication and signature manifestation are not built.
  • Host operator. In the prototype the administrator also operates the host, which is break-glass access to the superuser and the Keycloak console. Statements by the database superuser are logged in full.
  • One qa person may create and sign off the same access review. This is a known single-person control.
  • Recorded self-check. The test account sergio_qa belonged to the same person as the account svieira. It was granted qa, confirmed account decision #3 (unit UNIT02) and was then deactivated. That confirmation is recorded as independent although it was not. It is noted here and was not undone.

Who reaches production data, and how

Production data here means telemetry, runs and their logs, and the configuration the units run. Each path below is the only way the listed roles reach that data; a role not listed for a path has no way in through it. The roles are those of the access policy on this install (python3 apps/iam/compiler/cli.py hosts).

PathRolesWhat, and at which levelWhat is recorded
Run Monitoroperator (their units only), production_manager, maintenance, qc, qp, engineer, auditor, qa (which includes auditor's rights); bio_scientist and control_scientist pass its gate but may not read runs, so it shows them nothing usefulRuns and live telemetry, ReadEvery request carries the person's verified sign-in and is checked against the route table; reads are not logged one by one.
Configuration UI and the database APIEvery platform role, each with its own database grants and row rules (operators: their units only)Master data and configuration; writes and approvals for the roles that hold themEach change is stamped with the person and kept in the change history; approvals name the approver.
Grafana dashboardsengineer (Editor); auditor, maintenance, production_manager, qa, qc, qp (Viewer)Telemetry of every unit, Read, through Grafana's own database accountGrafana's own log only; the database sees Grafana's account, not the person (a known gap).
Database Management exportauditor, qa, qp, each only for the record groups their role may export (qp: telemetry, runs and the audit histories; auditor and qa: those and the master data, devices, recipes, schedules and account records)A file of whole tables, ExportEvery export in the export log (who, which tables, row counts, the file's checksum), which nobody can change. The file leaves the platform; what happens to it afterwards is not recorded.
AI workbench notebooksbio_scientist, control_scientist, engineer and qa (telemetry and master data, qa also runs, through auditor's rights); admin may open the workbench but reads nothing regulated; not auditor or operatorRead only, through the database API with the person's own sign-in, and only while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES are both trueEach read runs as the person in the database; nothing is logged per read. Outputs are removed from commits to git; what a person's code sends elsewhere is not recorded (a known gap).
MQTT ExplorerengineerLive messages of every unit as they arrive, not stored data; sending only to a unit flagged for commissioning (an engineer proposes it in the Configuration UI's Units tab, qa approves it)Under IAM_MQTT_PERSON, each personal credential in the policy history; before 31 October 2026, one shared account, removed on 1 November 2026.
Node-RED editorengineerThe ingest flows and the live messages; flow code can read Node-RED's database password (a known gap)Each deploy of flows, with the person's name, in Node-RED's audit log; flows outside the repository are reported by the health check.
pgAdminadminNo production data: the schema and the few configuration and policy tables the administrator's database role may readpgAdmin's own log.
The host shell (break-glass)Whoever operates the hostEverything, as the database superuserEvery superuser statement in the database server's log; see the host operator gap.

Decision record

Decisions taken for IAM 2.6, Phase 3 and the approval and commissioning screens, with the reason for each.

DecisionChoiceWhy
Test accounts on this installKept; documented as test accounts of one person. developer deactivated on 31 October 2026 as a test.The install is a one-person prototype; the accounts exercise the roles. An auditor must know that different account names in the records can be one human.
Approved definitions in the Configuration UI (approvals screen)Frozen in the UI in both modes: an approved or retired recipe, control algorithm or parameter set opens read-only, and is changed through ⎘ Copy to a new draft, which goes through approval again.The database refuses such edits only while IAM_DB_ROLES is true (and demotes to draft with a warning otherwise); the screen keeps one rule in both modes, so nothing approved is changed by accident.
Removing a step from a draft recipe (approvals screen)Remove step… copies the recipe to a new draft without that step and retires the old draft; nothing is deleted.No role may delete recipes or their steps (invariant I1); a retired draft stays in the record.
Retention proposal reminder on the Scheduler page (approvals screen)Kept in the proposer's browser only; the Approvals tab is where the proposal's real state is.The scheduler service holds no read of the approval records; giving it one only for a reminder was not worth widening its grants.
Run log retentionCompress only, never delete.Run logs are part of the regulated run record. Deletion may return later, with a durable record of what was deleted.
File save routes in the Config UI that carry a constraint (devices, recipes)Narrowed to roles holding the level without a constraint: engineer for device files, no role for recipe files.The platform API cannot yet evaluate existing_slot, draft_only or assign_only. Under enforcement the Config UI's "Save to Platform Library" is refused for recipe files, and for device files to every role but engineer; recipes in the database are not affected.
Export scopeDatabase Management exports schema medcea only, for now.The account and policy tables (schemas iam and iam_policy) stay out of exports until decided otherwise, although some belong to groups where auditor holds X.
Roles offered on the request form and in the User ManagerAll eleven; qc and qp tagged "not yet in use".A person can request the role the policy defines for their work; roles not yet used are marked as such rather than hidden.
Role descriptions on the public request formShown as the policy words them.One wording for every reader.
Order of the switchesIAM_ENFORCE requires IAM_DB_ROLES=true.assigned_run is checked only in the database; without it an operator could execute or stop another unit's run.
Q-table counts per unit (GET /api/units)Left visible to every role that reads master data.The counts show how many channels hold learned state, not the state itself; left as is by decision.
Report-only trialPlanned: at least one working day of report-only before the IAM_ENFORCE flip. Done: the administrator turned it on at 09:54 UTC on 31 October 2026, about ten minutes after the 2.6 deploy, once tests/iam-signin.sh (88 sign-ins, 120 API calls) agreed with the policy and the service logs held no refusal of a real person.The planned trial was to see what real use would refuse before it is refused. It was shortened by the administrator's decision: a one-person prototype with no run active, and a rollback that returns the services to report-only within about a minute. Refusals met in use are traced to the route table as they come.
Export filesFiles in apps/database/exports/ are ignored by git.Exports hold data and must not enter the repository.
Q-table retirement during a rollbackWhile IAM_DB_ROLES=false a Q-table cannot be retired from the UI.Retiring needs a qa person's verified sign-in at the database, which exists only while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES are both true. During such a rollback nobody can retire a Q-table.
Grafana Admin (IAM 3.1)Only Grafana's local break-glass account holds it, with its own generated password (GRAFANA_BREAKGLASS_PASS), shown in the credentials file and used by the report deploy. Nobody gets Grafana Admin through the platform sign-in.Before, the local admin's password was the platform administrator's own, so the admin person could reach Grafana Admin with their everyday password. A separate password keeps that a deliberate, logged break-glass step.
Grafana demo accounts developer and viewer (IAM 3.1)No longer created on a new install. Existing ones are kept with their platform role (they now get Grafana's role from it like everyone); deactivating them is a User Manager decision.Accounts are never created or deleted by a deploy script outside the account decisions. The rollback (IAM_GATES=false) re-creates them and their old Grafana roles.
Dashboard edits in the browser (IAM 3.1)Saving a change to a provisioned dashboard (the ones from the repository) is refused: those change only through the repository and the report deploy. A Grafana Editor (engineer) can still create a new dashboard, or copy one with "Save as", in the browser; such a dashboard was visible to other Grafana users until Grafana restarted, then it was gone, and it is not part of the record. Since 1 November 2026 Grafana keeps it (row "Grafana keeps its data").The engineer becomes Grafana Editor. Grafana keeps no data between restarts, so an edit in the browser would be lost at the next restart anyway, and it would bypass the repository, which is the dashboards' record. Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026: Editors may create new dashboards in the browser; only dashboards in the repository are kept.
Grafana sessions at the switch (IAM 3.1)No separate sign-out step. The new role setting restarts Grafana, which keeps no data, so every session ends and each person's Grafana role is worked out again at their next sign-in. Since 1 November 2026 Grafana keeps its sessions across a restart (row "Grafana keeps its data"): a new role setting reaches an open session when it ends, within 5 minutes of no use and at most one hour after its sign-in.A revocation step would have had to act as the break-glass account on every user, and could have removed Grafana Admin from the break-glass account itself.
Grafana session length (IAM 3.1, review)One hour from sign-in, whether used or not (since 1 November 2026 also 5 minutes after last use, row "Short sessions and one-hop sign-out"); then Grafana sends the person through the platform sign-in again, which checks the role.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026: a removed role or a deactivation reaches Grafana within the hour. Grafana does not see the end of a platform sign-in by itself. The cost is a pass through the sign-in every hour, usually without typing a password.
Rollback of Grafana's role setting (IAM 3.1)The existing switch IAM_GATES: set false, then ./deploy-infra.sh (Grafana is infrastructure). No new switch.One lever for every sign-in gate. The rollback admits any enabled account to Grafana again, so it is broad; it is for an emergency.
Who may use the AI workbench (IAM 3.2)Read on the workbench admits: engineer and qa keep it, with admin, bio_scientist and control_scientist. auditor and operator lose it.The policy's matrix is kept as written. The workbench has no read-only mode, so a person admitted with Read gets a full workspace that runs code; the matrix (invariant I6) already limits engineer to at most Read and keeps production roles out.
AI workbench session length (IAM 3.2)One hour: the hub's session and each notebook's own token expire after an hour, then the sign-in runs again and checks the role. Replaced by 5 minutes on 1 November 2026 (row "Short sessions and one-hop sign-out").Matches the one-hour idle shutdown of a workspace. The cost is that a notebook tab open longer than an hour reloads through the sign-in.
Home folders of people who lose the workbench (IAM 3.2)Kept; nothing is deleted. Removing a person from the hub keeps their home folder too.Deleting a person's work is removal, which the policy gives nobody on the workbench; administration (Configure) is not deletion. Files come back if access is restored. Removing a folder for good is a host operator's task outside the platform.
Stored sign-in tokens on the AI workbench (IAM 3.2)Not stored. The role is checked at each sign-in, so a role change reaches the workbench when the sign-in runs again: within the hour until 1 November 2026, within 5 minutes since (an open notebook tab about 10), rather than at the next token refresh. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.Storing them would let the hub administrator read every person's Keycloak tokens and act as that person in the platform's other services, including a qa confirmation of the administrator's own change. Since 31 October 2026 the hub administrator holds a narrower role (workbench-admin) without the right to read them, but they stay unstored: storing them would make that one missing right the only thing between the administrator and every person's tokens.
Rollback of the workbench gate (IAM 3.2)The existing switch IAM_GATES: set false, then ./infra/deploy-workbench.sh or ./deploy-infra.sh. The administrator account (ADMIN_USER) is then the hub administrator, through the same workbench-admin role, so it still cannot open others' workspaces, and removing a person from the hub still keeps their files.One lever for every sign-in gate. The two protections are not part of the gate, and rolling them back would only bring back an administrator who can read and delete people's work.
What the AI workbench administrator may do (IAM 3.2, 31 October 2026)A hub role of its own, workbench-admin, with exactly: the admin page, listing and reading hub users, starting and stopping workspaces, and removing hub users (JupyterHub scopes admin-ui, list:users, read:users, servers, delete:users). Never tokens, access to workspaces, sharing, changing users (which includes making oneself administrator), groups or stored sign-in state. Its members are the roles the policy gives Configure on the workbench (admin) with IAM_GATES true, and the administrator account alone with it false. Nobody holds JupyterHub's built-in administrator role in either mode; deploying strips it from accounts that held it. The deprecated admin_access setting is kept false with a note that it does nothing, and no check relies on it. Taken by the platform owner's standing direction.The built-in role can create a token in any person's name, which opens that person's workspace, and its rights cannot be narrowed; the setting meant to stop it had been ineffective since JupyterHub 2.0, which the live test found (see the known gaps). The admin page's group view stays empty: reading groups was left out.
What the workbench test counts as access (IAM 3.2, 31 October 2026)The hub's "Authorize" page for someone else's workspace counts as access: the administrator must get 403 at the authorize step. Creating a token for another person must itself be refused (403); a token that is created fails the test even if it is then refused, and is deleted at once.The hub shows the authorize page only to someone who may already open that workspace, and a token it creates for a person is that person's own and always works. The test passed on both before the fix because it never pressed "Authorize" and accepted a refused token.
Node-RED and pgAdmin sign-in findings (IAM 3.3, 3.4, 31 October 2026)Test fixes only. The sign-in test reads the Node-RED editor token as the editor itself does, fails when the token is not accepted or not revoked, and gives its test accounts e-mail addresses at <project>.example.org; pgAdmin's own refusal message is reported. No change to pgAdmin.Both failures were the test's: it damaged the Node-RED token before using it, and pgAdmin refuses the reserved .invalid domain when it creates a user. The access rules worked as the policy says. Demo accounts still use .invalid, so pgAdmin would refuse one if it were ever given admin.
Node-RED storage (IAM 3.3)Node-RED keeps its flows, palette modules and editor sessions on a volume, so a restart no longer empties it. The health check compares the running flows with the repository and reports editor changes.Before, every restart stopped telemetry from being stored until the flows were deployed again, and the editor's own sign-in setting restarts Node-RED. The cost is that flows changed in the editor survive restarts; the health check is what makes them visible.
Palette modules in Node-RED (IAM 3.3)Nobody installs palette modules from the editor, and a function node cannot load npm modules of its own. Modules come only from the list in the repository, installed by the Node-RED deploy.A module is code that runs with the ingest account. Taking it through the repository gives it a review and a record.
Node-RED and the MQTT Explorer during an IAM_GATES rollback (IAM 3.3)They keep the list compiled from the policy (engineer), and Node-RED keeps its editor rights from the policy.The pre-policy list would close both to engineer and open them to admin, who must not reach telemetry or device control (invariants I2 and I11).
Read-only access to Node-RED (IAM 3.3)Not offered: Node-RED gives editor rights to the roles holding Update on its flows, and nothing to the others. Default taken by the implementer; confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.Node-RED's read-only mode still shows the flows' configuration, which holds the ingest database password.
Node-RED editor session (IAM 3.3)8 hours, as long as the platform sign-in's own session. Default taken by the implementer; confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. Never in effect: Node-RED read the setting only inside its sign-in settings, so editor sessions lasted its default of a week. Replaced by one hour on 1 November 2026 (row "Short sessions and one-hop sign-out").A shorter session would send the editor through its sign-in in the middle of unsaved edits. Deactivation still ends access within about four minutes at the platform sign-in; a narrower role applies at the next editor sign-in.
Node-RED time zone (IAM 3.3)NODE_RED_TZ now sets Node-RED's time zone. Before, the setting never reached Node-RED, which ran in UTC. Default taken by the implementer; confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.The setting is asked at install and described as Node-RED's time zone. Stored telemetry is unaffected: its time stamps come from the boards. Times shown in Node-RED's log and debug output move to local time.
Host-shell onboarding without a sign-in (IAM 3.4)Kept as the host operator's break-glass path: flash-device.sh --unregistered and the other terminal paths that flash a board without an admitted sign-in stay, and are listed as a known gap.They need a shell on the platform host and serial port access, which only the host operator has, and they register nothing and give the board no credential. Closing them belongs with separating the host operator from the administrator.
EDGE_WIZARD_ROLE while IAM_GATES is true (IAM 3.4)Ignored with a warning; the access policy alone decides who may onboard (engineer, maintenance). The setting is used only after a rollback to IAM_GATES=false.The policy is the one source of access rights; a second setting that could narrow or widen it would not show in the policy review.
maintenance on the onboarding wizard (IAM 3.4)Admitted, as the policy says; the restriction to existing slots stays with the database at registration, and the erase before it is recorded as a known gap until the wizard checks it.Refusing maintenance would contradict the policy; no account holds the role today, so the gap affects nobody yet.
Rollback of the personal MQTT credentials (IAM 3.5)A switch of its own, IAM_MQTT_PERSON (default false, a ./guide.sh recipe, health check iammqtt), instead of the plan's IAM_GATES=false. The enable and rollback recipes run ./deploy-infra.sh and the MQTT Explorer's deploy; iam-api reads the switch from the shared settings, so it needs no redeploy.IAM_GATES governs every sign-in gate: rolling it back to undo the Explorer would cut seven roles off every host.
Where a person may send over MQTT (IAM 3.5)Only to a unit's control topics; never to control_setpoint, which a person may read.Boards report their state on control_setpoint: a person sending there could forge what a board reports.
Commissioning screen (IAM 3.5)Not in this step: the MQTT Explorer is read-only in practice until a Configuration UI action exists where an engineer proposes a unit's commissioning flag and qa approves it. Listed as a known gap. Built with the Approvals tab on 31 October 2026 (rows below).Decided by the platform owner: building it belongs with the other approval screens.
The shared MQTT_EXPLORER_PASS (IAM 3.5)Kept in config.env at the switch, unused while it is true, for the rollback; removed in a later step after a trial period. Superseded by the row below on 1 November 2026.A rollback then brings back a working Explorer at once. Anyone using that password outside the page (a command-line client) loses access at the switch.
Removing the shared MQTT Explorer account (IAM 3.5, 1 November 2026)The trial is over: the personal credentials have been on since 31 October 2026 and the broker refuses the shared account on both ports. The account and MQTT_EXPLORER_PASS are removed from the code: nothing generates, stores or gives it to the broker in either setting of IAM_MQTT_PERSON. The switch stays for the broker's listener split; while it is false the Explorer has no credential and its page says it needs the switch on. A leftover MQTT_EXPLORER_PASS in an older config.env is ignored with a one-line note. The health check (iammqtt) fails in either setting while the broker still knows the account. Decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026, ahead of a fresh installation.A credential that reads and writes every topic and names no person should not wait in config.env for a rollback that would bring it back. Retiring the false setting altogether would also change the broker's configuration and the guide's recipes; keeping the switch is the smaller change. Consequence: rolling the switch back now stops the Explorer instead of returning to the shared account.
One credential per sign-in (IAM 3.5)Each page gets its own user name, explorer-<username>-<6 random hex>, at most three active per person; the plan's single explorer-<username> is not used. Default taken by the implementer; confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.With one name per person, a second tab or a renewal would change the password under the first. The person and the policy history still identify who holds each credential.
Credential lifetime (IAM 3.5)8 hours, renewed in the background; stored in the page's memory only. Default taken by the implementer; confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.A working day, as the platform sign-in. Revocation does not wait for it: deactivation and role changes end it within about 15 seconds.
A sign-in token reused after a change (IAM 3.5)iam-api refuses a new credential when the account was deactivated or changed role after the token was issued.The sign-in proxy may forward a token up to 5 minutes old; without this check the page could get a fresh credential with the old role right after a revocation.
MQTT Explorer credentials after a policy change or a switch (IAM 3.5, review)./deploy-infra.sh ends every Explorer credential issued under an MQTT policy other than the current one, and every Explorer credential when IAM_MQTT_PERSON changes in either direction; each ending is recorded in the policy history. The Explorer then asks for a new credential. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.A credential must mirror the person's current rights. Without this, a narrowed policy left issued credentials with their old topics for up to 8 hours, and switching the personal credentials off and on again brought old credentials back.
MQTT Explorer credentials while the database cannot be read (IAM 3.5, review)The broker drops every send right at once and keeps the read rights it last read for at most 30 seconds; after that it admits no person credential until it can read them again. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.A revocation (deactivation, narrowing) is only seen through a successful read. Keeping the old rights for as long as the read fails would let a deactivated person keep reading for up to 8 hours.
Guide recipe for turning on the personal MQTT credentials (IAM 3.5, review)The recipe deploys iam-api right after setting the switch and before the broker changes; if iam-api cannot be deployed, the recipe stops there.Decided by the platform owner: the recipe must redeploy whatever reads IAM_MQTT_PERSON. Otherwise the broker could drop the shared account while no credential service exists, leaving nobody able to use the Explorer.
Node-RED without its own sign-in (IAM 3.3, review)The break-glass (NR_ADMIN_AUTH=off) stays, is listed in the policy's break-glass register, and ./deploy-infra.sh records it in the policy history (break_glass_seen) when it finds it, before turning the sign-in on again. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.The break-glass is there to repair a broken editor sign-in, so a token-only mode would not serve it. While it is on, Node-RED's admin interface is open to every program on the platform host and inside the cluster that may reach it; the record shows that it happened and when Node-RED started that way. Who set it is not recorded (it needs the host's cluster access).
Where pgAdmin is linked (review)From the Platform Management page of the portal, as a card for administrators (admin); no longer from Database Management.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. Database Management is for the roles that export records (auditor, qa, qp), who cannot use pgAdmin; pgAdmin shows the administrator the schema and configuration, not production data.
Reading platform data from the AI workbench (IAM 3.2b)Per person through the database API: a notebook signs its person in with a device code (a link and a short code, as the onboarding wizard's terminal sign-in) on a public Keycloak client, workbench-notebook, keeps the sign-in only in memory and reads config-api with it, so the database applies that person's role and row rules. No database password and no stored sign-in token is in any workspace; the hub still stores no tokens. The administrator's role reads nothing regulated this way (invariant I2). Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.A shared database account for the workbench would read every unit's data for everyone and record nobody; the hub's own tokens would let the hub administrator act as every person. The device code needs no secret in the workspace, and every read is the person's own.
Scientists read telemetry (IAM 3.2b)bio_scientist and control_scientist hold Read, not export, on telemetry. Invariant I5 now allows Read there and still refuses export and writes. Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.Research works on the production measurements without taking a copy out through Database Management. As a consequence of the matrix, the two roles also pass the Run Monitor's gate; it lists runs, which they may not read, so it shows them nothing useful. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.
Which platform services a notebook may reach (IAM 3.2b)The database API, and Keycloak for the sign-in itself; nothing else of the platform (no private addresses otherwise). Both hops are compiled from the access policy (network.clients.notebooks) and open only while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES are both true, in config.env and in the running config-api and database: the workbench deploy checks both, the switch recipes run it (last on an enable, first on a rollback), and the database and frontend deploys close the route on a running workbench whenever either switch is false. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.The decision named the database API only, but a notebook cannot sign in or renew its sign-in without Keycloak, which every browser on the network reaches anyway. Before both switches are true the database API would run a verified notebook request as its anonymous role, which still holds the old blanket grants: opening it then would give every notebook user write access to the platform's tables.
Where scientists keep their notebooks (IAM 3.2b)In a separate research repository on GitHub (WORKBENCH_NOTEBOOK_REPO, suggested smvieira/medcea-notebooks, created by the platform owner), not in the platform's repository. Each scientist pushes with their own GitHub account and a personal token kept only in their home folder; commits carry their name. The workspace has the JupyterLab Git panel. Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.Research code is not production code and must not need a platform review to be saved; the commits stay attributable to the person. The platform holds no GitHub credential.
Cell outputs in git (IAM 3.2b)Removed on every commit: nbstripout is set up as a required global git filter in every home folder at each workspace start. Behind it, a git check in every workspace refuses a commit whose notebooks still hold outputs (a repository's own settings can turn the filter off, not the check), and if nbstripout cannot be installed the filter is still required, so git refuses to commit notebooks rather than store them whole. The rest (code and text cells) is not checked; recorded as a known gap. Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.Outputs are where telemetry values end up in a notebook, and the scientists hold Read, not export.
Workbench home folders (IAM 3.2b)Kept: removing a person from the hub, losing the workbench and every redeploy keep them. Every uninstall that deletes the namespace or MicroK8s (including the wipe that ./deploy-infra.sh offers when MicroK8s is not fully up) lists them, with the directory that holds each one and how to keep it, before it asks for confirmation. Backups are not built: a known gap, and a planned item. Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.Scientists' notebooks must persist. Making an uninstall refuse would block a reinstall; a warning with the way out leaves the decision with the person at the host.
Workbench image (IAM 3.2b)Pinned by digest (WORKBENCH_IMAGE, default quay.io/jupyter/scipy-notebook:latest@sha256:d416eea5…c2ce): the image that was running on 31 October 2026, checked live to bring JupyterLab 4.6.4, the Git panel 0.55 and nbdime; nothing is added to it.With latest, JupyterLab and its Git panel could change under people at any pull, and the Git panel is now part of how work is kept.
Notebook sign-ins only read (IAM 3.2b review)A token from the notebooks' client (workbench-notebook) is served for reads only: the database refuses any other request ("may only read") before it takes the person's role, and runs the reads in a read-only transaction. In the Configuration UI the same person keeps every right their role has. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.The decision made the notebooks a read path. Notebook code is nobody's validated code, and engineer and qa, who may open the workbench, may change master data or approve configuration; without this, a notebook could do that with their sign-in, recorded exactly like a change made in the Configuration UI.
qa reads telemetry and runs from notebooks (IAM 3.2b)Intended: qa includes the auditor's read rights and is admitted to the AI Workbench, so a qa notebook reads telemetry and runs as rr_qa; auditors stay out of the workbench and inspect through logged exports. Confirmed by the platform owner on 31 October 2026.qa is the quality unit and works with the data; the auditor triggers no system action (SoD-7) and leaves a record of what it takes.
Production data access (31 October 2026)Left as it is (see who reaches production data, and how). No role reaches production data through pgAdmin. Auditors get no ad-hoc query path: they export through Database Management, where each export is recorded, and analyse the files offline; they stay out of the AI workbench.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. Each path is either recorded (exports), the person's own role in the database (the database API, notebooks) or a gated read-only view (Monitor, Grafana); an ad-hoc query path for auditors would add one that is none of these.
Return and Withdraw (approvals, 31 October 2026)qa may return a draft or a waiting proposal with a reason (a returned draft stays an editable draft; a returned proposal is closed and never applies), and the person who proposed a change may withdraw it while it waits. Both are recorded in medcea.approval and never count as an approval.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. Without them a wrong proposal could only wait, or be replaced by a newer one.
Parameter-set editor and Retire (approvals, 31 October 2026)The Configuration UI gains a parameter-set editor (engineers write drafts, qa approves them in the Approvals tab), and recipes, recipe steps and parameter sets are retired instead of deleted.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. Nobody holds delete on recipes (invariant I1), so the old Delete buttons could only fail.
Commissioning while a run is running or scheduled (31 October 2026)Refused, at the proposal and again at the approval, while the unit has a run in R (running) or S (scheduled); a policy refusal, so a warning while IAM_DB_ROLES is false.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. A flagged unit lets engineers command it and refuses every run start, so flagging it under a scheduled run would make that run fail to start.
The run side of "no commissioning while a run is running or scheduled" (review, 31 October 2026)Scheduling a run (a new run, or a failed run set back to scheduled) is refused on a unit flagged for commissioning, and reads the unit's row under a lock first, so it cannot slip past a commissioning approval in progress; a policy refusal, so a warning while IAM_DB_ROLES is false.Follows from the platform owner's decision above; found in the review of the build. Without it a run could be scheduled during the approval check and leave a flagged unit with a scheduled run.
Who closes a log-setting proposal (review, 31 October 2026)As for commissioning: qa (approve or return) or its proposer (withdraw). Another person's new proposal for the same setting is refused while one waits; the proposer may replace their own, which then reads "replaced". A proposal needs a token that carries a person.Follows from the platform owner's Return and Withdraw decision above; found in the review of the build. Before, any later proposal silently closed the earlier one, with no record of who closed it.
Ending commissioning (31 October 2026)Applies at once when an engineer ends it, and waits in the Approvals tab for qa's review afterwards.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. Ending it removes command rights (a narrowing, like a deactivation); making it wait would keep those rights open.
Approval status for authors (31 October 2026)Every person sees the approval status of the drafts they changed and their own proposals (needed to withdraw them), through a read function; the approver's name and note are not shown to them, a return's reason is.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand. The approval history stays with qa, qp and auditor.
After-the-fact reviews of master data and devices (31 October 2026)Not listed in the Approvals tab for now; the policy's help text no longer says they are. Listed as a known gap.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand. An unreviewed change blocks nothing, so the queue keeps to what waits.
Who sees commissioning proposals (31 October 2026)The proposer's name and reason go to engineer, qa, qp and auditor and to the proposer; the approver's name to qa, qp and auditor only. Every other role sees the flag and "waiting for qa". The run that blocks a unit is shown only to roles that read every unit's runs.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand. An operator must not learn another unit's run state.
Applying commissioning makes no author (31 October 2026)The qa person who applies a commissioning change does not become an author of that unit's master data, so they can still review the unit's other changes.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand. The flag changes only under dual control, like a status.
Commissioning in the policy history (31 October 2026)Every commissioning on and off is appended to the tamper-evident policy history (commissioning_enabled, commissioning_disabled), with the unit, the actor and the reason.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand. The flag opens MQTT command rights, as a unit assignment opens data rights.
Waiting changes in the health check (31 October 2026)./health-check.sh (check iampending) warns when a pending change (a commissioning proposal, a log setting, or the review of a commissioning end that applied at once) has waited for qa more than 7 days.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand. The same limit as account decisions (iamdecisions).
Proofs for approvals and commissioning (31 October 2026)Proved on the throwaway test database (tests/iam-db.sh W8, tests/iam-db-fixture.sh); production gets read-only checks after the deploy, and a walk-through on production is optional. The live broker test (tests/iam-mqtt.sh --commissioning) is opt-in and leaves its proposal and approval rows, which are append-only.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand.
Walk-through of approvals and commissioning on production (31 October 2026)Done by hand after the deploy, with the test accounts eng (engineer) and svieira (qa), one person. Parameter set q_test (#1): created and edited by eng, approved by svieira at 21:59:54 UTC (approval #6, no note). Test mode on UNIT01: proposed by eng at 22:30:02 (pending change #1), approved and applied by svieira at 22:30:36 (approval #7, commissioning_enabled); eng got an MQTT Explorer credential (mqtt_credential_issued, 22:34) and could send to a concrete UNIT01 control topic; ended by eng at 22:36:45, applied at once (pending change #2, commissioning_disabled), reviewed by svieira at 22:40:14 (#8). A second round: on (pending change #3, approved 22:40:18) and off (#4, 22:42:01, reviewed 22:43:18). Afterwards both units were off, nothing was pending and the policy history verified. The health check passed 50 of 50 checks, iampending included, and the throwaway test database passed 215 of 215 checks with IAM_DB_ROLES=true. Also that evening svieira confirmed a unit assignment of UNIT01 (decision 14, 22:13).Evidence that the screens, the database functions, the broker and the policy history work together on the live install, beyond the throwaway database. The maker and the checker are test accounts of one person (see test accounts), so it shows the mechanism, not independent review. The deploy and the walk-through found the two defects listed in the known gaps, and four faults in the throwaway database's tests (among them md5(), which production's PostgreSQL image does not support), fixed in commit b057b42.
Manual for approvals and commissioning (31 October 2026)The chapters and this record now; no new edition until the next phase.Default taken by the implementer, announced to the platform owner on 31 October 2026, who let it stand.
Network policy for the broker (IAM 3.5)Deferred. Listed as a known gap.It needs a new kind of rule (any source on the LAN port, only the Explorer's proxy on the WebSocket port) in the policy compiler; decided by the platform owner to come later.
Where a run is released (31 October 2026)qp releases a completed run in the Configuration UI's Approvals tab: every completed run without a release is listed for the roles holding approval on runs (today only qp) with a summary and a Release action. The Scheduler's completed runs and run window show a Released badge with who and when.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. One queue for everything that waits for an independent person, and the Scheduler shows the outcome where runs are followed.
No hold or refusal of a run (31 October 2026)No hold or refuse action: a run qp will not release stays unreleased in the queue. Deviations and CAPA (Spec RACI 10) are not built; listed as a known gap.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. A hold without a deviation record behind it would be a record with no content; the deviation process is a later piece of work.
Release note and run summary (31 October 2026)An optional note, recorded in the release and in its approval row (release_run with a note; the version without one keeps working). qp keeps read access on runs only, not run logs: the summary shows status, unit, recipe and parameter set with their approvals, creator, every executor, attempts, cycles, start and end, and why the person may or may not release, computed by the same rule the release applies. No run log.Decided by the platform owner on 31 October 2026. The note keeps the release's reasoning with it; the summary gives qp what to decide on without widening their access to the logs, and one rule keeps the screen and the database from disagreeing. An operator never sees another unit's runs through it.
Runs without a recorded person are never released (1 November 2026)A completed run with no recorded person behind its creation or start (one completed before migration 008b, or started through a fallback while the database did not enforce roles) is listed for qp with the reason "no recorded person created or started this run" and is refused by the database. There is no override, retrospective release or release with a mandatory note.Decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026. The release rule (the releaser neither created, changed nor executed the run) cannot be checked for such a run, and a release that cannot show its independence is not a release.
pgAdmin's server entry per person, and its version (1 November 2026)The IoT Postgres entry is no longer shared from pgAdmin's built-in user: each enabled account pgAdmin admits gets its own copy at every pgAdmin start, from the list ./deploy-infra.sh reads from Keycloak. pgAdmin is pinned to 9.19.0 by image digest instead of following the floating tag 9.Decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026 ("apply proper fix", "update to the last pgadmin"): pgAdmin does not share a server's username or password file, so the shared entry could not connect for anyone signed in through Keycloak. The cost is that a newly granted admin sees the server only after the next ./deploy-infra.sh. A pinned digest changes only through the repository, so an upgrade is a reviewed change.
The pgAdmin database password in each admin's storage folder (1 November 2026)pgAdmin 9.19 connects with a password file only when it sits in the signed-in person's own storage folder, so the start hook copies rr_admin_login's password file there for every listed administrator (mode 600). An administrator can therefore see that file, and the password in it, in pgAdmin's storage manager, and could download it.No working alternative keeps it from them: a password typed at the prompt or saved in pgAdmin is the same password, and pgAdmin refuses a file elsewhere. rr_admin_login holds only rr_admin's grants (reads a few master-data and policy tables, proposes settings), the same reach pgAdmin already gives, and the database is reachable only inside the cluster. A rotated password restarts pgAdmin (medcea/config-checksum), which refreshes every copy.
AI workbench start-up and who it shows (1 November 2026)The workspace's installs (real-time collaboration, VS Code, nbstripout) and its git settings run before the workspace starts instead of alongside it; every install has a time limit, and the hub waits up to 15 minutes for a first start. The workbench moves to chart 4.4.4 (JupyterHub 5.5.4) and keeps the pinned notebook image; the hub's warning that the image's JupyterHub version (6.1.1) differs is accepted. The container account stays jovyan; the terminal prompt and the git author name show the person's username, and each person sets their own git e-mail. Decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026.On a new home folder the installs raced the workspace's start and no document opened until a restart. Keeping the installs keeps the deployment to one Helm release with no image registry. Renaming the account inside the container would change every path (/home/jovyan, the examples' mount) for no gain in what is recorded: commits and the prompt are what a person and a reviewer read, and the hub's own records already name the person.
Short sessions and one-hop sign-out (1 November 2026)Each app's own session is short and, when it ends, the app signs in again through the platform sign-in without a prompt, as whoever is signed in on that browser now: the AI Workbench hub and each notebook's token 5 minutes, Grafana 5 minutes without use and at most one hour.
