The Node-RED editor keeps one hour and pgAdmin 60 minutes idle and at most one day (its shortest), not 5 minutes: at the end of an editor session undeployed flow changes are at risk, and a new pgAdmin session drops open Query Tool transactions. The user menu's Sign out passes through the AI Workbench's own logout when the workbench is installed; the workbench's File → Log Out continues from its logout to the platform sign-in's "Do you want to log out?" page and then to the portal. Without the workbench, Sign out is as before. Every address in the chain is fixed at deploy, and no sign-in token is in any address. Decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026. Changed in the review the same day, by the implementer, for the owner to confirm: Sign out first ends the sign-in gate of the app it is chosen in and the platform sign-in, and only then passes through the workbench's logout, instead of starting at the workbench; Grafana's session keeps a one-hour maximum and ends after 5 minutes without use, instead of 5 minutes outright; and Node-RED drops, at each start, stored editor sessions longer than the hour.Reported live the same day: after Sign out and signing in as another account in the same browser, the AI Workbench still served the previous person. Its own one-hour session never heard of the sign-out, and Grafana, pgAdmin and the Node-RED editor behaved the same way. A sign-out that visits every app was designed and set aside as too fragile. Short sessions make every app follow the person signed in to the platform within minutes, also after an account switch or an expired sign-in, not only after Sign out. The one hop through the workbench ends the session most often reported at once. The editor's setting had been ignored until then, so editor sessions lasted a week. The review's changes: starting at the workbench left the app signed out from open for up to 4 minutes and made the whole sign-out depend on the workbench answering (a workbench that is down or failed to deploy stopped it before anything ended); a hard 5-minute Grafana end reloaded the page and dropped an Editor's unsaved panel work every 5 minutes, the same reason the editor and pgAdmin keep an hour; and editor sessions issued under the week would otherwise have outlived the change by days. The remaining windows are listed in the known gaps. Exercise E10 in the training chapter checks this behaviour.
Grafana keeps its data (1 November 2026)Grafana keeps its database in its own volume (grafana, 2Gi by default, GRAFANA_STORAGE). Editors (engineer) may create dashboards in the browser, or "Save as" copies of the platform's, and keep them; the platform's own dashboards stay read-only and change only through the repository and the report deploy. Sessions survive a restart of Grafana, so a session ends only at its own bounds (5 minutes without use, at most one hour after its sign-in), and a changed Grafana role setting reaches an open session then, no longer at the restart. The volume was not backed up when this was decided (the nightly backup copies it since the same day); every uninstall and the MicroK8s wipe name it before they ask to confirm, and the health check warns when it is missing. The volume's directory on the host is closed to Grafana's own account (mode 700) at every start of Grafana, because the host's volume storage creates it open to every local account, which could otherwise replace Grafana's database with one of its own (review of 1 November 2026); the health check warns when it is open. Decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026.Until then Grafana kept nothing between restarts: an Editor's own dashboard vanished at the next deploy, which the known gaps listed. The owner wants Editors to build and keep working dashboards in Grafana; the dashboards that are part of the platform's record remain the ones in the repository. Keeping sessions across a restart means a role change no longer ends every Grafana session at once; the 5-minute and one-hour bounds of "Short sessions and one-hop sign-out" bound that instead, as they already did for a change to one person's role. Exercise E11 in the training chapter checks this behaviour.
A widening change of the platform skeleton (2 November 2026)From IAM slice 4.2a every deploy refuses a change in git that gives any person role access it does not have, until qa has approved it as a skeleton version and it is applied. No report-only period: until approvals exist (slice 4.4) such a change cannot deploy. All Phase 4 entries of the policy therefore landed in slice 4.1, before the check exists.Decided by the platform owner on 2 November 2026: a person's access changes only with a second person's approval, also when the change comes from git.
Who may approve a policy version at apply (2 November 2026)Keycloak shows the approver's account enabled, holding qa, with a person_id that is no author's and no enabled admin account's (I17 at apply). A disabled admin account of the same person does not refuse. No confirmed account decision is required; the qa account and person_id that ./deploy-infra.sh seeds are recorded in the policy history as a bootstrap fact.Decided by the platform owner on 2 November 2026.
Rolling a policy back (2 November 2026)apply.sh --rollback-to may return to an approved version or to a confirmed bootstrap or rollback version (version 1 included), and is refused when that version would give more under today's skeleton than was approved. Only the policy part of each owning deploy is re-run.Decided by the platform owner on 2 November 2026: a rollback restores at once, then qa confirms or disputes it.
Break-glass and the skeleton gate (IAM 4.2a, 2 November 2026)A policy break-glass (MEDCEA_POLICY_FILE with a reason) changes only the matrix and the waivers, never the rows for iam_policy and iam_users; it is typed at the terminal, the file is copied and recorded (break_glass_seen) before anything is compiled, and it ends per component. Break-glass does not bypass the skeleton gate: a git change that gives access is refused under break-glass too.The owner's answer OA-a ("a widening git change cannot deploy until approvals exist") read conservatively. An earlier draft of the design called break-glass "the only way" past the gate; that was withdrawn.
Version 1 of the access policy (IAM 4.2a, 2 November 2026)Made from the factory file defaults.yaml by apply.sh --bootstrap inside the iam deploy, applied at once, with nothing re-rendered (every compile is proved identical first). The person named with --by is recorded as a claim; a qa person of another person_id confirms it in the Policy UI once that exists (IAM 4.3). Until confirmation is possible the health check counts it as OK.Decided in the design of slice 4.2a: a starting point is needed before any approval can be compared with something, and the factory file is what was in force.
Seeded accounts on the record (IAM 4.2a, 2 November 2026)./deploy-infra.sh records the accounts it creates and the person_id it sets as account_seeded facts; while the history cannot take them yet they wait in state/seed-facts.jsonl and the next deploy records them. On this install the qa and engineer accounts made before 4.2a are recorded once as observed.The owner's answer OA-b: the seeding of QA_USER and of person_id is recorded on the hash chain; who may approve is read from Keycloak at apply, without a confirmed account decision.
Policy-host tokens in both database modes (IAM 4.2a, 2 November 2026)The configuration API refuses a token of the policy host's client for anything but the four policy calls whether IAM_DB_ROLES is true or false.Without it, such a token under IAM_DB_ROLES=false would have had the legacy write rights of the anonymous role. No such token exists before IAM 4.3; the rule is in place before the client is.
What the skeleton gate counts as widening (IAM 4.2a, 2 November 2026)Besides a person role's reach: any new route without sign-in, upstream shared by every host, host service or client, network source allowed into a pod, host outside the sign-in proxies, service identity's privilege or membership, database row filter, only_rpc or token client, and any weakening of the invariants, the waivable list or the constraint rules. Help texts do not count.The owner's answer OA-a read conservatively. The owner's first decision named person roles; the review found real widenings outside them that would have been accepted silently. Consequence: no slice before IAM 4.5 may widen the skeleton.
What the policy controls defend against (IAM 4.2a, 2 November 2026)The deploying account's own tools, used carelessly or with stale state, cannot widen access without approval. The same account acting deliberately against them (psql as postgres, editing the scripts, forging digests) is the platform's break-glass class: found afterwards (the policy history, the version audit, the health check's recomputation of every accept), not prevented.Decided in the critique of the 4.2a design: preventing that would need a second administrator the prototype does not have (see known gaps, Host operator).
Policy approvals with the person's own token (IAM 4.1, 2 November 2026)iam-api's database account (iam_svc) approves nothing: the A level left its ceiling. A qa person approves a policy version through the configuration API with their own token of the policy host; the database refuses any other path, and keeps the signed token as evidence for apply.sh to verify.A service account that knows its password could otherwise approve in someone's name; a signed token cannot be made by anyone who only holds a database password.
A blank person_id approves nothing (IAM 4.1, 2 November 2026)Every approval in the database (recipes, algorithms, parameter sets, changes, run releases, controller state, policy versions) now refuses a token whose person_id is blank, as account decisions already did; the approval queue shows such a token as unable to approve."Not your own work" is checked by person; a blank person would match nobody. ./deploy-infra.sh gives every enabled account a non-blank person_id, so no current approver is affected.
Confirming version 1 (3 November 2026)Version 1 is confirmed in the Access Policy by a qa account other than the one apply.sh --bootstrap --by named (svieira, itself the install's QA_USER): sergio_qa. Its person_id differs from svieira's, so the database's two-person rule is met formally, but on this prototype both accounts belong to one human (see the test accounts). The confirmation exercises the mechanism; it is not independent review.The owner's choice (IAM 4.3, design §14.6.9): the alternative, leaving version 1 unconfirmed until a second real person exists, would keep the health check warning indefinitely and leave the confirmation path untested on the live platform. A deployment with real users must have the version confirmed by a different human. Exercise E16 shows it: done 4 November 2026, history event 51 by sergio_qa, after the database refused svieira's own confirmations.
Scoped re-render of the policy artefacts (3 November 2026)Each script that deploys something compiled from the access policy can re-render only that, and only where the cluster differs from the compile (--policy-only, --gate-only); ./apps/iam/apply.sh --resume runs all of them. A scoped run restarts no application: a sign-in proxy rolls by itself when its gate changes, Grafana and pgAdmin redeploy their whole section when their roles drifted (their roles are one input of a release that needs all the others), and the onboarding wizard restarts only when its roles changed, never during an onboarding run: it defers (exit 4), --resume records apply_deferred, and the next --resume closes it. The deploy scripts refuse options they do not know.The owner's choice (IAM 4.2b, design §14.5): a policy change should touch only what the policy compiles, so it reaches the platform without a full deploy's restarts and cannot carry unrelated changes with it; an apply must never kill a board being flashed. A deferral closes only through --resume, because a plain wizard deploy is not an apply event and closing it otherwise needs a new event in the policy history (a migration), which this slice avoids. Refusing unknown options: a mistyped --policy-only used to run a full deploy. Exercise E15 shows it.
The manual in the platform (3 November 2026)The platform manual is served by the portal at /manual/, linked by a Platform manual card on its main page, behind the portal's own sign-in: its audience is everyone the portal admits, every platform role, and nobody without one. No access policy change: the portal's gate already covers every path of its address. Each publish builds it from the chapters in the repository; the health check warns when the portal's copy lags.The owner's choice: the people who use and run the platform read the procedures where they work, and the copy is the current one. The manual holds no secrets (no password, key or token), but it does describe administration procedures, the break-glass routes and the known gaps; those are visible to every role, which the owner accepted, since every reader is a vetted platform account and the controls do not depend on their being unknown. Exercise E14 shows it.
The approval diff (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)What a qa person approves is a diff: rendered by iam-api for a change, and by the host (apply.sh --propose-skeleton) for a skeleton version. The browser hashes the bytes it shows; that is a consistency check, not an integrity control, because the header and the body come in one response. The binding is iam-api's: before it forwards an approval it recomputes the diff (a change) or compares it with the diff the host stored (a skeleton version, which the database checks too). Applying the version (4.5) recomputes it on the host once more.Decided in the design of slice 4.4: the approval must name exactly what was shown, on the skeleton it was computed on, without trusting the browser.
Who made a skeleton version (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)A skeleton version's maker is the person named with apply.sh --propose-skeleton --by, a claim; the --by username and the OS account that ran it are written into the version's reason. The approver attests "I did not run apply.sh --propose-skeleton for this version.", as for confirming a bootstrap, and may not be the claimed person. Residual: an attestation is a statement, not proof.Whoever runs apply.sh holds the host; the script cannot prove who typed it.
A version that widens the approver's own role (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)Refused: by iam-api before forwarding, and for a skeleton version also by the database. The check uses the closure of the approver's signed-in role over includes; the approver's other accounts are checked at apply (4.5). Consequence: a version that adds to qa or auditor cannot be approved through this path (every policy approver holds qa, which includes auditor), and needs a separate decision, recorded here when it arises.Decided by the platform owner on 4 November 2026 (owner decision 5): approved always means an eligible approver.
The residuals of approving through the configuration API (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)Accepted until 4.5: a stored checker verdict is iam-api's claim (submit re-runs the checker in iam-api, the database only reads the verdict); a change version's diff_digest is checked by iam-api, not by the database, until 4.5 recomputes it at apply. A qa person who bypassed iam-api cannot reach the approve call (only the policy host's token is admitted, for four calls only, and the configuration API is reachable only from the Configuration UI, the node and the notebooks), but someone holding that path could record any well-formed diff digest for a change.Closing it in the database would need the compiler in PostgreSQL; slice 4.5 closes it on the host instead.
Break-glass blocks approvals (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)While a policy break-glass (source: file) is open, the database refuses every policy approval: "a policy break-glass is open (event N): approvals wait until a normal deploy of … ends it". The Break-glass tab lists the open ones.Approving against a matrix nobody approved, still in force, would give the approval a meaning it does not have.
An approved 4.4 version must not survive a revert of 4.4 (4 November 2026)Before reverting slice 4.4, withdraw any open change or skeleton version, approved ones included: in the Access Policy, or with ./apps/iam/apply.sh --withdraw-open (see IAM 4.4).It was approved under 4.4's checks, which the reverted code no longer makes, and slice 4.5 could otherwise apply it.
The checker verdict of a new draft (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)When an admin starts a draft, iam-api records the checker's verdict at once: it saves one cell unchanged, in the same transaction, so the history holds one extra "entry saved" event whose before and after are equal. A draft is never without a verdict, and nothing in the database changed for it.Decided by the platform owner on 4 November 2026 (owner decision 1).
Who may withdraw a policy version (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)Any admin may withdraw any open version, approved ones included, not only its authors: withdrawing grants nothing. When the Access Policy is unavailable the host operator withdraws with ./apps/iam/apply.sh --withdraw-open.Decided by the platform owner on 4 November 2026 (owner decision 3).
Verifying the record from the Access Policy (IAM 4.4, 4 November 2026)From slice 4.3 until 4 November 2026 the History tab's chain status read "Cannot verify the record: permission denied for function history_record": the Policy service (iam_svc) may run iam_policy.verify_chain() but not the owner-only function it hashes each event with. Found by the 4.4 live test (tests/iam-policy.sh step 11). verify_chain() now runs as its owner (SECURITY DEFINER, search path pinned); it only reads the history, which iam_svc already reads, and returns the verdict. The health check's own chain check (iamchain, as postgres) was never affected.Granting history_record() to iam_svc would widen the accepted skeleton, which no slice before 4.5 may do; running the verifier as its owner grants nobody anything.
Exercises E17 and E18 with one human (4 November 2026)Both run with the install's test accounts, as E16 did: maker admin, approver sergio_qa, their person_ids checked to differ. The two-person control, and E18's attestation "I did not run apply.sh --propose-skeleton for this version.", are met by the accounts, not by two humans, on this prototype. E17 ends by withdrawing the approved version. Results, 4 November 2026: E17 version 5 by admin, approved by svieira (qa; a different person_id, not the planned sergio_qa), withdrawn; E18 version 6 proposed --by admin from commit fa81d0c, approved by sergio_qa with the attestation, withdrawn by system:apply. Both prove the mechanism, not independent review (see E17, E18).Decided by the platform owner on 4 November 2026 (owner decisions 2 and 4); see the test accounts.
Nightly backups to a local folder (1 November 2026)One CronJob (apps/backup), daily at 02:30 UTC: pg_dump in custom format of the platform database and Keycloak's, the roles without passwords, and a copy of every AI workbench home, Node-RED's /data and Grafana's volume, into one dated folder per run in BACKUP_DIR on the host (default /var/backups/medcea, mode 700), with sizes, SHA-256 checksums and an OK marker written last. Folders older than BACKUP_KEEP_DAYS (14) are deleted after a good run, never the newest complete one. No uninstall deletes the folder. The health check warns after 26 hours without a complete backup and fails after 72. The restore is a documented, manual procedure (./apps/backup/backups.sh restore-help); no script runs it. Decided by the platform owner on 1 November 2026.Nothing backed up the workbench homes, Grafana's database or Node-RED's data, and the databases only through a manual export; the owner wants a copy from which a person's lost work or the whole platform can come back. A local folder needs no other service or credential; on its own disk it survives a reinstall and the mistakes it guards against, and the owner can point it at another disk. The restore stays manual because it stops services and drops databases, and what to put back (one home, or everything after a reinstall) is a decision a script cannot take safely. The job reads the volumes' whole directory read-only rather than each volume, so a new person's home is copied from its first night without a redeploy. Same host and disk is listed as a known gap. Exercise E12 in the training chapter checks a backup and a restore.

Running the platform

This chapter is for the administrator who runs the MedCEA host. It covers installing the platform with ./install.sh, the deploy scripts and the order they run in, the rule that only one deploy runs at a time, and ./guide.sh, the question-and-answer front end to all of these commands. It then explains the seven IAM switches in config.env that turn the access controls on step by step (and WORKBENCH_ENABLED, which the guide switches the same way), which of them a fresh install leaves off and how to turn those on, what the health check reports, which IAM test scripts to run and what a good result looks like, how operators are given units, how to uninstall, how the domain names reach other devices through Tailscale, and the credentials file. It ends with troubleshooting for problems an administrator can meet. Run every command from the repository root on the host.

Installing and updating

./install.sh is the one entry point. It runs three stages in order: the infrastructure (./deploy-infra.sh), every application (./deploy-apps.sh --all), and the health check (./health-check.sh). The same command updates an existing install: finished steps are skipped or updated in place, so it is safe to run again after a pull or after a failure. If a stage fails, the script stops, names the stage and its log file in logs/, and tells you to fix the cause and run it again.

CommandWhat it does
./install.shInstall or update everything: infrastructure, applications, health check. Asks for any missing settings first.
./install.sh --configureReview every setting (current values offered as defaults), then install.
./install.sh --infra-onlyOnly the infrastructure: MicroK8s, PostgreSQL, Keycloak, Grafana, Node-RED, the portal and the AI workbench.
./install.sh --apps-onlyOnly the applications. Needs the infrastructure and config.env.
./install.sh --helpThe options, including the uninstall levels (see Uninstalling).

At the end of an install the script prints the steps for trusting the platform's certificate authority on your devices and then the credentials box (see The credentials file). While any IAM switch is still false it then prints one line pointing to ./guide.sh → IAM switches → Bring IAM to full enforcement (see After a fresh install); a fresh install always prints it.

config.env and the setup questions

All settings live in config.env in the repository root. It is not in git and is readable only by its owner (mode 600), because it holds every platform password. You do not write it by hand: the first run of ./deploy-infra.sh (or ./install.sh) creates it from config.env.example by asking questions. Press Enter to accept the value shown in brackets. Later runs ask only for required values that are still empty. To review every setting, run ./deploy-infra.sh --configure, or ./infra/configure.sh to edit the settings without deploying.

The questions cover:

  • Project name (PROJECT_NAME, default medcea): the MQTT topic root, the Kubernetes namespace and the Keycloak realm. Lowercase, starting with a letter, 2 to 31 characters from a-z 0-9 -. Changing it after boards are flashed means reflashing them.
  • Domain (DOMAIN): every web page is served at <service>.<DOMAIN>. The menu offers a name (for example medcea, so the portal is portal.medcea) when Tailscale is up, the Tailscale or LAN IP with nip.io, or a custom domain you provide DNS for. Changing the domain later means running ./deploy-infra.sh again.
  • The platform administrator: username, a real e-mail address you read (Keycloak password resets go there, and it is the pgAdmin login) and password. Addresses on special-use domains (.local, .test, .invalid, .example, .localhost, .arpa, .onion) are refused here and for the qa person and the engineer: pgAdmin will not start with one.
  • The first qa person and the first engineer: username and e-mail for each. The qa person must be someone other than the administrator. Their initial passwords are generated and replaced at their first sign-in.
  • The PostgreSQL superuser password, the database volume size, and your Docker Hub username and access token (needed to pull the Bitnami Helm charts).
  • On the first run (no config.env yet) and on a full review (--configure): the time zone for Node-RED flows and schedules; the backup folder (BACKUP_DIR, an absolute path, default /var/backups/<project>; better on another disk than the data's, and when you cannot write its parent the applications stage asks for sudo once to create it) and the days to keep backups (BACKUP_KEEP_DAYS); and whether to deploy the AI workbench.

Some values are generated and never asked: the qa and engineer initial passwords, the Keycloak break-glass console account (kc-breakglass) and its password, and the passwords of the database service roles. Configuration also checks for Tailscale on every run. USE_TAILSCALE records whether the domain depends on it: when it is true, a deploy stops if Tailscale is down, rather than quietly falling back to the LAN address.

Password rules

A password you type must be 8 to 64 characters from A-Z a-z 0-9 . _ ~ - only. Other punctuation breaks the URLs and YAML files the password is written into, so the question asks again. Password input is hidden and typed twice. Pressing Enter generates a 20-character password (or keeps the current one if there is one), shows it once, and lists it in the credentials file.

Every command writes its own log in logs/ (readable by the platform user only): deploy-infra-<timestamp>.log, deploy-apps-…, a component deployed on its own app-<component>-deploy-… (or -uninstall-…), app-iam-units-…, deploy-workbench-…, health-check-…, the throwaway database test iam-db-fixture-<command>-… and each live test iam-<name>-…. A command run by another (a component by ./deploy-apps.sh, a test by the fixture) writes into its caller's log. The first line of the output names the file. MEDCEA_NO_LOG=1 turns it off for one run.

Configuration runs before the deploy log starts, so your answers and generated passwords never reach logs/. When configuration rebuilds config.env on a newer config.env.example (keeping your values), it saves the previous file as config.env.bak.

The deploy scripts and their order

install.sh is a thin wrapper. Each stage also works on its own, and after the first install you will usually run the stages directly.

ScriptWhat it deploys
./deploy-infra.shMicroK8s (bootstrapped only on the first run), PostgreSQL, Keycloak with the realm, clients and roles, the MQTT broker, Node-RED, Grafana, pgAdmin, the sign-in proxies, the portal, the AI workbench (unless WORKBENCH_ENABLED=false), the access policy's route table and the realm's public keys. It writes a log to logs/deploy-infra-<timestamp>.log.
./deploy-apps.shA checkbox menu of the application components under apps/. ./deploy-apps.sh --all deploys every component without asking.
./apps/<component>/deploy.shOne component on its own. The components are access, backup (the nightly backup, see Backup and restore), database, edge-wizard, frontend, iam, monitor, mqtt-explorer, node-red, report and scheduler (./deploy-apps.sh --help lists them).
./infra/deploy-workbench.shRedeploys only the AI workbench (JupyterHub, chart 4.4.4: JupyterHub 5.5.4). A workspace that is already running keeps its old setup until it is stopped and started again. Who may sign in and who is hub administrator come from the access policy; if the policy does not compile, the script stops before it changes anything and the running workbench stays as it is.

The order rules are:

  • Infrastructure first. The applications need the cluster, Keycloak and PostgreSQL that deploy-infra.sh creates. ./install.sh --apps-only refuses without config.env.
  • Database before scheduler. deploy-apps.sh runs components in alphabetical order, so database comes before scheduler. When you deploy them one by one, run ./apps/database/deploy.sh first: the scheduler deploy refuses until the database functions it needs exist.
  • Access after infrastructure. Run ./deploy-infra.sh, then ./apps/access/deploy.sh. The access deploy runs the database deploy itself when it needs to. Since 1 November 2026 the order also matters for Sign out: ./deploy-infra.sh registers the AI Workbench's logout address with each app's sign-in, and the access deploy then points Sign out at it. In the other order, Sign out shows Keycloak's "Invalid redirect uri" and ends nothing until ./deploy-infra.sh has run.
  • After a pull of new IAM code: ./deploy-infra.sh (the route table), then ./apps/database/deploy.sh, then ./deploy-apps.sh --all while no recipe run is active. Until all three have run, the health check's iamgrants and tests/iam-schema.sh stay red.
  • From IAM 4.2a, every deploy that compiles the access policy reads the applied version from PostgreSQL first and refuses while PostgreSQL is not Ready; ./deploy-infra.sh repairs PostgreSQL before it reads the policy, so run it first in that case. A git change that gives access is refused by every deploy (see The skeleton gate).
  • iam-api (the iam component, IAM 3.5) after ./apps/database/deploy.sh and ./apps/access/deploy.sh: alphabetical order already does this. While IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES is false it is skipped with a warning. Then turn on IAM_MQTT_PERSON (see IAM switches).
  • After rotating or removing a realm key in Keycloak, run ./deploy-infra.sh. It republishes the keys and restarts config-api when they changed.
  • pgAdmin restarts whenever ./deploy-infra.sh changes its sign-in settings (pgAdmin's client secret, the roles it admits, or ADMIN_PASS), which signs everyone out of pgAdmin; a run that changes none of them leaves it running. Only on an install deployed before 1 November 2026 (not after a fresh install, where the secret never left the Kubernetes Secret): the first ./deploy-infra.sh after that date restarts pgAdmin once, when its secrets move into a Kubernetes Secret, and removes the older copies of pgAdmin's deployment that still held them; pgAdmin's client secret was readable in plain text until then, so give it a new one once: in the Keycloak console, realm medcea, Clients, pgadmin, Credentials, Regenerate; then run ./deploy-infra.sh, which reads the new secret and restarts pgAdmin.
  • pgAdmin's IoT Postgres entry is loaded for each account that may sign in to pgAdmin when pgAdmin starts. ./deploy-infra.sh lists the e-mail addresses of the enabled Keycloak accounts holding a role pgAdmin admits (admin) and gives the list to pgAdmin; when the list changed, pgAdmin restarts (everyone is signed out of it) and loads the entry for each address, which can add several minutes to its start. So after giving someone the admin role, or changing an admin's e-mail address, run ./deploy-infra.sh: until then they can sign in to pgAdmin but see no server. An account without an e-mail address, or with one on a reserved test domain such as .invalid, .test or .example (pgAdmin refuses those), gets no entry; the deploy names it in a warning. If Keycloak cannot be read, the deploy keeps the list pgAdmin already has, so nobody loses their entry, and says so; only when pgAdmin has no list yet does it go on with the platform administrator's address alone. Since 1 November 2026 pgAdmin runs version 9.19.0, pinned in k8s/pgadmin/pgadmin.yaml; on an install from before that date the first ./deploy-infra.sh after it restarts pgAdmin once.
  • Node-RED after ./deploy-infra.sh: when Node-RED's settings changed, the deploy restarts it. Its flows are kept on a volume and survive. The first deploy onto that volume (and a fresh install) starts with no flows; the deploy then says "Node-RED runs no flows" and telemetry is not stored until you run ./apps/node-red/deploy.sh. Do that first deploy while no recipe run is active.

The scheduler runs recipe code straight from the repository (schd/), and the onboarding wizard runs edge/ from it. Do not pull or edit the repository while a recipe run is active: check first that pgrep -af '[r]un_recipe.py' prints nothing.

When your login session is not yet in the microk8s group (for example right after the first install added you to it), the scripts run their microk8s calls through sudo and say why they ask for your password. Log out and back in to stop that.

IAM 4.1: deploying the policy versions schema

Slice 4.1 adds the database side of policy versions (migration 008c), a function the access component installs (iam._changed_since) and every Phase 4 entry of the access policy. Nothing calls them yet. After pulling it, run three deploys in this order, back to back, best through ./guide.sh → Policy → Deploy IAM 4.1, which holds the deploy lock for all three:

  1. ./apps/access/deploy.sh. It must come first: the database deploy refuses without iam._changed_since ("run ./apps/access/deploy.sh first (IAM 4.1)"), and the health check's iamgrants is red from the pull until this step.
  2. ./apps/database/deploy.sh: migration 008c, the compiled functions and the grants.
  3. ./deploy-infra.sh, at once: the new route table, which the User Manager reads within a minute, and the NetworkPolicies.

From the end of step 2 until the User Manager has read the route table of step 3 (about one to two minutes), the User Manager answers 503 to a grant, an activation, or a role change into qa, qp, admin or auditor: the database and the route table disagree about what such a change widens (the new policy_page row). Do not grant or change roles until all three deploys have finished. A refused decision changed nothing and can simply be repeated. Every other decision and every other service is unaffected.

Between steps 2 and 3 the health check's iamgrants and tests/iam-schema.sh are red; after step 3 and one minute, everything is green. To take slice 4.1 back out, while no policy version exists: run apps/database/medcea_db_migrations_down_008c.sql as postgres in one transaction (the command is in its header), then revert the code, then ./apps/database/deploy.sh and ./deploy-infra.sh; the 503 window opens in reverse. The down file refuses once a version exists.

IAM 4.2a: version 1 and the skeleton gate

Slice 4.2a turns the access policy into numbered versions on this host. The database records version 1 (the factory policy of apps/iam/policy/defaults.yaml, applied at once), every deploy then compiles from the applied version (the host file state/policy-applied.yaml, see Policy source), and every deploy runs the skeleton gate. Nobody's access changes: version 1 compiles to exactly what defaults.yaml compiled to before. After pulling it, check that git status -- apps/iam shows nothing (the bootstrap refuses uncommitted changes there), then run three deploys in this order, back to back, best through ./guide.sh → Policy → Deploy IAM 4.2a, which asks your username (--by) and holds the deploy lock for all three:

  1. ./apps/database/deploy.sh: the 4.2a database functions (the policy's system actors, the event rules, the filter of the policy history) and the grants. It must come first: the other two record events through these functions.
  2. ./deploy-infra.sh, at once: the route table's digest moves once (it no longer counts the name of the policy file), and the accounts the install seeded are recorded in the policy history (account_seeded, see the policy history).
  3. MEDCEA_POLICY_BOOTSTRAP_BY=<your username> ./apps/iam/deploy.sh: no policy version exists yet, so it runs ./apps/iam/apply.sh --bootstrap --by <you> (without the variable, the platform administrator ADMIN_USER is named). It checks every compile is unchanged, writes version 1 in one database transaction, writes state/policy-applied.yaml, checks the grants, records bootstrap_applied, and restarts iam-api as every iam deploy does.

There is no 503 window: no grant or role changes. Between steps 1 and 2 the health check's iamenforce and tests/iam-schema.sh are red for a few minutes, because the database and the route table disagree about the digest; after step 2 they are green. Step 3 prints [iam] Policy versions..., then the bootstrap's lines (state empty, the tree's commit, the account you named, "version 1 compiles to exactly what defaults.yaml compiles to (N targets): nothing is re-rendered", [policy] state/policy-applied.yaml: version 1 (bootstrap) written, the grants check, the report) and ends "version 1 (bootstrap) applied: nothing was re-rendered". The health check then has two more checks, 54 in all (55 since the Platform manual check of 3 November 2026): Access policy version (OK: "version 1 (bootstrap), qa confirms it in the Policy UI once it exists (IAM 4.3)") and Policy artefacts (OK; the Policy UI's host is counted as not deployed). Then run tests/iam-schema.sh and ./apps/iam/apply.sh --status.

If the bootstrap waits (Keycloak not reachable, or a --by that is not an enabled account with a person_id), the iam deploy warns and finishes, and Access policy version FAILs "no applied version: a widening git change is not gated until version 1 exists" until ./apps/iam/deploy.sh runs again. On a fresh install the same happens inside ./install.sh: the iam component bootstraps version 1 in ./deploy-apps.sh --all.

Taking slice 4.2a back out. Version 1 and its events stay in the database, frozen; the down file of 008c refuses while they exist, by design. Under the deploy lock, while the 4.2a code is still there, move the host's policy files aside: bash -c 'source infra/lib-lock.sh && medcea_lock && source infra/lib-policy-source.sh && policy_state_retire rollback' (it moves state/policy-applied.yaml, state/seed-facts.jsonl, state/break-glass/ and the mark state/.policy-versioned into state/retired-<time>-rollback/; the 4.1 code refuses a host file). Then revert the slice in git, run ./apps/database/deploy.sh (the 4.1 functions come back) and ./deploy-infra.sh (the route table's digest moves back once). Applying 4.2a again later finds version 1 and does not bootstrap again.

The applied access policy

From slice 4.2a the access policy in force is the database's applied version, not the file in git. apps/iam/policy/defaults.yaml stays the factory file: version 1 was made from it, and it still holds the platform's skeleton (roles, resource groups and their routes, hosts, the network rules, the invariants: everything but the matrix of who holds which level, and the waivers). The matrix and the waivers come from the applied version.

IAM 4.3: the Policy UI

Slice 4.3 (November 2026) adds the Access Policy at https://policy.<domain>: iam-api serves it on a second port (8083, Service iam-admin) behind its own sign-in proxy (iam-oauth2-proxy, Keycloak client policy). Nobody's access changes and the policy is not changed. Run three deploys in this order, best through ./guide.sh → Policy → Deploy IAM 4.3 (the Policy UI), which holds the deploy lock for all three:

  1. ./deploy-infra.sh: the Keycloak client policy (with groups, person_id and the medcea-api audience), policy.<domain> in the certificate and the name service, the portal's Access Policy card, and POLICY_DOMAIN, POLICY_CLIENT_SECRET and POLICY_COOKIE_SECRET in config.env.
  2. ./apps/access/deploy.sh: the User Manager's Access Policy link, and Sign out on the new address.
  3. ./apps/iam/deploy.sh: ConfigMap iam-compiler (the policy compiler and the factory file, about 360 KiB, applied server-side), iam-api with its second port, then the sign-in proxy. Like the User Manager's, the proxy admits admin only until every iam-api pod answers on port 8083 that it enforces, then admin, auditor, qa and qp; without that answer the deploy stops with "the Policy UI's gate stays admin-only".

Afterwards the health check has one warning on purpose: Access policy version "version 1 (bootstrap) is not confirmed by qa yet (the Policy UI)", until a qa person confirms it (exercise E16). From then on the check also fails when the Policy UI shows another applied version than the database, and warns when it ships another skeleton than the accepted one (run ./apps/iam/deploy.sh). ./apps/iam/deploy.sh --gate-only re-renders the Policy UI's proxy when it drifted. To remove it: ./apps/iam/uninstall.sh (the proxy, the second port, the compiler ConfigMap and the card); the Keycloak client stays, and a confirmation already recorded stays in the history.

IAM 4.4: drafting and approval

Slice 4.4 (November 2026) lets an admin draft a change to the access policy in the Access Policy and a qa person approve it there, and lets the host operator propose a git skeleton change for the same approval (apply.sh --propose-skeleton, below). Nothing approved is applied in 4.4: applying a version arrives with slice 4.5. Nobody's access changes, no grant changes and there is no 503 window. Run two deploys in this order, best through ./guide.sh → Policy → Deploy IAM 4.4 (drafting and approval), which holds the deploy lock for both:

  1. ./apps/database/deploy.sh: the new bodies of the policy's approve and why-not functions (break-glass blocks approvals; a skeleton version needs the attestation, a fresh proposal and an approver whose role it does not widen). No new table, column, function or grant.
  2. ./apps/iam/deploy.sh: iam-api with the write side of the Policy UI, the new page, and ConfigMap iam-compiler with the new diff code (its checksum restarts iam-api).

In the minute between the two the old page still serves and cannot write: harmless. ./deploy-infra.sh and the access deploy are not needed (the route table, the proxies and the clients do not change). Approving needs IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES to be true, as confirming does. The health check stays green after the deploy. It WARNs "version N (<kind>) is approved; applying it arrives with IAM 4.5 (./apps/iam/apply.sh N)" for as long as an approved version waits, "skeleton version N was proposed against skeleton …, the accepted one is now …: return it and propose again" when a deploy accepted a narrowing under a proposal, and, as before, an open version older than 7 days.

Taking slice 4.4 back out.

  1. ./apps/iam/apply.sh --status must show no open change or skeleton version. If one is open, withdraw it: in the Policy UI (an admin, while the 4.4 page still serves), or from the host with ./apps/iam/apply.sh --withdraw-open --by <you> --reason "<why>" (when the page is the reason for the rollback, or after the revert: the 4.3 page has no Withdraw). Last resort, with the 4.4 apply.sh already reverted: SELECT iam_policy._withdraw_open('<reason>') as postgres in the PostgreSQL pod (actor system:apply). An approved 4.4 version must not survive a revert of 4.4: it was approved under 4.4's checks, which the reverted code no longer makes, and slice 4.5 could otherwise apply it.
  2. Revert the slice in git.
  3. The same two deploys.

What stays, by design: the withdrawn and approved versions, their events and their evidence (permanent); a proposed skeleton that was never accepted (harmless). The reverted approve function no longer refuses under break-glass and no longer checks skeleton versions.

Policy source

Every compile of the policy (every deploy, the health check, the onboarding wizard, flash-device.sh) reads the host file state/policy-applied.yaml when it exists. It is the applied version composed with the accepted skeleton, written by the deploy scripts under the deploy lock: by ./apps/iam/apply.sh after it applies a version, and by every deploy that finds it missing or different from the database. Nobody edits it by hand. state/ is readable by the deploying account only (mode 700, the file 600) and is not in git. Its first two lines say what it is:

# medcea-applied: version=1 kind=bootstrap entries_digest=… skeleton_digest=… git_commit=… written_at=…
# medcea-applied-body: body_digest=… groups=… roles=… dropped=0 keys=…

Every compile checks those lines against the body: a file edited by hand or cut short stops every deploy and the onboarding wizard with "its header does not match its body (edited by hand, or truncated)". Restore it with ./apps/iam/apply.sh --resume --by <you> if an apply was interrupted, or with any deploy, which rewrites it from the database. Health compares the whole body with the database's rows as well (Access policy version).

Before compiling, each deploy reads the applied version from PostgreSQL:

  • No version yet (before the bootstrap, or before migration 008c): the deploy compiles defaults.yaml. A host file at that point is refused: "state/policy-applied.yaml names a version but the database has no policy versions".
  • PostgreSQL not Ready: the deploy refuses and compiles nothing, with or without a host file. ./deploy-infra.sh is the one that repairs PostgreSQL: right after the Kubernetes API answers it only checks the skeleton gate (when the database can be asked; a widening git change stops it there, before anything changes), and it reads the applied policy only after its PostgreSQL step, so run it first. ./deploy-apps.sh asks PostgreSQL once before its first component that compiles the policy and stops the whole run when it is not Ready.
  • A host file without its database (after a restore of an older dump, or a database dropped by hand): restore the database the file belongs to, or move the file aside with policy_state_retire as in Restoring step 2a, only after reading this section. The next deploy then writes it again from the database. apps/database/uninstall.sh --purge-data (after its DROP), ./deploy-infra.sh --reset (after the namespace is gone) and --uninstall (after MicroK8s is removed) do this themselves, under the deploy lock, into state/retired-<time>-<why>/; nothing is deleted.
  • A version was applied here, but the host file is missing: the first host file also writes the mark state/.policy-versioned. While it exists, a compile outside a deploy (the onboarding wizard at its start, flash-device.sh) refuses "state/policy-applied.yaml is absent, but a policy version was applied on this host" instead of falling back to defaults.yaml, which may hold an unreviewed change. The next deploy writes the file again. A restore's step 2a keeps the mark; a deploy that then finds a database with no applied version (a dump older than the bootstrap) moves the mark into state/retired-<time>-no-version/ itself. A purge, a reset and an uninstall move it with the rest.
  • Versions exist but none is applied: refused; see ./apps/iam/apply.sh --status.

What these checks defend against, and what they do not. They stop a widening of access from reaching a deploy through the platform's own tools: a change in git, a variable left exported in a shell, a file edited during a deploy, a stale or hand-edited state/ file, a cold or crashed PostgreSQL, a restore or a purge. They do not stop the account that deploys the platform from going round them on purpose (it can run psql as postgres, edit the scripts, or rewrite the host file with recomputed digests): that is the host operator's break-glass, found afterwards through the policy history, the version audit and the health check, not prevented. A host file forged that way passes the compile's own checks, is found by the health check, and is overwritten by the next deploy; the onboarding wizard can compile it in between (see known gaps).

Two edge cases: flash-device.sh run from another clone of the repository, which has no state/, compiles defaults.yaml (harmless while version 1 equals it; it matters once a later version differs, IAM 4.5). The onboarding wizard compiles once when it starts, so a new applied version reaches it only at its next restart (./apps/edge-wizard/deploy.sh --gate-only restarts it only when its roles changed).

The skeleton gate

Every deploy compares the skeleton in git with the skeleton the platform accepted last. A change that gives access is refused before anything is compiled or changed. It counts as giving access when it adds, for any role: a route, a host, a database privilege or row filter, a tool setting, an MQTT topic or a level; and also, for nobody in particular: a route anyone may call without signing in, an upstream every signed-in host reaches, a host's service or client, a network source allowed into a pod, a host outside the sign-in proxies, a hop or a token client of a calling pod, any privilege or membership of a service identity, any database row filter, and any weakening of the checker's own rules (the invariants, the waivable list, break-glass, the database roles, the levels, the resource types, the classes, the constraints, the schema version). Help texts and descriptions do not count. The deploy stops with:

ERROR: this git change gives access that has not been approved (skeleton <git> over the accepted <accepted>):
  <role>: <n> new: route iam GET /api/… ; …
  anonymous and services: public monitor GET /api/… ; …
This change needs a qa-approved skeleton version: put HEAD back at the accepted skeleton, keep the change as a commit on a branch, and run ./apps/iam/apply.sh --propose-skeleton --by <you> --reason "<why>" --from-git <commit>; qa approves it in the Policy UI. Applying an approved skeleton version arrives with IAM 4.5.
Break-glass (MEDCEA_POLICY_FILE) does not bypass this check: it changes the matrix, never the skeleton.
Nothing was changed.

The network rules count in full: besides each pod's allowed sources, any change to how a source is resolved (the ingress namespace, a proxy's release name, a pod's or a calling pod's selector, the ports a pod carries, a calling pod's only_rpc list, the hosts that skip the shared upstreams) is a change that gives access, and so is any change to a database role name, a service identity's MQTT login or database password variable, or the MQTT Explorer's credential settings. Removing an allowed source, a rule, a hop, a resource group or a role only narrows. A key the gate does not know yet counts as giving access too (developers classify it in apps/iam/compiler/explain.py; tests/test_policy_source.py fails until they do). The refusal names such a change "section <key> changed".

The way forward for such a change: put HEAD back at the accepted skeleton, keep the change as a commit on a branch, and propose that commit as a skeleton version (./apps/iam/apply.sh --propose-skeleton --by <you> --reason "<why>" --from-git <commit>, since slice 4.4); a qa person approves it in the Access Policy. Applying it is slice 4.5: until then it still cannot deploy, and the health check FAILs "the git skeleton widens: a deploy refuses it (proposed as version N, <status>: put HEAD back at the accepted skeleton; the proposal waits on its branch)" while the deploying tree carries it. A change that only narrows access, or changes nothing a person can reach, is checked first: the git skeleton with the applied matrix must pass the policy checker, otherwise the deploy refuses "the git skeleton … with the applied matrix fails the checker; it is not accepted and nothing was changed" (fix the change in git). When it passes, it is accepted by the next deploy and recorded (skeleton_accepted, with the skeleton it replaced, and skeleton_synced); the health check WARNs "the git skeleton narrows: accepted at the next deploy" until then. A change to the matrix: in git is not refused but has no effect: the matrix comes from the applied version (the health check WARNs "git may lag"). ./apps/iam/apply.sh --status shows the accepted and the git skeleton; developers check a change offline with python3 apps/iam/compiler/cli.py skeleton-gate --against-git HEAD --check.

Policy break-glass

When a deploy must compile another matrix than the applied version's (an emergency, before approvals exist), break-glass names a policy file of your own for one run: MEDCEA_POLICY_FILE=<file> MEDCEA_POLICY_BREAK_GLASS="<reason>" ./apps/<component>/deploy.sh (or ./deploy-infra.sh). Without the reason the deploy refuses ("set MEDCEA_POLICY_BREAK_GLASS=<reason>"). For several components at once, export MEDCEA_POLICY_FILE and run ./deploy-apps.sh [--all] --break-glass "<reason>" (asked once, one event naming every selected component that compiles the policy) or ./install.sh --break-glass "<reason>" (asked once, two events: the infrastructure and the applications). Without --break-glass, deploy-apps.sh and install.sh ignore an exported MEDCEA_POLICY_FILE and say so. ./guide.sh → Policy → How to use policy break-glass shows the same and runs nothing.

  • Break-glass does not bypass the skeleton gate. A git change that gives access is refused under break-glass too, before anything is recorded.
  • It changes only the matrix and the waivers. The file's skeleton must equal the accepted one, and its rows for iam_policy and iam_users must equal the applied version's: break-glass can never change who approves policy or account changes.
  • The deploy prints the file and the reason and asks you to type the word break-glass at the terminal (deploy-apps.sh/install.sh --break-glass ask once for the whole run); without a terminal it refuses.
  • The file is copied to state/break-glass/<time>-<pid>.yaml; that copy is what is compiled, so editing the original afterwards changes nothing. The copy stays as evidence.
  • Before any compile, the deploy records break_glass_seen in the policy history (the copy's SHA-256, the reason, your OS account, the component) and prints in red "BREAK-GLASS: compiling … recorded as event N".
  • The health check's Access policy version FAILs while it is open. It ends per component: that component's next successful deploy without MEDCEA_POLICY_FILE records break_glass_ended as its last step (a deploy that fails leaves it open). ./deploy-infra.sh (infrastructure and, when enabled, the AI workbench) and ./deploy-apps.sh --all end it everywhere. The onboarding wizard's component ends only on a machine with a user session (its deploy skips otherwise).
  • Write the reason and the decision in your operations log.

Re-rendering only what drifted

Since slice 4.2b (November 2026) each script that deploys something compiled from the access policy has a scoped mode. It compiles its targets, compares each with what the cluster runs, prints one line per target (unchanged, re-rendered, not deployed or deferred) and changes only what differs. A second run therefore prints unchanged everywhere and changes nothing. Use it when the health check's Policy artefacts names a stale target, or after the host file changed; ./apps/iam/apply.sh --resume runs all of them for you. ./guide.sh → Policy → Re-render the policy artefacts runs them in one lock.

CommandTargetsOn a drift
./deploy-infra.sh --policy-onlyThe route table and MQTT rows (ConfigMap medcea-iam-lib), the application pods' network policies, Node-RED's editor rights, Grafana's roles, pgAdmin's sign-in roles, the Node-RED and portal gatesRe-renders that target only. iam-api restarts only when the MQTT rows changed, and the MQTT Explorer credentials issued under the old rows end, as in a full deploy. Grafana and pgAdmin redeploy their whole section (they restart; pgAdmin's sessions end), and only for pgAdmin is Keycloak asked who gets the database entry. Logs to logs/deploy-infra-policy-<time>.log.
./infra/deploy-workbench.sh --policy-onlyThe AI workbench's gate (who may sign in, hub administrators, the refusal message) and the notebooks' network hopsThe same Helm upgrade as a full deploy; the hub restarts only because its values changed. The example notebooks are not touched.
./apps/<c>/deploy.sh --gate-only for frontend, scheduler, monitor, mqtt-explorer, accessThe sign-in gate of that application's host(s): the roles it admits and its 403 pageA Helm upgrade of that proxy only; it rolls by itself because its configuration changed. No other part of the application is touched and nothing is restarted. The User Manager's gate widens only after every access pod proves it enforces, as in a full deploy; without the proof it stays admin-only and the command fails.
./apps/iam/deploy.sh --gate-onlyThe Policy UI's gate (since slice 4.3)A Helm upgrade of iam-oauth2-proxy, proof-gated like the User Manager's.
./apps/edge-wizard/deploy.sh --gate-onlyThe roles the onboarding wizard admits (its --required-role)Restarts the wizard service, which compiles them when it starts. While an onboarding run is in progress it does not restart (that would kill the run): it prints deferred and exits 4. Run ./apps/iam/apply.sh --resume --by <you> after the run has finished.

None of these touches Keycloak's realm, the database (except ending outdated MQTT credentials, as above), the TLS certificates or DNS, and none restarts an application. The database grants have no scoped mode: ./apps/database/deploy.sh applies them.

The deploy scripts refuse an option they do not know (exit 2, nothing runs). Before 4.2b a mistyped option such as ./deploy-infra.sh --polcy-only ran a full deploy.

apply.sh

./apps/iam/apply.sh applies policy versions. As of slice 4.4 it has these modes; it logs to logs/apply-policy-<time>.log:

CommandWhat it doesLock
--bootstrap --by <username>Version 1 from the factory file, only while no version exists. The iam deploy runs it; by hand only when that waited. Asks you to type yes (a permanent record).yes
--bootstrap --check-only --by <username>Would version 1 bootstrap? Writes nothing.no
--resume --by <username>Finishes an apply that was committed but did not record its end, or one with a deferred target: rewrites the host file, runs every scoped mode under its lock (each re-renders only what drifted), checks the grants, records the end. A target that had to wait (the onboarding wizard during an onboarding run) is recorded as apply_deferred after the end; the health check's Access policy version WARNs until a later --resume re-renders it. The iam deploy runs --resume itself when it finds an unfinished apply. Asks [y/N].yes
--statusThe applied version (who it is claimed by, whether qa confirmed it), open versions, the last policy events, the accepted and git skeleton, the host file, the live digests, open break-glass, recorded and queued account facts.no
--propose-skeleton --by <username> --reason "<text>" --from-git <rev> [--check-only]Since slice 4.4. Proposes the skeleton of commit <rev> (--from-git is mandatory: the deploying tree stays at the accepted skeleton) as a submitted version of kind skeleton, for a qa person's approval in the Access Policy. Only a skeleton that widens access and passes the checker with the applied matrix is proposed ("nothing to approve" otherwise), never under break-glass, never while another change or skeleton version is open. --by is resolved in Keycloak and recorded as the proposer (a claim); the --by username and your OS user are appended to the reason. It prints what each role gains, the digests, and which roles a qa approver may not approve for; asks [y/N]; writes the version and its diff in one transaction (system:apply). Nothing is applied. --check-only writes nothing and takes no lock.yes
--withdraw-open --by <username> --reason "<text>" [--check-only]Since slice 4.4. Withdraws the one open change or skeleton version (draft, submitted or approved) from the host, when the Access Policy cannot (a rollback of 4.4, an aborted test). Shows the version and its authors and asks [y/N]; "nothing is open" exits 0. --by is not looked up in Keycloak (withdrawing grants nothing); it and your OS user are appended to the reason. The withdrawn event names system:apply.yes
<version>, --rollback-toRefused: "arrives with IAM 4.5".—

--by is your Keycloak username in the realm: it is recorded as the person who applied the version (a claim), and a qa person other than you confirms the version in the Policy UI once it exists (IAM 4.3). Exit codes: 0 done; 1 refused or failed, nothing changed; 2 a usage error or a precondition (the bootstrap waits: no migration 008c, Keycloak down, an unknown --by); 3 committed but not finished: run --resume (never for --propose-skeleton or --withdraw-open, after whose commit nothing follows). MEDCEA_CONFIRMED=1 skips its own question when the caller has asked already (the iam deploy and the guide do).

One deploy at a time

Every script that changes the cluster or config.env takes the deploy lock first: install.sh, deploy-infra.sh, infra/configure.sh, deploy-apps.sh, each apps/<component>/deploy.sh and uninstall.sh, infra/deploy-workbench.sh, apps/iam/units.sh, tests/iam-signin.sh and the other test scripts that create things, and guide.sh for the length of a switch recipe. health-check.sh, the read-only tests and every --help do not take it. Two deploys side by side would interleave Helm releases, Keycloak clients and rewrites of config.env.

The lock is held by the kernel on the file .medcea.lock in the repository root. It is released the moment the holding process ends, even after kill -9 or a closed terminal, so there is never a stale lock to delete. A script started by another script that holds the lock (for example install.sh running deploy-infra.sh) uses its parent's lock.

Reading a refusal

A refused script prints Another MedCEA deploy is already running: and exits with code 1. What follows tells you who holds it:

  • A running deploy: its PID, start time, terminal and command, then Wait for it to finish, or stop it with: kill -- -<pgid>. Use that group kill, not a plain kill <pid>: a helm --wait or rollout status the run is waiting on would survive and keep the lock.
  • Leftover processes: The run that started it has ended; these leftover processes still hold the lock, with each process and the kill command to stop them.
  • A run as another user, usually under sudo: the holder could not be inspected, and the message suggests sudo fuser -v on the lock file.

guide.sh reports a held lock without exiting: it says another deploy is running and returns you to the menu with nothing changed.

guide.sh, the guided front end

./guide.sh walks you through the platform's commands by question and answer. It is the recommended way to run anything you do not run every day, and the only supported way to change an IAM switch.

  1. Run ./guide.sh, or ./guide.sh --dry-run to see what it would do without running or changing anything. Every question and confirmation is still asked in a dry run.
  2. Pick an area: Install and uninstall; Infrastructure; Applications; Operators and units; IAM switches (enable or roll back one switch, or bring every switch still off to full enforcement); Health and tests; Edge devices; Policy (read-only views; the IAM 4.1 and 4.2a deploys; policy versions: status, would version 1 bootstrap, bootstrap by hand, finish an interrupted apply, print the applied policy; how to use policy break-glass, shown and never run); Backups.
  3. Pick an action and answer its questions. Answers are checked the way the target script checks them (unit codes, usernames, component names that exist on disk), and nothing you type may start with -, so an answer cannot turn into an option.
  4. Read the plan: the exact command, whether it is read-only, changes the platform or is destructive, what it changes, whether it takes the deploy lock, and whether it needs sudo.
  5. Confirm. A read-only command asks [Y/n]; a change asks [y/N]; a destructive one (an uninstall, --purge-data, --reset, --bootstrap, erasing a board) wants the word yes typed in full, and --purge-data also the project name.
  6. The command runs in your terminal, so its own prompts work, and the guide reports its exit code.

The guide never asks for a password or secret: the commands it runs ask for their own. Ctrl+D (end of input) leaves the guide. For units and policy commands it asks for --by, your own Keycloak username, and offers your last answer as the default for the rest of the session.

Switch recipes

For an IAM switch or WORKBENCH_ENABLED, the guide shows the switch's current value, then the recipe for enabling it or rolling it back:

  • Before you start: a checklist of preconditions, which you confirm once. The guide does not test the checklist items for you. Some preconditions it does check itself and refuses the recipe with the reason (for example, enabling IAM_DB_ROLES while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT is not true).
  • Steps, in order: each is either a change to one line of config.env or a command. The recipe takes the deploy lock once for all its steps and stops at the first step that fails, or at Ctrl+C.
  • Notes: what happens afterwards and how to check it.

The switch must be on exactly one active line in config.env; with none or several, the guide refuses and says which lines to fix. A switch that another recipe's order depends on (IAM_DB_ROLES, IAM_POSTGREST_JWT, IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES) must be written literally as "true" or "false".

Before the first change, the guide copies config.env to config.env.guide-<timestamp>.bak (mode 600, not in git). It shows only the changed line, never the lines around it, which may be passwords. If a step fails, it tells you whether config.env was already changed and prints the cp -p command that puts the backup back. Restoring the file does not undo the steps that already ran; each step is safe to repeat, so the usual fix is to correct the cause and run the recipe again. After a recipe completes, the guide offers to delete the backup, because it is a full copy with every password. ./install.sh --uninstall-all deletes any backups that are left.

IAM switches → Bring IAM to full enforcement runs the enable recipe of every IAM switch still false, in order, under one lock, and stops at the first problem; running it again resumes. See After a fresh install.

The IAM switches

The access controls (IAM) were built in phases. Each phase has a switch in config.env, turned on in order with a recipe in ./guide.sh (IAM switches). A fresh install has the first two on and the rest off (see After a fresh install). Each one can be rolled back the same way.

SwitchWhen trueValue on the install of 31 October 2026
IAM_GATESEach sign-in gate, Grafana's own role setting, the AI workbench's sign-in, the device onboarding wizard and pgAdmin admit only the roles the access policy compiles for their host. False puts every gate back on the list used before the access policy (operator, qa, auditor, admin), except Node-RED and the MQTT Explorer, which keep engineer and Node-RED's editor rights from the policy; the User Manager on admin only, Grafana on any enabled account (at least Viewer) and the AI workbench on any enabled account, with ADMIN_USER as hub administrator; device onboarding goes back to EDGE_WIZARD_ROLE (empty: any signed-in account), and pgAdmin stays admin only.true
IAM_NETPOLOne network policy per application pod: only its listed callers can connect; anything else times out.true
IAM_POSTGREST_JWTconfig-api (PostgREST, behind the System Configuration UI and the onboarding wizard) verifies the person's sign-in token.true
IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLESEach database client (scheduler, monitor, Database Management, Node-RED, Grafana, pgAdmin) logs in as its own database role instead of the superuser.true
IAM_DB_ROLESThe database enforces per-role grants, row-level security and guard triggers, including the operator's unit and assigned-run checks.true
IAM_ENFORCEThe five Python services (scheduler, monitor, Database Management, the Config UI's platform API, the User Manager) refuse what the route table does not allow. False: they only log medcea_iam: would refuse … and let the request through.true (since 31 October 2026)
IAM_MQTT_PERSONThe MQTT Explorer connects with a short-lived credential of each person's own, made from their role and accepted only on the broker's WebSocket listener; False: one sign-in check for both broker listeners (services and boards), and the Explorer has no credential: its page says it needs this switch. The shared mqtt-explorer account is gone in both cases (removed 1 November 2026; see MQTT Explorer).true (since 31 October 2026, IAM 3.5)
WORKBENCH_ENABLEDdeploy-infra.sh also deploys the AI workbench (JupyterHub, about 2 GB of images).true

The values above are those in config.env on 31 October 2026, on the install that ran before any reinstall. IAM 2.6 first ran in report-only mode; IAM_ENFORCE was turned on that day, followed by ./deploy-infra.sh and ./deploy-apps.sh --all, and IAM_MQTT_PERSON, the last switch, after the Phase 3 deploy. A fresh install does not start there: it has only the first two switches on until Bring IAM to full enforcement has run (see After a fresh install). Always read the current value in ./guide.sh, which shows it from config.env before offering a recipe.

Two parts enforce whatever IAM_ENFORCE says: the User Manager's admin port and Database Management. They have no off switch.

Order and prerequisites

  1. IAM_GATES and IAM_NETPOL stand on their own. For IAM_NETPOL, every caller of an application pod must be listed under network: in apps/iam/policy/defaults.yaml.
  2. IAM_POSTGREST_JWT needs AUTHENTICATOR_DB_PASS set and different from POSTGRES_PASS (configuration generates it).
  3. IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES, then check that the health check shows dbclients OK.
  4. IAM_DB_ROLES: the guide refuses it until IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES are both true, and the database deploy refuses it, changing nothing, until every database client has moved to its own role. Before it, every operator needs a unit in effect, confirmed by qa (see Operators and units); an operator without one sees no telemetry or runs and starts none.
  5. IAM_MQTT_PERSON needs ./apps/iam/deploy.sh to have run (it deploys iam-api, which issues the credentials and logs in to the database only as its own role, so IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES must be true), ./apps/access/deploy.sh since the IAM 3.5 pull (a fresh install's ./install.sh has run it), and IAM_DB_ROLES true (the broker reads the credentials through its compiled grants). Until it is true the MQTT Explorer has no credential (there is no shared account any more).
  6. IAM_ENFORCE needs IAM_DB_ROLES true: only the database checks that an operator's run belongs to their unit. The guide lists this in its checklist but does not refuse; the health check reports iamenforce FAIL while IAM_ENFORCE is true and IAM_DB_ROLES false. It should follow a report-only trial in which every "would refuse" line from a real person is intended policy, because each becomes a refusal.

Recipes

./guide.sh runs these steps for you. They are listed here so you know what each recipe does.

SwitchEnableRoll back
IAM_GATESset true; ./deploy-infra.sh; ./deploy-apps.sh --all. Check with tests/iam-hosts.sh and tests/iam-workbench.sh.IAM_GATES=false ./apps/access/deploy.sh (narrows the User Manager first); set false; ./deploy-infra.sh; ./deploy-apps.sh --all. Node-RED and the MQTT Explorer keep their gate (engineer). Accounts with production_manager, maintenance, qc, qp, engineer, bio_scientist or control_scientist lose the portal and every other gated host until it is true again. Grafana admits any enabled account again; its old grafana_* roles are re-created, so Editor and Admin go only to the old built-in accounts that still exist. The AI workbench admits any enabled account again; its hub administrator still cannot open others' workspaces, and removing a person from the hub still keeps their files.
