IAM_DB_ROLESset true; ./apps/database/deploy.sh (it restarts config-api itself); ./infra/deploy-workbench.sh, which opens the AI workbench notebooks' route to config-api and Keycloak now that config-api and the database have both switched.set false; ./infra/deploy-workbench.sh (closes the notebooks' route first); ./apps/database/deploy.sh, which also closes the route on a running workbench before the database changes, so no notebook reaches config-api while the older, wider grants are back. While IAM_ENFORCE is true, roll that back too unless the rollback is brief.
IAM_ENFORCEpython3 tests/iam-routes-diff.py --strict; set true; ./deploy-infra.sh.set false; ./deploy-infra.sh.
IAM_MQTT_PERSONWith no run active: set true; ./apps/iam/deploy.sh (it refuses if iam-api cannot run yet, before anything else changes); ./deploy-infra.sh (restarts the broker; the boards reconnect within seconds); ./apps/mqtt-explorer/deploy.sh (it waits until iam-api has read the switch, about a minute). Engineers then reload the Explorer. Check with ./health-check.sh (MQTT person credentials) and tests/iam-mqtt.sh.set false; ./deploy-infra.sh; ./apps/mqtt-explorer/deploy.sh. Every personal credential is ended (kept in the database, never reused), and the Explorer has no credential until the switch is true again: the shared account does not come back (removed 1 November 2026).
WORKBENCH_ENABLEDset true; ./deploy-infra.sh.set false; ./deploy-infra.sh. This does not remove a workbench already running; later deploys only skip it.

After switching IAM_ENFORCE

The services read the switch on each request from the shared access settings that ./deploy-infra.sh publishes (the medcea-iam-lib ConfigMap), so the change is live within about a minute with no restart. From then on a refused request is answered 401 (no token or a bad one), 403 (the route or role is not allowed) or 503 (the service cannot verify tokens). Each application pod also keeps a copy of the switch from its last deploy, used only if the route-table mount is missing; it stays at the old value until the app is deployed again, and the health check reports iamenforce as FAIL until then. Redeploy the apps with ./deploy-apps.sh --all when no recipe run is active (Bring IAM to full enforcement asks for this at its end). Then check with tests/iam-check.sh, ./health-check.sh and tests/iam-signin.sh.

A rollback of IAM_ENFORCE returns the services to logging "would refuse" within about a minute.

When IAM_MQTT_PERSON changes, ./deploy-infra.sh appends enforcement_enabled or enforcement_disabled with {switch: IAM_MQTT_PERSON} to the same history, and every credential the Explorer is given is an mqtt_credential_issued event naming the person, the role, the topics and the expiry (never the password). On every switch change, and whenever the MQTT part of the access policy has changed, ./deploy-infra.sh ends the credentials issued before it and records mqtt_credentials_retired with their number; open Explorer pages then ask for a new credential.

When IAM_ENFORCE or IAM_DB_ROLES changes, the deploy appends an enforcement_enabled or enforcement_disabled event to the access policy history, a hash chain in the database that the health check verifies. For IAM_ENFORCE the event carries {switch: IAM_ENFORCE}. Each ./deploy-infra.sh also writes the current values of IAM_GATES, IAM_NETPOL and IAM_POSTGREST_JWT into the route-table settings with the time, which the quarterly access review records.

A git revert of IAM code comes only after the switch is rolled back. A reverted deploy breaks a config-api that is still in token mode, and reverting the database phase also needs a manual database step, which the guide's IAM_DB_ROLES rollback notes name.

After a fresh install

Every new config.env is made from config.env.example, and neither ./install.sh nor the setup questions change the IAM switches. A fresh install therefore comes up with the sign-in gates and the network policies on, but without token checks at config-api, database roles, row-level security or service enforcement:

SwitchFresh installWhat it does when true
IAM_GATEStrueEach sign-in gate, Grafana, the AI workbench, the onboarding wizard and pgAdmin admit the roles the access policy compiles for them.
IAM_NETPOLtrueOnly each application pod's listed callers can connect to it.
IAM_POSTGREST_JWTfalseconfig-api verifies the person's sign-in token.
IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLESfalseEach database client logs in as its own role instead of the superuser.
IAM_DB_ROLESfalseThe database enforces per-role grants, row-level security and guard triggers, including an operator's units.
IAM_ENFORCEfalseThe Python services refuse what the route table does not allow, instead of logging "would refuse".
IAM_MQTT_PERSONfalseThe MQTT Explorer uses a personal, short-lived credential. While false, the Explorer has no credential at all (the shared account was removed on 1 November 2026).

The health check warns while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT, IAM_DB_ROLES, IAM_ENFORCE, IAM_MQTT_PERSON or IAM_GATES is off, so a fresh install never prints "All checks passed". It does not warn for the other two: with IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES off the dbclients check is OK as long as every client is on postgres, and no check reads IAM_NETPOL; read those two in config.env. That is deliberate: each switch has its own rollback, and an administrator turns them on in order. Some cannot be on at the very first install:

  • IAM_DB_ROLES needs the scheduler and Node-RED already deployed and logging in to the database as their own roles (IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES). On a fresh install the database is deployed before them, so its deploy would refuse.
  • Operators need units assigned and confirmed by a qa person before IAM_DB_ROLES, or they see no telemetry or runs and start none (see Operators and units).

The order is: IAM_POSTGREST_JWT; IAM_DB_SERVICE_ROLES; assign the operators' units with apps/iam/units.sh assign … --by <your username> and have a qa person confirm them in the User Manager; IAM_DB_ROLES; IAM_ENFORCE (after a report-only trial, see The IAM switches); IAM_MQTT_PERSON; then, with no recipe run active, ./deploy-apps.sh --all, so every app pod's copy of IAM_ENFORCE is true. Until that redeploy ./health-check.sh reports iamenforce FAIL ("pod IAM_ENFORCE copy still false"); after it the IAM checks are OK.

Bring IAM to full enforcement

./guide.sh → IAM switches → Bring IAM to full enforcement runs that order for you. It uses each switch's own enable recipe, the same one the switch's own entry runs, so nothing in it differs from turning the switches on one by one.

  1. It reads every IAM switch from config.env. Each must be on exactly one active line and written literally as "true" or "false"; otherwise it refuses and changes nothing. If every switch is already true, it says so and stops.
  2. It shows the plan: each switch still false, in the order above (IAM_GATES and IAM_NETPOL only if they are false), with its purpose, its checklist, its steps and its notes. You confirm the whole plan once ([y/N]).
  3. It takes the deploy lock once for the whole chain (refused while another deploy runs), then runs the switches in turn. For each, it asks that switch's checklist when its turn comes, because some items only become true after the switches before it.
  4. Before IAM_DB_ROLES it runs apps/iam/units.sh list under its lock. If an enabled operator has no unit in effect, or the list cannot be read, it says what that means and asks whether to go on; answering no stops the chain there.
  5. Each switch's steps run as in its own recipe: one line of config.env changed, with its own backup, and the deploys in order.
  6. When IAM_ENFORCE was one of the switches, it then asks "No run is active: redeploy the apps now?" ([y/N]) and, on yes, runs ./deploy-apps.sh --all under the same lock, so every app pod carries IAM_ENFORCE="true". On a fresh install no run is active: answer yes. If you answer no, or the redeploy fails, it says that every switch is true but the apps are still to be redeployed, and ./health-check.sh reports iamenforce FAIL until you run ./deploy-apps.sh --all.

On a fresh install, expect a run made soon after the install to stop at IAM_ENFORCE's checklist, whose first item is that a report-only trial is over. Answer no unless the services have already run long enough with "would refuse" lines that are all intended (the trial needs no other switch, so it can run while you work through the earlier ones). Then run the trial (see The IAM switches) and choose the action again: it resumes from IAM_ENFORCE and goes on to IAM_MQTT_PERSON. In the same way, if the operators' units are not yet assigned and confirmed by a qa person, answer no at the question before IAM_DB_ROLES, assign them, and choose the action again.

It stops at the first refused checklist, failed step or Ctrl+C (exit code 130). Ctrl+C while it waits at a question stops it at once, before any step of that switch; during a step it stops once that step's command has ended. It then says which switch stopped and why, which switches are already done, which were not started, and the backups it made. Fix the cause and choose Bring IAM to full enforcement again: switches already true are skipped, so it resumes from the one that stopped. If that switch's own line was already set to true before its steps failed, put its backup back first, using the cp -p command the guide prints (only that switch changes back), or finish it with its own entry in IAM switches. Do not put back an earlier backup: it would turn off switches whose deploys already ran. After a complete chain the guide offers to delete the backups.

./guide.sh --dry-run shows the plan and every command and config.env change, without running anything, changing anything or taking the lock.

The health check

./health-check.sh checks every service and IAM control listed in health-checks.conf and prints one line per check. It is read-only and does not take the deploy lock. Options: --verbose shows detail for passing checks too, --timeout SECS sets the per-check timeout (default 8), --config FILE reads another check list.

ResultMeaning
OKThe check passed.
WARNNot broken, but not in its final state: for example a switch still false, a pod's copy of a setting that is out of date, or a decision waiting a long time.
FAILSomething is wrong or disagrees with config.env. The message names the cause and usually the command that fixes it.
SKIPThe check does not apply yet (for example the component it reads is not installed).

The summary is All N checks passed., or a count of OK, warnings and failures. The script exits 1 if any check failed. Services that are still starting usually pass a minute later.

The checks iamdbroles, postgrest, iamenforce and iammqtt WARN on purpose while their switch is false, so a platform that is not fully enforcing never prints "All checks passed". grafanarole, iamworkbench, onboardroles and pgadminroles WARN while IAM_GATES is false; the proxies' gates and IAM_NETPOL have no health check; test them with tests/iam-hosts.sh and tests/iam-network.sh. On the install of 31 October 2026 every switch was true, IAM_MQTT_PERSON included, and the health check passed all 50 checks it had that evening (52 before IAM 4.2a, 54 after it, 55 with the Platform manual check of 3 November 2026; see the manual in the portal). A fresh install shows these WARNs until Bring IAM to full enforcement has run, and iamenforce FAIL after it until the apps are redeployed (the chain asks for that at its end).

The IAM checks

Check (type, and the name the output shows)OK whenWARN or FAIL
iamchain (Access policy history)The hash chain of the access policy history verifies.FAIL names the event where the chain is broken.
iamdbroles (Database roles mode)The database records IAM_DB_ROLES=true, as config.env says.WARN while false. FAIL when the database and config.env disagree (run ./apps/database/deploy.sh).
iamgrants (Database grants)The database's grants equal the compiled policy (checked in a read-only transaction).FAIL on any drift. Expected right after a pull until ./apps/database/deploy.sh has run.
iampolicy (Access policy version)A version of the access policy is applied, state/policy-applied.yaml is exactly that version (its header and its whole body against the database), the skeleton in git adds no access over the accepted one, no earlier accept added any, the last apply recorded its end, no policy break-glass is open, the version audit and the policy history's chain are clean, and no waiver's review date has passed. Before the Policy UI exists (IAM 4.3) it reads "version 1 (bootstrap), qa confirms it in the Policy UI once it exists".FAIL on any of those, naming the command: no version yet ("./apps/iam/deploy.sh bootstraps it"), the host file absent or different (the next deploy rewrites it), a host file without a version (see Policy source), a widening skeleton ("a deploy would refuse it"), an unfinished apply (./apps/iam/apply.sh --resume --by <you>), an open break-glass (the components still to deploy). iamartefacts (Policy artefacts)The route table and the MQTT rows in ConfigMap medcea-iam-lib (data and annotations) and the roles each sign-in proxy admits are what the applied version compiles to. iampending (Changes waiting for qa)No proposed configuration change (unit commissioning, a log setting, the review of a commissioning end) has waited for qa for over 7 days.WARN on an older one, with its number (Configuration UI, Approvals tab). SKIP before the database deploy that adds the approval functions.
dbclients (Database clients)Every deployed database client logs in as the role the switch says.WARN while a client disagrees; deploy it again (Grafana and pgAdmin with ./deploy-infra.sh).
postgrest (PostgREST tokens)config-api runs the token-verifying setup, its keys match what the realm publishes, and a forged token gets 401.WARN while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT is false. grafanarole (Grafana roles)Grafana runs the role setting compiled from the access policy, refuses a role without a setting, and cannot give Grafana Admin through the sign-in.WARN while IAM_GATES is false (any enabled account gets in). iamworkbench (AI Workbench gate)The AI workbench admits exactly the roles the access policy compiles, its hub administrators are the policy's and hold only the hub role workbench-admin with exactly the decided abilities (admin page, list and read users, start and stop workspaces, remove hub users), nobody can hold JupyterHub's built-in administrator role (which could create a token for anyone and open their workspace), removing a person from the hub keeps their files, no sign-in tokens are stored, and notebooks cannot reach the platform's private addresses except Keycloak and config-api, compiled from the access policy and only while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT and IAM_DB_ROLES are true.WARN while IAM_GATES is false. FAIL on any difference, or while the hub still runs older settings (run ./infra/deploy-workbench.sh). Also FAIL right after a switch recipe changed IAM_DB_ROLES or IAM_POSTGREST_JWT without its workbench step, or when the workbench was deployed before config-api and the database had both switched: the workbench deploy then opened no route, and running it again fixes it. SKIP while WORKBENCH_ENABLED is not true.
onboardroles (Onboarding roles)The running onboarding wizard admits exactly the roles the access policy compiles (engineer, maintenance).WARN while IAM_GATES is false. pgadminroles (pgAdmin roles)pgAdmin's sign-in admits exactly the roles the access policy compiles (admin), read from the Kubernetes Secret pgadmin-config without printing the client secret, and neither pgAdmin's deployment nor an older copy of it (a ReplicaSet) holds a secret as a plain value.WARN while IAM_GATES is false. FAIL on a difference; while the deployment or an older copy still carries its sign-in settings or default password as plain values (a pgAdmin deployed before 1 November 2026); or when the Secret pgadmin-config is missing: run ./deploy-infra.sh. It does not check that the running pgAdmin has restarted with the Secret's current values. It also FAILs when pgAdmin cannot read its password file /pgpassfile, without which the IoT Postgres entry fails with "no password supplied" (when the check cannot reach pgAdmin's container at all, for example while it starts, it says that instead), and when an address in pgAdmin's list (see the deploy scripts) has no pgAdmin account or no IoT Postgres entry, naming the address; it WARNs while the list is empty. It reads pgAdmin's own database read-only and prints no password.
backupage (Backups)The newest complete nightly backup in BACKUP_DIR is at most 26 hours old.WARN beyond 26 hours, FAIL beyond 72 or when the folder cannot be read (run the check as the account that deployed the backup); SKIP before ./apps/backup/deploy.sh and on its first day. iammqtt (MQTT person credentials)Under IAM_MQTT_PERSON=true: the broker, its credential sync, the MQTT Explorer page and iam-api all run personal credentials; the LAN port's file holds no personal credential and the WebSocket file nothing else. In either mode: the removed shared mqtt-explorer account is in no password file, and the Explorer page's mode follows the switch (off while false).WARN while false (the Explorer has no credential), naming iam-api if it is not deployed, and when the broker's credential sync has not reached the database for over 5 minutes. FAIL when any part disagrees with config.env (the message names the deploy to run), a name is on the wrong listener, or IAM_DB_ROLES is false. It reads usernames only, never a password hash.

IAM test scripts

These scripts check the running platform. Run them from the repository root, or from ./guide.sh under Health and tests. Each exits 1 on any failure and prints a PASS or FAIL line per item.

ScriptWhat it checksLockGreen looks like
tests/iam-check.shEach of the five Python service pods has the route table, settings and keys mounted, its settings say the same IAM_ENFORCE as config.env, and a forged request sent straight to the pod is caught.No (read-only)Every pod PASS. With IAM_ENFORCE=false the forged request leaves a "would refuse" line; with true, a "refused" line and a 401. The User Manager's admin port and Database Management always refuse.
tests/iam-schema.shThe route table in the ConfigMap and in every pod equals a fresh compile; the IAM schemas and their grants are in place; the hash chain verifies, and an edit to it is refused (in a transaction that is rolled back).No (read-only)Every item PASS. Red right after a pull until ./deploy-infra.sh and ./apps/database/deploy.sh have run.
tests/iam-hosts.shEach signed-in host's live gate admits exactly the roles the policy compiles (for Grafana, the AI workbench, the onboarding wizard and pgAdmin, their own role setting), its 403 page names them, its proxy pod is newer than its configuration, Keycloak accepts its sign-in scopes, and at least one enabled account can open it. For pgAdmin also that it can read its password file and that each listed address has its IoT Postgres entry (SKIP while the list is empty).No (read-only)Every compiled host PASS; hosts not compiled yet are SKIP with the phase that adds them.
tests/iam-signin.shCreates one throwaway account per role (test-<role>-<random>, e-mail @<project>.example.org, which pgAdmin accepts), signs each in to every gated host as a browser does (the onboarding wizard and pgAdmin included: 12 accounts on 12 hosts), and compares who gets in with the policy. Where an account gets in, it calls one read-only API per service and checks the services refused exactly the calls the route table refuses.YesNo mismatch. A refused call answers 401 or 403; an allowed call never answers 401 or 503. The accounts are deleted on exit, also on Ctrl+C. On the AI workbench no workspace is started; the throwaway administrator removes the other test accounts from the hub and stays listed there itself. On Node-RED each editor sign-in must be accepted with exactly the policy's rights and then revoked. When pgAdmin turns an account away for any reason other than its role, the test prints pgAdmin's own message.
tests/iam-workbench.shCreates a throwaway administrator and a throwaway bio_scientist. The scientist starts a workspace; the administrator must see the hub's admin page and hold the rights of the hub role workbench-admin (which it receives through its hub group, so the test reads the rights it actually holds) and no built-in administrator rights, must be refused (403) at the hub's authorize step for the scientist's workspace (an "Authorize" page there counts as access and fails), must be refused (403) when it tries to create a token for the scientist or change the scientist's hub account, must be refused when it tries to read the scientist's tokens or shares (403, or 404 "not found", which the hub answers when someone holds that right only over their own account), must see no stored sign-in token, and stops and removes the scientist from the hub. Then the scientist's role is removed, and the account disabled: each time a fresh sign-in must be refused.YesEvery step PASS, and the scientist's home folder survives its removal from the hub. The accounts and that home folder are deleted on exit.
tests/iam-mqtt.shNeeds IAM_MQTT_PERSON=true. Creates a throwaway engineer, operator and administrator. The operator gets no MQTT credential; the engineer gets one with the policy's topics, which reaches the broker's WebSocket file within 20 seconds, connects there and is refused on the LAN port; the shared mqtt-explorer account is refused on both. Write rows exist only for units flagged for commissioning without a running run. The administrator then deactivates the engineer in the User Manager: the credential leaves the broker within 20 seconds, and the engineer's old session gets no new one. With --commissioning UNIT (an existing unit with no run running or scheduled) it also creates a throwaway qa account and, through the Configuration UI as a browser, has the engineer flag UNIT for commissioning, checks the engineer cannot approve it, has qa approve it, sees the engineer's write rows for UNIT within 20 seconds, ends it and sees the rows go within 20 seconds; the unit is ended on exit whatever happens.YesEvery step PASS. The accounts are deleted on exit; the deactivation stays in the account decisions, and with --commissioning the proposals, approvals and the policy history's commissioning events stay, all append-only.

tests/iam-signin.sh leaves a Keycloak login event in each test account's name, which stays after the accounts are deleted.

Two offline checks need no cluster: python3 tests/iam-routes-diff.py compares the route table with the routes each service serves (the IAM_ENFORCE recipe runs it with --strict), python3 tests/test_policy.py runs the policy unit tests, and python3 tests/test_config_ui_approvals.py and tests/test_config_ui_commissioning.py check the Configuration UI's approval and commissioning screens (under node, when installed).

Operators and units

An operator works in the units they are assigned to. Once IAM_DB_ROLES is true (or IAM_ENFORCE), an operator sees the telemetry, runs and run logs of every zone of their units, including zones added later, and starts or stops runs only there. An operator with no unit in effect sees an empty Monitor and can start nothing. Assign units with apps/iam/units.sh, or through ./guide.sh under Operators and units.

CommandWhat it does
apps/iam/units.sh list [<username>]Columns USERNAME, ROLE, ENABLED, UNITS, WAITING_QA. UNITS are in effect (a * marks one in effect before qa checked it, its decision still waiting); WAITING_QA are not in effect yet. Warns about an enabled operator with no unit in effect.
apps/iam/units.sh assign <username> <UNIT>… --by <your username>Proposes the assignment. It waits for a qa person other than you to confirm it in the User Manager's Decisions tab, and is not in effect until then.
apps/iam/units.sh remove <username> <UNIT>… --by <your username>Removes an assigned unit at once, or withdraws an assignment still waiting for qa.
apps/iam/units.sh remove --sub <uuid> <UNIT>… --by <your username>The same for an account Keycloak no longer has; list prints the command.

--by is your own Keycloak username in the platform realm. It is recorded as the person who made the change, so you cannot confirm it yourself. --reason TEXT adds a reason. Unit codes are case-insensitive. The script takes the deploy lock and is not run by deploy-apps.sh. It warns, without refusing, about an account that is not an operator, is disabled or has no person id. Exit code 1 means an unknown user or unit, an SQL failure or a held lock; 2 means a usage error (a missing --by included), a database or access deploy that has not run yet, or Keycloak unreachable.

When the qa person confirms such a decision in the Decisions tab, they must also tick the statement "I did not run units.sh for this assignment.", because --by is a claim the script cannot prove: whoever runs it has the host.

--bootstrap

assign … --bootstrap puts the unit in effect at once, before any check, with its decision still waiting for qa. It is allowed only while Keycloak has no enabled qa account other than yours and the operator's, so nobody else could confirm. Otherwise it is refused with exit code 2, naming who could confirm. A qa person who later refuses that decision removes the unit. ./guide.sh asks for yes before running it.

Each assignment or removal writes one account decision per unit (maker shown as system:deploy (by <username>)). When qa confirms an assignment, a unit_assigned event is added to the access policy history with the qa person as actor; a removal adds unit_removed; a withdrawal adds account_decision_withdrawn.

A unit that is still assigned to someone cannot be deleted in the System Configuration UI. Remove the assignment with units.sh remove first.

Backup and restore

Since 1 November 2026 the component backup (apps/backup/, decision of that day) copies the platform's data every night at 02:30 UTC, 45 minutes before the scheduler's own nightly job. One Kubernetes CronJob, medcea-backup, writes one dated folder per run into BACKUP_DIR on the platform host (config.env; /var/backups/medcea by default):

In the folderWhat it is
db/medcea_db.dumpThe platform database (POSTGRES_DB): every run, recipe, measurement, device, approval, account decision and the access policy history. pg_dump custom format, read with pg_restore.
db/keycloak.dumpKeycloak's database: the realm, every account with its password hash, roles, clients and their secrets.
db/globals.sqlThe database roles and their memberships, without passwords (a reference: a new install's deploys create the roles with its own passwords).
volumes/claim-<name>.tar.gzEach person's AI workbench home, with their notebooks and their GitHub token if they saved one.
volumes/node-red.tar.gzNode-RED's /data: the flows as they run, flows_cred.json and its key, the palette modules.
volumes/grafana.tar.gzGrafana's database: Editors' own dashboards, folders, annotations, alert rules, the Grafana accounts and sessions.
MANIFEST, SHA256SUMSEvery file with its size and SHA-256, written at backup time; sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS checks them.
OKWritten last. A folder without it is a run that failed or is still running; nothing treats it as a backup.

Not copied: PostgreSQL's own volume (the dumps are its consistent copy), the broker's persistence (retained messages, which the boards publish again) and the hub's database (the AI workbench rebuilds it as people sign in). config.env, the platform CA .ca/ and edge/provisioned/ are files in the repository folder, not in the cluster: keep your own copy of them, which a full restore after a reinstall needs.

The backups hold secrets: Keycloak's password hashes and client secrets, pgAdmin's stored files inside the platform database, people's homes with their git tokens. ./apps/backup/deploy.sh creates BACKUP_DIR with mode 700 for the account that runs it (with sudo when the parent folder is not writable), and the job hands every folder to that account, mode 700. Copy them only to storage that is as well protected.

Settings and the job

  • BACKUP_DIR: an absolute path. On the same disk as the data (the deploy warns when it is), a disk failure takes both; point it at another disk, or copy the folders off the host. Changing it takes effect at the next ./apps/backup/deploy.sh; the old folder stays where it is.
  • BACKUP_KEEP_DAYS (14): after a good run, folders older than this are deleted, complete or not. The newest complete folder is never deleted, so a host that was off for weeks keeps its last good backup. Only folder names of the form 20261102-023000Z are ever deleted.
  • The job runs in a pod from the same image as PostgreSQL, so pg_dump has the server's version. The postgres password reaches it only from the Kubernetes Secret rdbms-postgresql, never on a command line or in its log. It reads the volumes' directory (/var/snap/microk8s/common/default-storage) read-only, as a whole rather than volume by volume, because a person's workbench home appears at their first start and the job must find it without a redeploy. It runs as root inside the pod to read every person's files, with no other privilege, no access to the Kubernetes API and a read-only image.
  • A host that was off at 02:30 runs the night's backup when it is back within 6 hours. A failed run is not retried; the health check shows it and the next night runs again.
  • Grafana's and Node-RED's files are copied while they run. A save during the copy (unlikely at 02:30) can leave that copy inconsistent; the next night's is not.

Using it

Command (also in ./guide.sh → Backups)What it does
./apps/backup/backups.sh nowRuns the job once now, waits for it (up to an hour; --timeout SECS) and prints its log, which names files and sizes only. Takes the deploy lock.
./apps/backup/backups.sh listThe folders, newest first: complete or incomplete, size, number of files, when it finished; and how old the newest complete one is.
./apps/backup/backups.sh verify [FOLDER]Checks every file of a complete folder (default the newest) against its SHA256SUMS, and that no unlisted file was added.
./apps/backup/backups.sh restore-help [FOLDER]Prints the restore steps below with that folder's paths and this install's names. It runs none of them.

The health check's Backups line is OK while the newest complete backup is at most 26 hours old, WARN beyond, FAIL beyond 72 hours, and SKIP before the component is deployed and on its first day. Run ./health-check.sh as the account that deployed the backup: it owns the folder.

./apps/backup/uninstall.sh (and every uninstall level of install.sh) removes the CronJob and leaves BACKUP_DIR with every backup in it. Delete it by hand when it is no longer wanted.

Restoring

The restore is shown, not run: it stops services and drops databases, and only you can decide what to put back. Restore only the parts you need. Check the folder first with ./apps/backup/backups.sh verify FOLDER. In the commands below, F is the folder (for example /var/backups/medcea/20261102-023000Z); restore-help prints them with the real path. psql and pg_restore run in the PostgreSQL pod and read the password there; the dump goes in on standard input.

After a reinstall, restore once ./install.sh has finished, with the old config.env if you kept it, and within BACKUP_KEEP_DAYS: the new install's first nightly run deletes the older folders (except the newest complete one). To be safe, copy the folder you will restore from out of BACKUP_DIR first.

The platform database.

  1. Stop the services that write to it: microk8s kubectl -n medcea scale deployment node-red scheduler monitor config-api dbmgmt access iam-api --replicas=0 (one that is not installed is reported "not found").
  2. Drop it and restore it: 
    microk8s kubectl -n medcea exec -i rdbms-postgresql-0 -- sh -c 'PGPASSWORD="$(cat "$POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE")" exec psql -X -U postgres -d postgres -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -c "DROP DATABASE IF EXISTS medcea_db WITH (FORCE)"'
microk8s kubectl -n medcea exec -i rdbms-postgresql-0 -- sh -c 'PGPASSWORD="$(cat "$POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE")" exec pg_restore -U postgres -d postgres --create --exit-on-error' < F/db/medcea_db.dump
  3. (2a, since IAM 4.2a) Move the host's policy file aside, so the next deploy writes it again from the restored database: bash -c 'source infra/lib-lock.sh && medcea_lock && source infra/lib-policy-source.sh && policy_state_retire restore'. It moves state/policy-applied.yaml, the queue of account facts and any break-glass copies into state/retired-<time>-restore/ and deletes nothing; ./apps/backup/backups.sh restore-help prints the same step. It keeps the mark state/.policy-versioned, so until the next deploy the onboarding wizard (at a restart) and flash-device.sh refuse to compile rather than fall back to defaults.yaml. Without it every deploy refuses while the file names a version the restored database does not have applied (see Policy source). A dump older than version 1 leaves no version: the next ./apps/iam/deploy.sh bootstraps it again.
  4. ./apps/database/deploy.sh: the migrations, grants and role passwords of this config.env.
  5. Start the services again (the same scale command with --replicas=1), then ./health-check.sh: Access policy history must be OK, which shows the restored hash chain is intact.

Keycloak. microk8s kubectl -n medcea scale deployment keycloak --replicas=0; the same two commands with keycloak and F/db/keycloak.dump; scale it back to 1; then ./deploy-infra.sh, which sets the client secrets, the break-glass account and the realm settings back to this config.env. Every account change made after the backup is lost: deactivations included, so check the User Manager's decisions afterwards.

One person's AI workbench home. The person's workspace is stopped (they sign out, or the AI Workbench admin page → Stop). After a reinstall they sign in once first, so their new home exists, and stop it again. Find the home's directory and put the copy back over it:

PV=$(microk8s kubectl -n medcea get pvc claim-<name> -o jsonpath='{.spec.volumeName}')
D=$(microk8s kubectl get pv "$PV" -o jsonpath='{.spec.hostPath.path}')
sudo tar -C "$D" -xzpf F/volumes/claim-<name>.tar.gz --numeric-owner

That overwrites files of the same name and leaves newer files in place. For one file only, list the archive with tar -tzf and add the file's name as listed (for example ./notebooks/a.ipynb) to the same tar -x command.

Node-RED. microk8s kubectl -n medcea scale deployment node-red --replicas=0; find D as above with the claim node-red; replace its content with test -n "$D" && sudo find "$D" -mindepth 1 -delete && sudo tar -C "$D" -xzpf F/volumes/node-red.tar.gz --numeric-owner; scale it back to 1; then ./deploy-infra.sh and ./apps/node-red/deploy.sh, which put back this install's settings, credentials and the repository's flows. The health check's Node-RED flows vs git then names any flow only the backup had.

Grafana. microk8s kubectl -n medcea scale deployment grafana --replicas=0; find D with the claim grafana; replace its content as for Node-RED from F/volumes/grafana.tar.gz, then close the directory again with sudo chmod 0700 "$D" (the archive brings back the volume's open mode; Grafana also closes it at each start); scale it back to 1; then ./deploy-infra.sh (Grafana's break-glass password) and ./apps/report/deploy.sh (the platform's dashboards).

A backup or a restore writes nothing into the platform's records. The job's log is in the Job's pod while Kubernetes keeps it (the last three of each outcome): microk8s kubectl -n medcea get jobs -l app=medcea-backup. ./apps/backup/backups.sh logs in logs/. A restore of the platform database brings back the records of its night; anything recorded after it is gone, and nothing records that it happened, so write the restore down in your own operations log.

The manual in the portal

The portal serves this manual at https://portal.<domain>/manual/, linked by the Platform manual card on its main page (see the platform manual in the portal). Everyone the portal admits may read it; there is no access policy change behind it, because the portal's gate covers every path of its address. The decision is in the decision record ("The manual in the platform", 3 November 2026).

What is served is always built from docs/manual/chapters/ at the moment of publishing, never the committed docs/manual/index.html, which may be older: docs/manual/build.py --out <temporary file> --standalone, so publishing never changes the working tree. The page is stored gzipped (about 160 KiB for 570 KiB) in the Kubernetes ConfigMap portal-manual, with the page's sha256 and the commit it came from as annotations, and the portal's nginx sends it compressed.

After a change to the manual (a chapter edited, then python3 docs/manual/build.py and a commit), publish it:

  1. ./guide.sh → Infrastructure → Publish the manual to the portal (it runs ./infra/publish-manual.sh, which takes the deploy lock and writes logs/publish-manual-<time>.log; it publishes the manual and nothing else).
  2. It prints the page's size, the size in the ConfigMap, the sha256 and the commit, restarts the portal (a few seconds) and ends "the portal serves it at /manual/". On an install whose portal is older than the manual (deployed before 3 November 2026), it only stores the manual and says the portal is not set up to serve it yet, without restarting it: run ./deploy-infra.sh once, which adds the /manual/ route, the mount and the card.
  3. ./health-check.sh: Platform manual is OK.

./deploy-infra.sh publishes the manual too, every run, before it updates the portal page. Both refuse, and the portal keeps the copy it has, when build.py refuses the chapters (a dead link, a duplicate id, a script; the message names the chapter) or when the compressed page would not fit in a ConfigMap (1 MiB at most; the limit used is 1,000,000 bytes). deploy-infra.sh stops there with an error, before the portal changes: fix the chapter and run it again.

The health check's Platform manual (type manual) rebuilds the page into a temporary file and compares it with what the ConfigMap holds. OK when they are the same; WARN when the portal serves an older build ("the portal serves the manual of <commit> …, the repository builds …", with the guide entry to run), when nothing is published yet, or when the portal does not mount the manual (run ./deploy-infra.sh); FAIL when build.py refuses the chapters, or when the ConfigMap's content is not the page its annotation names (someone changed it by hand: publish again); SKIP before the portal exists.

The ConfigMap is the portal's, like the portal's web server configuration (k8s/portal/nginx.conf, ConfigMap portal-nginx); ./apps/frontend/deploy.sh adds only the background image's folder to the portal. ./deploy-infra.sh --reset removes them with the namespace, and the next ./deploy-infra.sh publishes again.

Uninstalling

There are three levels, all through install.sh. Each lists what it removes and asks you to type a word to confirm, and refuses without a terminal.

CommandRemovesKeeps
./install.sh --uninstall-apps [component …] (type apps)All application components, or the ones named, each through its own apps/<component>/uninstall.sh.The infrastructure and the database contents.
./install.sh --uninstall-infra (type reset)Every platform service, every application and all data in the namespace: the database, Keycloak users, Grafana's volume (the dashboards Editors made in the browser), workbench home folders.MicroK8s (so a reinstall is fast), config.env, the platform CA, the backups in BACKUP_DIR.
./install.sh --uninstall-all (type uninstall)All of the above plus MicroK8s, the onboarding wizard service, edge/.venv, the MedCEA block in /etc/hosts, logs/ and the credentials files. Asks separately about config.env (with the guide's backups) and the platform CA .ca/; the default is to keep them.edge/provisioned/ (the board provisioning records), edge/firmware/ and the backups in BACKUP_DIR. Tailscale and installed packages are not touched.

The infrastructure levels map to ./deploy-infra.sh --reset and ./deploy-infra.sh --uninstall. ./deploy-infra.sh --reset --secrets also blanks the generated credentials in config.env.

AI workbench home folders

Each person's workbench files live in their own volume, claim-<username>. Stopping a workspace, removing a person from the hub, a role that loses the workbench, WORKBENCH_ENABLED=false and every workbench redeploy keep it. Only the nightly backup copies it (Backup and restore), and deleting the namespace or MicroK8s deletes it: --uninstall-infra, --uninstall-all, ./deploy-infra.sh --reset and --uninstall, and answering yes to "Fully wipe MicroK8s and reinstall?", which ./deploy-infra.sh asks when MicroK8s is installed but not fully up. Before they ask you to confirm, these list each home with the directory that holds it on the host, for example:

AI workbench homes that this deletes (newest nightly backup: 20261102-023000Z in /var/backups/medcea, which this keeps):
  - bio1: claim-bio1 (10Gi), on this host in /var/snap/microk8s/common/default-storage/medcea-claim-bio1-pvc-…

When no complete backup is found the line says "nothing backs them up" instead. To keep them, restore them from the nightly backup after the reinstall, or answer no, then either have each person commit and push their notebooks to the research repository (Saving your notebooks to git), or copy each listed directory off the volume with sudo cp -a <directory> ~/workbench-homes-backup/. After the reinstall, a person's files go back into their new home once their workspace has started once (upload them in JupyterLab, or kubectl cp). Run the uninstall again afterwards.

A home folder may hold telemetry a scientist read into a notebook. A copy you take off the volume is a copy of platform data: keep it on the host and delete it once it is restored.

Grafana's volume

Grafana keeps its database in the volume grafana (size GRAFANA_STORAGE in config.env, 2Gi by default; an existing volume keeps its size). It holds the dashboards Editors created or copied with "Save as" in the browser, their folders, annotations and alert rules, and the Grafana accounts and sessions of the people who signed in. The platform's own dashboards are not in it: they come from the repository with ./apps/report/deploy.sh. Only the nightly backup copies it, and the same commands as above delete it. Before they ask you to confirm, they list it after the workbench homes:

Grafana's volume, which this deletes (newest nightly backup: 20261102-023000Z in /var/backups/medcea, which this keeps):
  - grafana (2Gi), on this host in /var/snap/microk8s/common/default-storage/medcea-grafana-pvc-…

To keep Editors' dashboards, answer no and have each Editor export the ones they want (dashboard → Share → Export → Save to file), then import them after the reinstall (Dashboards → New → Import). You can also copy the listed directory with sudo cp -a <directory> ~/grafana-backup/: it holds grafana.db, Grafana's whole database, including the old accounts and sessions, so keep it to look things up in rather than to put back. The health check's Grafana volume line warns when the volume is missing or not bound.

The break-glass password for Grafana's local admin is set in that database: ./deploy-infra.sh sets it on every run, so after you change GRAFANA_BREAKGLASS_PASS run ./deploy-infra.sh. A changed ADMIN_USER does not rename Grafana's existing admin login.

--purge-data

./install.sh --uninstall-apps --purge-data (or ./deploy-apps.sh --uninstall --purge-data) also drops the platform database medcea_db with all its data and its database roles. It applies only when database is among the components removed, and asks for the project name to confirm.

--purge-data cannot be undone. It drops every run, recipe, measurement, device record and audit record in medcea_db, including the access policy history. If config-api still verifies tokens, it cannot connect after the purge until ./apps/database/deploy.sh and then ./apps/frontend/deploy.sh have run.

./apps/access/uninstall.sh --purge-data drops the User Manager's data: access requests, account decisions and their histories. Unit assignments in effect survive (the script warns while there are any), and the next ./apps/access/deploy.sh records each one again as a decision waiting for qa. Assignments that were still waiting for qa are lost and must be assigned again.

Keycloak has its own database, so purging medcea_db does not remove realms, users or clients. Those go only with --uninstall-infra or --uninstall-all.

DNS and Tailscale

With a name-based domain (DOMAIN="medcea", LOCAL_DNS="true", as on this install), the platform answers DNS for *.medcea itself, on its Tailscale address, port 53. The host resolves the names from a managed block in /etc/hosts. Other devices on your tailnet need one setting in the Tailscale admin console, which no command can make for you:

  1. Open https://login.tailscale.com/admin/dns.
  2. Nameservers, Add nameserver, Custom.
  3. Enter the server's Tailscale IP (PLATFORM_IP), turn on "Restrict to domain" (Split DNS), domain medcea, and save.
  4. Each device needs "Use Tailscale DNS settings" on (the default). On Linux: sudo tailscale set --accept-dns=true.
  5. Test from any device: nslookup portal.medcea must answer the server's IP. Then open https://portal.medcea, typing the https:// the first time, because browsers treat a one-word ending as a search until they have visited it.

Each ./deploy-infra.sh checks this setting at the end and reports when split DNS is active.

A rebuilt or replaced server

A new machine gets a new Tailscale IP, but the admin console keeps sending the domain to the old one. The server itself still resolves everything through /etc/hosts, so only other devices fail. ./deploy-infra.sh warns about this in its DNS step and, at the end, prints the old and new addresses and the edit to make: in the admin console, edit the nameserver restricted to the domain and change the old IP to the new one. If the old server is gone for good, remove it under Machines too.

With an IP-based domain (<ip>.nip.io) no DNS setup is needed. When you move from one domain to another, the old nip.io addresses keep working as permanent redirects (ALIAS_DOMAIN).

The credentials file

Each ./deploy-infra.sh writes the passwords a person needs to credentials-<timestamp>.txt in the repository root, readable only by you, and shows it in a box on the terminal. The box is written straight to the terminal, never to the deploy log. It lists:

  • the platform admin (portal, User Manager, AI workbench, pgAdmin), the first qa person and the first engineer (Node-RED, MQTT Explorer, device onboarding: the platform admin cannot open Node-RED), with the portal address;
  • the Keycloak admin console break-glass account (realm master), for recovery only; every use is recorded in Keycloak's admin events;
  • the Grafana address, and Grafana's local admin account (the only Grafana Admin, for recovery only, at /login?disableAutoLogin; its password GRAFANA_BREAKGLASS_PASS is not the platform admin's); the demo accounts developer and viewer only while they still exist from an older install;
  • the pgAdmin address (platform admin role only);
  • where the MQTT service account passwords are (MQTT_*_PASS in config.env), that each board has its own credential, and how the MQTT Explorer connects: a personal credential per sign-in while IAM_MQTT_PERSON is true, no credential while it is false (there is no shared Explorer account any more);
  • the PostgreSQL superuser.

Save these in a password manager, then delete the file. config.env keeps its own copy, which re-deploys need. A person may change their own password from the user menu; the box then shows "(changed since the account was created; config.env's copy no longer works)" instead of a value nobody uses any more. A first password that must be replaced at first sign-in is marked "temporary".

The platform admin (the username you gave at setup, in the realm named after the project, medcea on this install) and the break-glass console account kc-breakglass in realm master are different accounts. Use the break-glass account only when the platform's own tools cannot do the job.

Troubleshooting

A person gets a 403 page from the portal

Symptom: the person signs in and sees "No access yet", or another 403 page. Cause: "No access yet" means Keycloak signed them in but their account has no platform role, so the portal's gate refuses them. A 403 with a different message is a failed sign-in (a provider error or an expired sign-in form), not a missing role. A host other than the portal shows "No access to <service>" with the roles that may open it. Fix: for "No access yet", give the account a role in the User Manager (a grant waits for qa to confirm); a password reset does not help. The role takes effect within about four minutes, without a new sign-in. For a failed sign-in, have the person open the portal again.

Grafana says the login was denied

Symptom: after signing in, a person lands on Grafana's own sign-in page with a message that the login was denied. Cause: their role holds nothing on the Grafana dashboards row of the access policy (for example operator, admin or a scientist), or the account has no platform role. Fix: none, if the role is right; Grafana is not for that role. Otherwise change the role in the User Manager. If everyone is refused, run ./health-check.sh: a grafanarole FAIL or a Keycloak scope problem (tests/iam-hosts.sh, sign-in line for Grafana) is the cause.

A person is refused right after switching to another account

Symptom: someone signs out of one account and in with another (for example to test a role), and an app shows "No access", Grafana says the login was denied, or the app still acts with the first account's role. Cause: usually the previous single sign-on session is still active in that browser: Keycloak, or an app that keeps its own session (Grafana, pgAdmin, the AI Workbench, the Node-RED editor), signed them in again as the first account without asking. Fix: sign out from the user menu (the person's name at the top right, then Sign out; the Sign Out buttons on the portal page end only the portal's own sign-in, and the portal then signs the first account in again), close the app's tabs and sign in again, or use a private window for the second account. Check the role in the User Manager only if the refusal stays.

The AI Workbench says "No access to AI Workbench"

Symptom: after signing in, a person sees the workbench's error page with "No access to AI Workbench" and the roles that may open it. Cause: their role holds nothing on the AI workbench row of the access policy (for example operator or auditor), or the account has no platform role. Fix: none, if the role is right. Otherwise change the role in the User Manager; the workbench checks it at the next sign-in, at the latest within 5 minutes (an open notebook tab within about 10). If everyone is refused, run ./health-check.sh: an iamworkbench FAIL is the cause, and ./infra/deploy-workbench.sh the fix.

A notebook in the AI Workbench says "The document is taking some time to load"

Symptom: in a workspace, no notebook or file opens, not even those in medcea-examples; JupyterLab shows "The document is taking some time to load" and keeps waiting. Cause: before 1 November 2026 the workspace installed real-time collaboration at the same time as it started, and the first start of a new home folder began before the install had finished. The browser then asked for documents through collaboration, which the workspace did not serve yet. Now the install finishes before the workspace starts, so this should no longer happen once ./infra/deploy-workbench.sh (or ./deploy-infra.sh) has run and the workspace has been started since. Fix: the person restarts their workspace (File → Hub Control Panel, Stop My Server, then Start My Server), or the administrator stops it from the hub's admin page. If it happens on a workspace started after the deploy, the workspace's log shows why: kubectl -n medcea get pods -l component=singleuser-server names the workspace pods, then kubectl -n medcea logs <pod> | grep medcea-home-setup (a line with WARNING names the install that failed; a later start tries again).

pgAdmin's IoT Postgres server fails with "no password supplied", or shows no username

Symptom: an administrator signed in to pgAdmin opens IoT Postgres and gets "no password supplied", the server's properties show an empty Username, or there is no IoT Postgres server at all. Cause: before 1 November 2026 the entry belonged to pgAdmin's built-in user and was shared; pgAdmin does not share a server's username or password file, so everyone else saw it without them. The password file also belonged to the wrong user, so even the built-in user could not use it, and pgAdmin 9.19 reads a password file only from the signed-in person's own storage folder (an absolute path elsewhere, and PGPASSFILE, are ignored). Now each admin gets an entry of their own when pgAdmin starts, with a copy of the password file in their storage folder, but only for the addresses the last ./deploy-infra.sh listed. Fix: run ./deploy-infra.sh, then ./health-check.sh; pgadminroles names any address still without its entry. The account must be enabled, hold admin and have an e-mail address in Keycloak on an ordinary domain (not .invalid, .test, .example or .local). pgAdmin writes one line per address to its log (kubectl -n medcea logs deployment/pgadmin | grep oauth2-servers).

The System Configuration UI fails after a realm key rotation

Symptom: after a signing key was rotated or removed in Keycloak, the System Configuration UI's data calls are refused and the health check shows postgrest FAIL naming key ids the realm no longer publishes. Cause: config-api and the Python services verify tokens against the keys published at the last ./deploy-infra.sh; config-api reads them only when it starts. Fix: run ./deploy-infra.sh. It republishes the keys and restarts config-api when they changed.

The Config UI stops answering for a few seconds during a switch

Symptom: while IAM_POSTGREST_JWT is switched in either direction, the System Configuration UI's requests fail for up to about 15 seconds. Cause: by design. config-api's database password changes in the same step as its mode, and nothing serves until the new pod is ready. Fix: none needed; do the switch when nobody is editing configuration, and reload the page afterwards.

Node-RED stores no telemetry after a deploy

Symptom: the Monitor and Grafana show no new readings, and the health check shows noderedflows FAIL with "Node-RED runs no flows". Cause: Node-RED started on a new, empty volume (the first deploy that gave it one, or a fresh install). Fix: run ./apps/node-red/deploy.sh.

The Node-RED editor keeps reloading or refuses an engineer

Symptom: an engineer passes the platform sign-in, but the Node-RED editor reloads in a loop or shows its own sign-in form that accepts nothing. Cause: Node-RED's own sign-in cannot work, for example its sign-in module could not be installed (noderedauth FAIL) or Keycloak refuses its callback. Fix: run ./deploy-infra.sh. Break-glass, when the editor is needed at once: microk8s kubectl -n <namespace> set env deployment/node-red NR_ADMIN_AUTH=off turns the editor's own sign-in off (only the platform sign-in remains, which admits engineer only). It restarts Node-RED. While it is off, any program on the platform host or inside the cluster that can reach Node-RED can use its admin interface without signing in, so keep it brief. The next ./deploy-infra.sh turns it on again and records a break_glass_seen event in the access policy history with the time Node-RED started without its sign-in; noderedauth FAILs until then.

Ending every Node-RED editor session

Symptom: you need every engineer signed out of the Node-RED editor now, for example after a role removal or when an editor token may have been exposed. Cause: Node-RED keeps editor sessions on its volume (/data/.sessions.json), so a restart alone does not end them; each lasts up to one hour from when it was issued. Sessions issued before 1 November 2026 were set to last a week; at each start Node-RED now drops every stored session longer than the hour, so the restart of that day's ./deploy-infra.sh ended them (its log says how many). Fix: clear the session file and restart Node-RED. Flows and palette modules are not affected:

microk8s kubectl -n medcea exec deployment/node-red -c node-red -- sh -c ': > /data/.sessions.json'
microk8s kubectl -n medcea rollout restart deployment/node-red

Engineers sign in again on their next visit. Telemetry ingest pauses for the few seconds the restart takes.

A newly onboarded board's first MQTT connections are refused

Symptom: right after onboarding, a board restarts once or twice before it publishes. Cause: its credential is registered after it boots and reaches the broker at the next sync, up to about 15 seconds later. Fix: none; the retry succeeds. A board flashed before the broker required credentials has none, never connects and must be onboarded again.

A change to the sign-in page does not show

Symptom: the login page still looks the same after you changed the theme files. Cause: Keycloak caches themes and never re-reads them while running. Fix: run ./deploy-infra.sh; it restarts Keycloak when the theme changed.

A deploy is refused although nothing is running

Symptom: "Another MedCEA deploy is already running", but the deploy you started earlier has ended. Cause: a background process started by that run, or a helm --wait or rollout status left after a plain kill, still holds the lock. Fix: the refusal lists the leftover processes and the kill command for them. Next time stop a run with the group kill it prints (kill -- -<pgid>). See One deploy at a time.

MicroK8s steps fail on Ubuntu 25.10 or later

Symptom: a MicroK8s start or addon enable fails with snapctl … from outside of a snap, or enable rbac dies after "No callback tokens file." Cause: Ubuntu's default sudo is now sudo-rs, which ignores sudo -E, and MicroK8s needs it for every privileged step. bootstrap-infra.sh switches to classic sudo for its own duration and restores the setting afterwards, so deploys work; a MicroK8s command you run by hand does not get that. Fix: by hand, run sudo update-alternatives --set sudo /usr/bin/sudo.ws, then the microk8s command, then sudo update-alternatives --auto sudo.

Other devices cannot open *.medcea after a server rebuild

Symptom: the host opens every page, laptops and phones on the tailnet do not. Cause: Tailscale split DNS still points at the old server's IP. Fix: change it in the admin console as ./deploy-infra.sh prints (see DNS and Tailscale).

An operator sees an empty Monitor

Symptom: an operator signs in but sees no telemetry or runs and cannot start a run. Cause: no unit is in effect for them, either none assigned or an assignment still waiting for qa. Fix: apps/iam/units.sh list <username>; assign a unit with --by and have a qa person confirm it in the User Manager's Decisions tab.

A deploy fails with "metadata.annotations: Too long"

Symptom: an app deploy stops with The ConfigMap "…" is invalid: metadata.annotations: Too long: must have at most 262144 bytes. Cause: kubectl apply keeps a copy of the whole object in an annotation, and annotations hold at most 256 KiB, so a ConfigMap carrying a large page cannot be applied. The Configuration UI's page (apps/frontend/html/index.html, about 254 KB) reached that limit on 31 October 2026. Fix: the frontend and scheduler deploys replace or create their page ConfigMap instead of applying it, so pull and deploy again. A deploy script that still applies a large file needs the same change (kubectl replace when the ConfigMap exists, kubectl create otherwise).

A qa decision cannot be confirmed

Symptom: a decision about the only qa person's own account, or one they made, waits forever. Cause: the person who checks a decision is never its maker or its subject. Fix: another qa person confirms it. With no enabled qa person at all, set a new QA_USER in config.env and run ./deploy-infra.sh (break-glass, outside the decisions).

Training: hands-on exercises

An exercise is a task you carry out on the platform from start to end, with the accounts and screens a real person would use. It has two purposes. A new person learns a task by doing it once under guidance. After a change to the platform, running the exercise shows that the screens, the database rules and the records still work together. This chapter says how to run an exercise safely, where to read what it recorded, the fixed form every exercise follows, the exercises themselves, and how to add a new one.

Account names. The exercises name the test accounts of the install of 31 October 2026: eng is the engineer account and svieira the qa account made at setup, viewer a demo auditor account from older installs. On another install, for example after a reinstall, read eng as the engineer account you gave at setup (ENGINEER_USER), svieira as the qa account (QA_USER), and viewer as any auditor account (a fresh install makes none). The "Last done" entries, with their decision and approval numbers, record runs on that earlier install and its database; a new install starts with none, and each exercise's "Last done" is updated when it is run there.

Exercises at a glance

ExerciseRolesWhat it provesLast done
E1 Request access and grant a roleapplicant, admin, qaAn approved request gives no access until a second person confirms it.Not recorded here
E2 Assign a unit to an operatorhost operator, qa, operatorAn operator sees a unit only after qa confirms the assignment, with the attestation.Partly (confirmation only): 31 Oct 2026, UNIT01 (decision 14)
E3 Draft and approve a parameter setengineer, qaA draft is used only after another person approves it, and is frozen afterwards.31 Oct 2026, q_test (approval #6)
E4 Return and withdrawengineer, qaWork can be sent back with a reason or taken back by its proposer, and both are recorded.Not yet on production
E5 Test mode and the MQTT Explorerengineer, qaCommands reach a unit only while qa has approved test mode, and stop when it ends.31 Oct 2026, two cycles on UNIT01
E6 Release a completed runproduction_manager, operator, qpOnly a person who neither created, changed nor executed a run can release it.Not yet
E7 Deactivate and reactivate an accountadmin, qaTaking access away acts at once; giving it back waits for a second person.31 Oct 2026, deactivation only (decision 12)
